Management *plans and executes* within the authority so granted. The delivery leader manages; the sponsor and board govern, and when the two blur — a board that manages, a delivery leader who self-governs — the control system loses the separation on which it depends. Distinguish, too, the **governance of an individual project** from the **governance of project management** — the organisation's standing framework of methods, gate regimes and delegation schemes within which every project is governed.

### The framework and standards context

The candidate is expected to know the consensus landscape, not any single proprietary method. ISO 21505 addresses the governance of projects, programmes and portfolios, framing project governance as an extension of corporate governance: the governing body remains ultimately answerable for the projects it funds, and delegates — never abandons — that accountability. ISO 21502 situates governance within wider project management practice, and method families such as PRINCE2 contribute defined roles and responsibilities, management by exception, and stage-based authorisation. Professional bodies' governance principles converge on the same tenets: a single accountable sponsor, a clear link from project to strategy through the business case, decision-making at the lowest competent level, and independent assurance proportionate to risk. The assurance dimension draws additionally on the "three lines" model — delivery management (first line), oversight and standards functions (second line) and independent review (third line) — while public-sector gateway regimes model staged independent review at key investment decisions. The Institute's examination tests understanding of these frameworks' principles; it neither presupposes nor certifies competence in any vendor's product, and PCI claims no accreditation or endorsement by their owners.

### The core structures and how they relate

Four roles anchor every well-formed governance design. The **sponsor** is the single named individual accountable for the business case and the realisation of benefits; the sponsor owns the "why" and holds the delivery leader to account for the "how". The **project board** (or steering committee) is the collective directing body through which the sponsor exercises governance, bringing together the interests — typically user, supplier and business — that must consent to major decisions. The **project management leader** is accountable for delivering the agreed outputs within the limits the board delegates. The **assurance function** provides those who govern with confidence, independent of those who deliver, that the information they receive is accurate and the project is competently run; it reports to the governance tier, never to the delivery leader whose work it examines.

These pieces relate through a chain of delegation and a mirrored chain of escalation. Authority flows downward — corporate body to sponsor, sponsor and board to delivery leader, delivery leader to team — each step with explicit limits. **Escalation** is the return path: the obligation to refer a decision upward the moment a *forecast* breaches a delegated limit, so that no tier is surprised by an exception it was entitled to decide. **Stage gates** punctuate this chain in time: predefined points at which continued investment must be positively re-authorised on current evidence rather than allowed to continue by inertia.

Governance operates entirely on information, which is why the reporting regime is a governance artefact, not an administrative one. Its foundations are accuracy (a single, reconciled version of performance truth), timeliness (information arriving while decisions can still alter outcomes) and proportionate summarisation (each tier receiving the detail its decision rights require). AI-enabled reporting can strengthen all three — but no decision right is ever allocated to a system.

### Designing a governance structure: a step-by-step method

Governance is designed, not inherited. The method below scales from a small internal change to a major programme; what varies is the weight of each step, never its omission.

1. **Tier the project by scale and risk.** Classify the project (for example low / medium / high) using objective criteria: budget, duration, novelty, regulatory exposure, degree of AI-enabled or safety-critical content. The tier drives everything that follows — board seniority, gate frequency, assurance depth.
2. **Name the roles — one accountable individual per role.** A single sponsor; a board small enough to decide (typically three to six voting members); a delivery leader accountable within delegated limits; an assurance function reporting to the sponsor or board, never to the delivery leader.
3. **Allocate decision rights in a decision-rights matrix.** For each recurring decision class — baseline change, contract award, risk acceptance, gate passage, benefit revision, deployment of an AI-generated forecast into the performance record — record who decides, who is consulted and who is informed. Every AI-assisted decision must show a named human owner.
4. **Set delegated tolerances.** Express limits for cost, schedule, scope and risk as both a percentage of baseline and an absolute cap, at each level: delivery leader, sponsor, board. Track consumption cumulatively, not change by change.
5. **Define escalation routes and clock speeds.** Escalation is triggered by a *forecast* breach of tolerance, not an actual one; each route states who is notified, within how many working days, and with what minimum information.
6. **Fix the gate and assurance schedule.** Position stage gates at commitment points (funding release, contract award, go-live) and commission independent review ahead of the highest-risk gates.
7. **Specify the reporting regime.** One agreed data set, a fixed cycle, exception-based highlighting, and a stated owner for every figure — including any drafted or flagged by an AI tool, validated before it enters the record.
8. **Document, approve and rehearse.** Write the structure into the initiation documentation, have the board approve it, and walk one hypothetical escalation through it before delivery begins.

### Worked example: setting delegated limits and testing an escalation

**Setup.** A systems-replacement project has an approved budget at completion of $12,000,000 and a 20-month schedule. It is tiered medium-high risk. The board sets: delivery-leader cost tolerance ±2.5%, schedule tolerance ±4 weeks; sponsor delegated limit +7.5% cumulative cost and +10 weeks; anything beyond, or any scope or benefits change, is reserved to the board.

**Arithmetic — setting the limits.**

- Delivery-leader cost tolerance: 2.5% × $12,000,000 = **$300,000**
- Sponsor cumulative delegation: 7.5% × $12,000,000 = **$900,000**

**Arithmetic — testing an escalation.** In month 9 a supplier fails; the recovery estimate is +$340,000 and +6 weeks. Changes already approved to date total $180,000.

- Single-event test: $340,000 > $300,000 → the event exceeds the delivery leader's tolerance and **must be escalated to the sponsor**. The 6-week slip also exceeds the 4-week schedule tolerance, confirming escalation on both dimensions.
- Cumulative test at sponsor level: $180,000 + $340,000 = **$520,000**; $520,000 < $900,000 → the decision sits **within the sponsor's delegation**. The sponsor may approve without convening the board, notifying it by exception report at the next cycle.
- Remaining sponsor headroom after approval: $900,000 − $520,000 = **$380,000**.

**Interpretation.** The regime placed the decision at the lowest competent level: the delivery leader escalated because the forecast breached tolerance; the sponsor decided because cumulative consumption remained inside delegation; the board was informed, not convened. Had the cumulative figure reached $900,000 — or had the forecast trend made a breach probable — the decision would have risen to the board *before* the money was committed.

### Common pitfalls

- **Sponsor as super-project-manager.** A sponsor directing daily work collapses the owner–deliverer separation, leaving no one to hold delivery to account.
- **Boards built for representation, not decision.** Twelve observers and no quorum rules produce briefings, not decisions.
- **Escalating on actuals.** Waiting until tolerance is actually breached escalates history; the trigger must be the forecast.
- **Salami-slicing delegation.** Ten changes each within individual limits can exhaust cumulative delegation unnoticed — track headroom cumulatively.
- **Uniform tolerances regardless of risk.** A ±2.5% tolerance fit for a routine project is negligent for a novel, safety-critical or heavily AI-dependent one.
- **Assurance reporting to the delivery leader.** Independence is lost the moment the reviewed party commissions, filters or edits the review.
- **"Watermelon" reporting.** Green on the cover, red inside — the symptom of a regime that punishes escalation rather than rewarding early warning.
- **Unowned AI outputs.** Machine-generated narratives, forecasts or risk scores entering the record unvalidated and with no named owner.
- **Governance by document, not behaviour.** A decision-rights matrix bypassed by informal approvals is not governance; test the routes, and use them.

### How AI changes the governance competency

Predictive and generative tooling changes the tempo of governance more than its architecture. Where boards once governed on periodic, backward-looking reports, AI-enabled environments offer continuous signals: forecasts of cost and schedule outturn, anomaly detection, consistency checks between reporting narrative and underlying numbers, and portfolio-level pattern recognition that surfaces drifting projects before their own reports admit it. Well governed, this shifts oversight from retrospective inspection towards exception-based supervision; badly governed, it produces a torrent of unvalidated indicators, dashboards mistaken for assurance, and decisions quietly ceded to scores nobody present can explain. The principles of this chapter are unchanged: no model holds a decision right, sits on a board or exercises a tolerance. What changes is the information on which accountable people act — and the discipline required before machine-generated information may inform a governance decision.

The delivery leader may appropriately automate the mechanical layer of governance: assembling reporting packs from source systems; drafting status narratives, minutes and decision logs; reconciling RAG ratings against the thresholds that define them; checking gate documentation for completeness; and tracking actions, decisions and escalations against owners and due dates. These uses reduce latency and transcription error and free attention for judgement. Validation, by contrast, is not delegable. Before an AI output reaches a sponsor, board or gate review, a competent professional must have traced any forecast to its inputs and assumptions; confirmed that a generated summary is faithful to its sources, not merely fluent; tested exception flags before they trigger escalation; and verified that sensitive or personal information has not reached unapproved tools. An unvalidated output presented to a decision-maker is a reporting failure, whatever produced it.

### Model risk, data caveats and human accountability

Predictive models learn from past projects and inherit their pathologies: optimism bias in estimates, sandbagged forecasts and thin samples of comparable work. Their outputs are probabilistic, degrade when context shifts — a new supplier, a novel technology — and can be systematically wrong while appearing precise. Boards face a specific hazard in automation bias: an unexplained model rating can anchor a gate decision more powerfully than the evidence justifies. Governance therefore extends to the tooling itself: models, prompts and thresholds belong under version control as governed artefacts; data lineage and access rights must be documented; and the assurance function should review the AI pipeline — data, calibration, failure history — with the same independence it applies to any other source of management information. Where an AI-derived indicator informs a gate recommendation, the gate record should say so.

Accountability does not transfer to a tool. The sponsor remains singly accountable for the investment, the board for decisions within its terms of reference, and the delivery leader for the integrity of information presented to both. Every AI-derived item in a governance pack must carry a named human owner able to explain and stand behind it, and AI use in preparing governance information must itself be disclosed and auditable. An escalation raised by an algorithm is a prompt to judgement, never a substitute for it — the classic failures of confused roles and orphaned decisions are amplified rather than cured when "the system flagged it" replaces a named owner.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI may draft the pack, flag the exception and forecast the outturn; only accountable people hold decision rights, exercise tolerances and approve gates. Every machine-generated signal placed before a board must be explainable, validated and owned by a competent professional — a score that no one can explain has no place in a decision that someone must own.

### Key points

- Governance directs and constrains; management plans and executes. The sponsor and board govern; the delivery leader manages within delegated limits.
- Four structures anchor every design: a single accountable sponsor, a board small enough to decide, a delivery leader, and an assurance function reporting to the governance tier — never to the delivery leader it examines.
- Authority flows down through explicit delegated tolerances; escalation is the mirrored return path, triggered by a *forecast* breach, not an actual one.
- Track tolerance consumption cumulatively as well as per event: single-event tests decide who is escalated to, cumulative tests who may approve.
- Stage gates force positive re-authorisation of continued investment at commitment points, supported by independent assurance proportionate to risk.
- AI changes the tempo of governance, not its architecture: continuous forecasts and exception signals, but never a decision right allocated to a system.
- Every AI-generated figure, forecast or flag before a governance body must be explainable, validated and owned by a named professional — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Practice questions

**1.** A project has a budget at completion of $8,000,000. The delivery leader holds a cost tolerance of ±3%; the sponsor holds a cumulative delegation of +7.5%. Previously approved changes total $150,000. A new event is forecast to cost +$260,000. Who decides?

- A. The delivery leader, because cumulative changes remain under the sponsor's limit
- B. The sponsor, because the event exceeds the delivery leader's tolerance but cumulative consumption is within the sponsor's delegation
- C. The board, because cumulative changes now exceed $400,000
- D. The delivery leader, with the board notified at the next cycle

**Correct answer: B.** The event exceeds the delivery leader's $240,000 tolerance (3% × $8,000,000), while cumulative consumption ($410,000) remains within the sponsor's $600,000 delegation.

**2.** Under management by exception, when must the delivery leader escalate to the sponsor?

- A. When actual costs have breached the delegated tolerance
- B. When the board requests an updated forecast at a stage gate
- C. As soon as a forecast indicates that a delegated tolerance will be breached
- D. Only when both cost and schedule tolerances have been breached simultaneously

**Correct answer: C.** Escalation triggers on a forecast breach so the higher tier can decide before commitment; waiting for actuals escalates history, and any single tolerance dimension suffices.

**3.** An AI tool assigns a project a high-confidence "red" delivery-risk score ahead of a stage gate. What must be true before the score may inform the gate decision?

- A. The tool must be formally accredited by the Institute
- B. The score must be validated, explainable and owned by a named, competent professional
- C. The gate decision must be deferred until the score returns to amber or green
- D. The score may be used as-is, since gate authority remains with the board in any case

**Correct answer: B.** Under "AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable", machine-generated signals inform decisions only once a named human has validated and can explain them; the Institute accredits no tools, and an unexplained score must neither delay nor silently anchor a decision.

## Chapter 6: Scope and Requirements Management

Scope and requirements management converts stakeholder need into a controlled, deliverable commitment: needs are elicited as requirements; requirements are analysed, prioritised and structured; the structure is baselined or ordered; and all subsequent change, delivery and acceptance is judged against it. This chapter sets out the definitions, the operating procedure, a worked prioritisation example, and the boundary between what AI may draft and what only an accountable professional may decide.

### 6.1 Requirements and scope: precise definitions

A **requirement** is a documented, verifiable statement of a need, capability or condition that a solution must satisfy to deliver value to its stakeholders. Requirements are conventionally layered: **business requirements** state why the organisation is investing; **stakeholder requirements** state what particular groups need from the outcome; **solution requirements** state what the delivered product or service must do (**functional**) and how well it must do it (**non-functional** — performance, security, usability, compliance); and **transition requirements** state what is needed temporarily to move from the current to the future state.

**Scope** is the boundary drawn around what the project will and will not deliver. The discipline distinguishes **product scope** — the features and functions of the deliverable itself — from **project scope** — the work that must be performed to produce it. The two are inseparable: every element of project scope should exist to satisfy an element of product scope, and every requirement should trace to work that delivers it.

**Elicitation** is the structured activity of drawing requirements out of stakeholders, documents, systems and observation. The Institute deliberately prefers *elicitation* to the weaker *gathering*: requirements are rarely lying about waiting to be collected. They must be surfaced through recognised techniques, **analysed** for completeness, consistency and feasibility, and **prioritised**, because stakeholder demand almost always exceeds the capacity of any funded delivery.

### 6.2 The standards context

The candidate should understand, at working level, where these practices sit in the wider body of professional guidance. ISO 21502 treats scope definition and control as core practices within a governed life cycle. The PMI *PMBOK Guide* tradition frames scope management as a flow from planning through requirements collection, scope definition, work breakdown, validation and control. ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148 provides the engineering-grade treatment of requirements quality — each requirement singular, unambiguous, feasible and verifiable. Business analysis guidance such as the IIBA *BABOK Guide* elaborates elicitation and analysis technique, while the Scrum framework defines the **product backlog** as the single, ordered source of work in adaptive delivery. The PML-AI examination does not test recall of clause numbers; it tests application of the shared discipline these sources describe in predictive, agile and hybrid settings alike. Reference to these publications is contextual only and implies no accreditation by, or affiliation with, their issuing bodies.

### 6.3 Running an elicitation and prioritisation cycle

Requirements work is a repeatable procedure, not an inspired conversation:

1. **Segment the stakeholders.** From the stakeholder register, group parties by interest and knowledge, then match technique to group: structured interviews for sponsors and senior users; facilitated workshops for cross-functional groups where conflict must surface; surveys for large user populations; observation for operational staff whose practice is tacit; document and system analysis for regulatory and legacy constraints; prototyping where needs are genuinely uncertain.
2. **Capture into a single register.** Record every candidate requirement with a unique identifier, source, statement of need (not solution), rationale, type and owner.
3. **Analyse.** Remove duplicates, test each statement for ambiguity and verifiability, and resolve conflicts by facilitated negotiation — escalating irreconcilable conflicts to the sponsor rather than quietly choosing a side.
4. **Prioritise.** Use MoSCoW (Must, Should, Could, Won't-this-time), a weighted scoring model, or cost-of-delay ranking, as fits the delivery approach.
5. **Confirm.** Walk the prioritised set back through the contributing stakeholders and record sign-off.

AI-mined candidate requirements enter this cycle only as proposals at step 2; the validation and ownership duties of section 6.7 apply in full.

### 6.4 From requirements to structure: breakdown or backlog

Once analysed and prioritised, requirements must be organised into a structure that can be planned, estimated and controlled. The choice is not stylistic: it follows from the delivery approach selected during planning, and the competent professional can justify which structure fits which component of the work.

**Predictive route: the work breakdown structure (WBS).** The WBS is a deliverable-oriented, hierarchical decomposition of the total scope. Decompose top-down: level 1 the project, level 2 the major deliverables, continuing until each **work package** can be reliably estimated and assigned to a single owner (the 8/80-hour heuristic). Enforce the *100 per cent rule* at every level — children must sum to exactly the parent's scope, no more and no less — and write a WBS dictionary entry (boundaries, acceptance criteria, exclusions) for each work package before mapping packages to the organisation as control accounts.

**Adaptive route: the prioritised backlog.** The backlog is an ordered, emergent list in which position expresses priority and detail is added just in time. Decompose epics into features and features into user stories, each carrying acceptance criteria. Keep the backlog appropriately detailed (fine near the top, coarse below), estimated and prioritised, and admit stories to a sprint only once they meet the definition of ready.

**Hybrid route.** Hold stable, contracted scope in a WBS and evolving exploratory scope in a backlog, mapping each backlog area to the WBS element it rolls up to so that cost and progress still integrate.

### 6.5 Worked example: MoSCoW prioritisation with a capacity check

1. **Setup.** An e-permitting platform release has 10 two-week sprints available; the team's demonstrated velocity is 48 story points per sprint. After elicitation and analysis, the prioritised backlog totals: Must-have 274 points, Should-have 130 points, Could-have 96 points (Won't-this-time items are excluded from the release).
2. **Method.** Delivery capacity = velocity × sprints. Governance guideline (as in DSDM-style MoSCoW practice): Must-have effort should not exceed ~60 per cent of capacity, so the Should and Could items form the contingency pool.
3. **Substitution.** Capacity = 48 × 10 = **480 points**. Must-have ceiling = 0.60 × 480 = **288 points**; actual Must-have demand = 274 ≤ 288, a margin of 288 − 274 = **14 points**. Committed scope = Must + Should = 274 + 130 = **404 points**, leaving 480 − 404 = **76 points** for Could-have items. Total demand = 274 + 130 + 96 = **500 points**, an oversubscription of 500 − 480 = 20 points (104.2 per cent of capacity).
4. **Result.** The release is baselined at 480 points: all Must and Should items plus 76 points of Could items; the remaining 96 − 76 = **20 points** of Could items sit below the cut line.
5. **Interpretation.** The Must-have set passes the 60 per cent test, so even significant estimating error should not threaten the guaranteed minimum scope. The 20 deferred points remain on the backlog — deferral is a visible, sponsor-approved decision, not deletion.

### 6.6 Baselining, change control and the golden thread

In predictive and hybrid settings, the approved scope statement, WBS and WBS dictionary together form the **scope baseline** — the reference against which delivery performance and all proposed change are judged through integrated change control. Establish it through formal approval and version control. Thereafter, every change follows one path: log it; assess impact on scope, cost, schedule, risk and benefits, using the traceability matrix to find every affected element and test; decide at the delegated authority level; if approved, update the baseline, the matrix and the affected parties.

Adaptive delivery does not freeze scope, but it is not uncontrolled: the backlog changes only through the product owner's ordering decisions, and the iteration commitment and definition of done provide the control surface.

In both modes, the connective tissue is **traceability**: the maintained, bidirectional linkage from business need through requirement, scope element and deliverable to test and acceptance. Maintain the traceability matrix from day one — requirement ID → design element → build item → test case → acceptance result. Each requirement carries **acceptance criteria**: objective, verifiable conditions that must be satisfied before acceptance. Write them as testable thresholds ("permit application processed end-to-end in ≤ 3 working days at 1,000 applications per week"), never adjectives. This enables the crucial distinction between **verification** (the deliverable was built correctly, to specification) and **validation** (the correct deliverable was built, satisfying the need); validation then becomes an audit trail, each requirement confirmed against its recorded criterion. Traceability also makes scope control auditable: an unauthorised addition with no requirement behind it is **scope creep**; a requirement with no deliverable against it is a delivery gap.

### 6.7 AI in scope and requirements work

Scope and requirements management is among the most text-intensive competencies in project delivery, and therefore among the most exposed to AI assistance. Language-model and analytics tooling can mine contracts, transcripts, service tickets and legacy specifications for candidate requirements; cluster duplicates and flag conflicts; draft user stories with first-cut acceptance criteria; propose a WBS decomposition or an ordered backlog; and populate a draft traceability matrix. Predictive models can flag requirements likely to prove volatile, estimate the downstream effect of a proposed change, and detect drift between delivered artefacts and baselined scope. Used well, this shifts the leader's effort from clerical capture towards analysis, negotiation and decision — precisely the activities only a professional can perform.

**The automation boundary.** The candidate is expected to draw this boundary deliberately. Automate the mechanical layers: extraction of candidate requirements, de-duplication and clustering, first-draft story and criteria wording, traceability-matrix population, and continuous consistency checks — orphaned requirements, untraced deliverables, criteria with no planned verification, breaches of the 100 per cent rule. What the leader must validate, and never delegate, are the judgements those drafts feed: whether the elicited set is genuinely complete for *this* project's stakeholders, whether wording is testable and unambiguous, whether prioritisation reflects real business value rather than historical pattern, whether a decomposition suits the chosen delivery approach, and — above all — whether a baseline change is warranted and whether a deliverable is accepted. An AI-generated backlog can look complete while omitting the one requirement that matters most; only professional review against the stakeholders and the contract exposes the gap.

**Model risk and data caveats.** Three caveats govern this competency. First, extraction and generation models produce *plausible* text: a hallucinated or subtly misworded requirement is visually indistinguishable from a sound one, and a fabricated acceptance criterion is more dangerous than a missing one because it invites false confirmation of delivery. Second, decomposition and prioritisation suggestions inherit the biases of their training corpus — a model trained on unlike projects will confidently propose an unsuitable structure, and unstated stakeholder needs, by definition, never appear in the documents a model reads. Third, requirements material is frequently commercially sensitive or personal; feeding it to external tooling without confidentiality, retention and data-protection safeguards is a governance failure regardless of the analytical benefit. AI-assisted outputs used in scope work must therefore be version-controlled alongside the baseline they inform.

**Human accountability.** Accountability is not transferable to tooling. The scope baseline is approved by named governance authorities; change is disposed by the accountable board on a professional's recommendation; acceptance is confirmed by the designated acceptor against verifiable criteria. Where AI contributed to any of these, the delivery leader owns the result as if authored personally, records that AI assistance was used, and must be able to explain and defend the decision without reference to the tool. An unvalidated AI output in a scope baseline is not efficiency; it is unexamined scope.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI may draft the requirement, propose the decomposition, populate the traceability matrix and flag the creeping scope — but the requirement set is confirmed with stakeholders, the baseline is approved, change is authorised and delivery is accepted only by an accountable professional who can explain, evidence and own every disposition.

### 6.8 Common pitfalls

- Eliciting only from the loudest or most senior stakeholders, then discovering operational requirements during acceptance.
- Recording solutions ("use product X") instead of needs, foreclosing better options.
- Breaking the 100 per cent rule, or structuring the WBS by department rather than deliverable, so gaps and overlaps hide.
- Prioritisation collapse: allowing every requirement to be classed Must-have, which destroys the contingency pool.
- Scope creep through undocumented "small favours" and gold-plating that never pass through change control.
- Baselining before known conflicts are resolved, guaranteeing early change traffic.
- Unverifiable acceptance criteria ("fast", "user-friendly") that make validation a negotiation instead of a test.
- Building the traceability matrix retrospectively for the audit, too late to control anything.
- Accepting AI-generated requirements, classifications or impact assessments without human validation and named ownership.

### Key points

- Requirements are layered — business, stakeholder, solution (functional and non-functional) and transition — and product scope is distinct from project scope; each must trace to the other.
- Requirements are *elicited* through techniques matched to stakeholder groups, captured as needs rather than solutions, analysed for ambiguity and conflict, prioritised and confirmed with stakeholders.
- Predictive delivery structures scope as a WBS governed by the 100 per cent rule and a WBS dictionary; adaptive delivery uses an ordered, emergent backlog; hybrid delivery uses both, mapped together so cost and progress integrate.
- In MoSCoW practice, Must-have effort should not exceed roughly 60 per cent of demonstrated capacity, so the Should- and Could-have items form the contingency pool; items below the cut line are visibly deferred, not deleted.
- The scope baseline (or the product-owner-ordered backlog) is the sole reference for change: every proposed change is logged, impact-assessed via the traceability matrix, decided at the delegated authority level and reflected in an updated baseline.
- Acceptance criteria must be verifiable thresholds, enabling the distinction between verification (built to specification) and validation (satisfies the need); orphaned work signals scope creep, and untraced requirements signal delivery gaps.
- AI may automate the mechanical layers of requirements work, but completeness, testability, prioritisation, change and acceptance judgements remain with a named, accountable professional: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Practice questions

**Question 1.** A release has 8 sprints available and a demonstrated velocity of 50 story points per sprint. The prioritised backlog shows Must-have demand of 260 points. Applying the MoSCoW governance guideline in this chapter, which statement is correct?

A. The Must-have set is acceptable, because 260 points is below the 400-point capacity.
B. The Must-have set is acceptable, because Must-haves may consume up to 80 per cent of capacity.
C. The Must-have set breaches the guideline, because 260 points exceeds the 240-point ceiling (60 per cent of 400).
D. The guideline cannot be applied, because velocity is relevant only to Could-have items.

**Answer: C.** Capacity is 50 × 8 = 400 points and the Must-have ceiling is 0.60 × 400 = 240 points, so a 260-point Must-have set erodes the contingency pool and must be renegotiated.

**Question 2.** At handover a deliverable meets every documented specification, yet the operational users demonstrate that it does not resolve the business need that justified the investment. Which statement best describes the situation?

A. Verification has failed and validation has passed.
B. Verification has passed but validation has failed.
C. Both verification and validation have passed, since the specification was met.
D. This is scope creep, because the deliverable exceeded its requirements.

**Answer: B.** Verification confirms the deliverable was built correctly to specification, which occurred; validation confirms the correct deliverable was built to satisfy the need, which it was not.

**Question 3.** An AI tool drafts requirements from workshop transcripts; one drafted requirement is later found to be hallucinated after being admitted to the baseline. Under the Institute's doctrine, who is accountable?

A. The tool vendor, since the model generated the erroneous text.
B. No one, since hallucination is a known and accepted model limitation.
C. The stakeholders who attended the workshop recorded in the transcript.
D. The professional who admitted it to the baseline without validation and named ownership.

**Answer: D.** Accountability is not transferable to tooling: every AI output must be explainable, validated and owned by a competent human before it enters a baseline — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

## Chapter 7 — Planning and Execution

This chapter examines the competency at the centre of delivery leadership: constructing an integrated plan, estimating with declared confidence, executing to a controlled cadence, and taking proportionate corrective action when performance diverges from commitment. It also establishes how AI-enabled planning and forecasting tools are used within the Institute's doctrine of professional accountability.

### 7.1 The integrated plan: a single system of commitments

Planning, in the sense examined by this credential, is not the production of documents but the construction of a single, internally consistent model of the work. An **integrated project plan** is one in which four elements — scope, schedule, resource and cost — are derived from one another and reconcile without contradiction. Scope defines *what* will be delivered; the schedule defines *when*; the resource plan defines *who and with what*; the cost plan defines *at what expenditure*. The test of integration is traceability: every scheduled activity traces to a scope element, every scope element carries a resource assignment, and every resource assignment generates a cost. A plan that fails this test — a schedule containing work absent from the scope statement, or a budget that does not reflect the resource histogram — is not a plan but a collection of unconnected assertions.

The structural spine of integration is the **work breakdown structure (WBS)**: a hierarchical, deliverable-oriented decomposition of the total scope. The WBS provides the common coding structure through which schedule activities, cost accounts and responsibility assignments are cross-referenced. Once the four elements are mutually consistent, they are **baselined**: formally approved, placed under change control, and thereafter changed only through the governance mechanisms established at initiation (Chapters 3 and 4). The baseline is the reference against which all subsequent performance measurement is made; without it, the terms *variance*, *progress* and *forecast* have no defined meaning.

### 7.2 Standards context

The concepts in this chapter align with the international consensus expressed in ISO 21502 (project management guidance) and ISO 21508 (earned value management), and with the bodies of practice maintained by the major professional associations, which distinguish predictive (plan-driven), adaptive (agile) and hybrid life cycles. PRINCE2-style methods contribute the discipline of stage-based authorisation and management by exception; earned value standards contribute the performance measurement baseline and its derived indices. PCI's syllabus draws on these sources generically: candidates are examined on the underlying logic, not on any single proprietary method, and PCI makes no claim of accreditation by, or affiliation with, the bodies that publish them.

### 7.3 Producing the integrated plan: the method

An integrated plan is one dataset expressed several ways, not separate documents reconciled after the fact. The Institute expects candidates to follow six steps in order.

1. **Fix the scope baseline.** Decompose the approved scope into a WBS to work-package level; give every package an owner and acceptance criteria. Anything not in the WBS is not in the plan.
2. **Sequence the work.** Build the activity network from the work packages, apply dependencies, and identify the critical path and total float.
3. **Resource-load the schedule.** Assign roles with real availability and level the histogram *before* baselining. **Levelling** extends dates to respect capacity limits; **smoothing** redistributes effort within float. A schedule that assumes 120% utilisation is not a plan; it is a wish.
4. **Cost the resourced schedule.** Derive the time-phased budget from the loaded schedule so that scope, dates and money share one source. This yields the **performance measurement baseline**.
5. **Add contingency explicitly.** Hold contingency against identified risks at a named level of the budget, never smeared invisibly through the estimates.
6. **Baseline under change control**, recording the maturity class and confidence attached to every estimate.

The consistency test is simple: change any one element and the others must be repriced. If scope can grow while the budget stands still, the plan was never integrated.

### 7.4 Estimating and estimate maturity

An **estimate** is a quantified prediction — of duration, effort or cost — made under uncertainty and accompanied by a stated basis and confidence. Three technique families recur throughout the syllabus, with a fourth, machine-assisted, mode of application.

- **Analogous estimating** scales from the actual outcomes of comparable past work. It is rapid, inexpensive and defensible early, but only as reliable as the analogy; a wide band should be declared (for example, of the order of −30% to +50% at concept stage).
- **Parametric estimating** applies a validated statistical relationship between historical data and a driving variable (cost per unit, hours per interface). Its precision depends entirely on the quality of the calibration data and the stability of the underlying relationship.
- **Three-point (PERT) estimating** captures uncertainty explicitly through optimistic (O), most likely (M) and pessimistic (P) values, typically combined through a weighted beta distribution: expected value E = (O + 4M + P) ÷ 6, with standard deviation σ ≈ (P − O) ÷ 6, giving an explicit confidence range.
- **AI-assisted estimating** uses machine-learning tools to propose reference-class ranges and flag optimism in human inputs. The estimator must be able to explain the drivers, validate them against project data, and own the published figure.

Estimates mature. Early, top-down figures produced for feasibility purposes carry wide ranges — often expressed as estimate classes with declared accuracy bands — and are progressively refined into bottom-up, definitive estimates as scope definition improves. The professional obligation is candour: every estimate presented to a sponsor must state its basis, its assumptions, its class and its confidence interval. A single-point figure offered without a range misrepresents knowledge the estimator does not possess; a number without a stated confidence is an opinion.

### 7.5 Worked example: from estimate to baseline to corrective action

A delivery leader plans a work package covering the build and test of **60 system interfaces**.

**Estimate (parametric).** Validated history shows 14 hours per interface at a blended rate of USD 95 per hour.

- Effort = 60 × 14 = **840 hours**
- Budget at completion (BAC) = 840 × 95 = **USD 79,800**

**Duration (three-point).** O = 9 weeks, M = 12 weeks, P = 21 weeks.

- E = (9 + 4 × 12 + 21) ÷ 6 = 78 ÷ 6 = **13 weeks**
- σ ≈ (21 − 9) ÷ 6 = **2 weeks**

The published estimate is therefore 13 weeks ± 2 weeks (approximately 68% confidence of completion between weeks 11 and 15), and the plan is baselined on this basis.

**Status at week 6.** The baseline planned 30 interfaces complete; 24 are actually complete and 384 hours have been booked.

- Planned Value: PV = (30 ÷ 60) × 79,800 = **USD 39,900**
- Earned Value: EV = (24 ÷ 60) × 79,800 = **USD 31,920**
- Actual Cost: AC = 384 × 95 = **USD 36,480**
- Cost Variance: CV = 31,920 − 36,480 = **−USD 4,560**; CPI = 31,920 ÷ 36,480 = **0.875**
- Schedule Variance: SV = 31,920 − 39,900 = **−USD 7,980**; SPI = 31,920 ÷ 39,900 = **0.80**
- Estimate at Completion: EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 79,800 ÷ 0.875 = **USD 91,200**; VAC = 79,800 − 91,200 = **−USD 11,400**

**Interpretation and corrective action.** Productivity is the root cause: 384 ÷ 24 = 16 hours per interface against 14 planned. The proportionate response is a corrective-action ladder, escalated only as far as necessary: first, diagnose why productivity is below plan (here, test-environment availability); second, re-sequence remaining interfaces to protect the critical path; third, adjust resourcing; only if these fail, seek de-scoping or a re-baseline through formal change control. The revised EAC and completion forecast are reported against tolerance immediately, not concealed until they harden into fact.

### 7.6 Execution, monitoring and control

Execution is the conversion of the baselined plan into delivered outputs; **monitoring and control** is the feedback loop that keeps the two aligned. The loop has four steps: measure actual performance; compare against the baseline to identify **variances**; analyse cause and forecast consequence; and select a proportionate response — corrective action within tolerance, escalation when tolerance is threatened, or a formal change to the baseline when the plan itself is no longer viable. Earned value management supplies the standard vocabulary — planned value, earned value and actual cost, with the variances and performance indices derived from them, as applied in the worked example and treated further in Chapter 8. Control is proportionate when the cost and intrusiveness of the response match the significance of the variance; management by exception exists precisely to prevent governance attention being consumed by noise.

In practice, control is a rhythm, not an event: a weekly team-level review of progress and impediments; a monthly baseline-variance review with thresholds agreed in advance (for example, variance beyond ±5% triggers analysis, beyond ±10% triggers escalation); and a standing rule that every red indicator carries a named owner and a dated action. In agile and hybrid delivery, cadence — fixed-length iterations, regular reviews and stable team capacity — performs the same integrating function that the baseline performs in predictive work: it makes throughput measurable and forecasting empirical.

### 7.7 AI in planning and execution

AI changes the economics of this competency; it does not change its logic. Machine-assisted planning tools can draft schedule networks from scope statements, propose resource-loaded sequences and run automated quality checks on plan integrity — dangling logic, missing dependencies, implausible durations, over-allocated resources and misaligned cost and schedule phasing. Predictive models trained on historical project data can generate parametric estimates with quantified ranges and, during execution, produce probabilistic forecasts of completion dates and costs, detect anomalies in progress data and surface emerging variances before they appear in period-end reporting. The integrated plan thus becomes a living dataset rather than a static document. The learning objectives of this chapter stand unchanged: the plan must remain mutually consistent and formally baselined, every estimate must carry a stated confidence, and every corrective action must be proportionate and defensible — whoever, or whatever, first proposed it.

**What the leader automates, and what the leader validates.** The delivery leader should automate the mechanical layer: assembly of progress data from source systems, schedule-quality and resource-loading checks, first-draft parametric and analogous estimates, variance detection within the delivery cadence, and the drafting of routine progress narratives. The leader must validate, before use, the layer on which judgement depends: the assumptions, exclusions and reference class behind any machine-generated estimate; the plausibility of a proposed critical path; the causes — not merely the existence — of a flagged variance; and any recommended corrective action, tested against its cost, risk and stakeholder consequences. Certain decisions are never delegated to tooling: approval of the baseline, the confidence statement attached to an estimate, the selection of corrective action, and any re-baselining, which passes through the change-control route established in the governance chapters.

**Model risk and data caveats.** Predictive outputs are only as representative as the data beneath them. An estimating model trained on projects of a different sector, scale, delivery method or economic era will produce confident but biased figures; the leader must ask what the model learned from and whether that population resembles the current project. Forecasting models degrade silently when progress data is poor — optimistic percent-complete reporting corrupts every downstream projection. Two behavioural risks deserve particular attention: **automation bias**, the tendency to accept a machine-generated number because it is precise, and **anchoring**, where an early AI estimate frames all subsequent negotiation. A spuriously exact figure is still an estimate and must be presented with its range and confidence. Generated narrative is a further hazard: drafted commentary can assert what the data does not support, so every generated statement must be traceable to source records. An output that cannot be explained in plain terms to a sponsor or an assurance reviewer cannot support a baseline decision.

**Human accountability.** Under the Institute's doctrine, accountability for the plan is non-transferable. The leader who baselines an integrated plan owns every number in it, however it was produced, and the leader who acts on a predictive warning — or declines to — owns that judgement equally. Good practice is to record which planning artefacts were AI-assisted, retain the validation evidence alongside the basis of estimate, and present forecasts with their provenance and confidence visible. The examination tests this directly: candidates are expected to determine when an AI-generated schedule, estimate or forecast should be accepted, adjusted or rejected, and to justify the decision.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** A model may draft the schedule, price the estimate and flag the variance first. The delivery leader interrogates the data and assumptions, sets the confidence, approves the baseline and selects the corrective action — and remains personally accountable for every commitment the plan makes.

### 7.8 Common pitfalls

- **Baselining before resource levelling**, producing dates no available team can meet.
- **Hidden padding at every level** instead of explicit, risk-based contingency owned at a named tier.
- **Single-point estimates** published without class or confidence band.
- **Anchoring the three-point on M**, with a token pessimistic value that understates σ.
- **Reading SV as time**: schedule variance in currency trends to zero at completion even on a late project; always check the critical path as well.
- **The rubber baseline** — re-baselining to erase variance rather than to reflect approved change.
- **Self-reported percent complete** without objective earning rules, which inflates EV.
- **Accepting AI-generated estimates unvalidated** — an unexplained figure fails the doctrine that AI proposes and the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Key points

- An integrated plan is a single, mutually consistent dataset in which scope, schedule, resource and cost derive from one another; the WBS is its structural spine and traceability is its test.
- The baseline is formally approved and held under change control; without it, variance, progress and forecast have no defined meaning.
- Estimates are published with basis, class and confidence: analogous for speed, parametric for calibrated precision, three-point (PERT: E = (O + 4M + P) ÷ 6) for explicit uncertainty.
- Monitoring and control is a four-step feedback loop — measure, compare, analyse, respond — governed by management by exception and executed to a fixed cadence.
- Corrective action follows a ladder of proportionality: diagnose, re-sequence, re-resource, and only then de-scope or re-baseline through formal change control.
- AI tools can draft plans, price estimates and flag variances early, but model risk, automation bias and anchoring demand validation of every output before use.
- Accountability is non-transferable: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Practice questions

**1.** A work package has an optimistic duration of 4 weeks, a most likely duration of 6 weeks and a pessimistic duration of 14 weeks. Using PERT, the expected duration is:

A. 6 weeks
B. 7 weeks
C. 8 weeks
D. 9 weeks

**Answer: B.** E = (4 + 4 × 6 + 14) ÷ 6 = 42 ÷ 6 = 7 weeks; the beta weighting counts the most likely value four times.

**2.** A project has BAC = USD 200,000, EV = USD 80,000 and AC = USD 100,000. Assuming current cost performance continues, the estimate at completion (EAC) is:

A. USD 200,000
B. USD 220,000
C. USD 250,000
D. USD 280,000

**Answer: C.** CPI = 80,000 ÷ 100,000 = 0.80, and EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 200,000 ÷ 0.80 = USD 250,000.

**3.** An AI planning tool generates a parametric cost estimate for a work package. Under the Institute's doctrine, the estimate may enter the baseline only when:

A. The tool's vendor certifies the model's accuracy for this class of project.
B. The figure lies within the sponsor's approved budget tolerance.
C. A competent professional can explain its derivation, has validated its inputs and calibration, and accepts ownership of it.
D. The tool has been trained on at least one comparable historical project.

**Answer: C.** AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable — every AI output must be explainable, validated and owned by an accountable human before it becomes a commitment.

## 8. Stakeholder Leadership and Engagement

### 8.1 Stakeholders, engagement and leadership: precise definitions

A **stakeholder** is any individual, group or organisation that can affect, is affected by, or perceives itself to be affected by a decision, activity or outcome of the project. This definition, common in substance to the principal international standards, is deliberately broad in three respects. First, it includes those who merely *perceive* an impact: perception drives behaviour whether or not the underlying impact is real, so a group that wrongly believes it will lose out must still be engaged. Second, it embraces those who can affect the project as well as those affected by it — regulators, funders, interface owners and suppliers alongside end users and operational teams. Third, it is dynamic: the stakeholder population changes as the project moves through its life cycle, and an analysis that was accurate at initiation may be dangerously stale by execution.

**Stakeholder engagement** is the deliberate, two-way process of involving stakeholders so that their needs, expectations and concerns are understood and addressed, and their support for project outcomes is won and retained. The Institute follows contemporary usage in preferring *engagement* to *management*: senior stakeholders, regulators and communities cannot be "managed" in any directive sense, but they can be understood, informed, consulted and influenced. **Stakeholder leadership** goes further still. It denotes the active shaping of attitudes and alliances — mobilising sponsors, building coalitions for change and confronting resistance constructively — and it is this leadership dimension that distinguishes the delivery leader from the delivery administrator.

**Communication** is the principal instrument of engagement but is not synonymous with it. Engagement also encompasses consultation, negotiation, joint problem-solving and shared decision-making. The **communication plan** is the documented expression of the engagement strategy: it matches message, channel, frequency and sender to the needs of each stakeholder group, and it is derived from analysis rather than composed from habit.

### 8.2 The analytical dimensions: interest, influence and attitude

Rigorous engagement rests on three analytical dimensions:

- **Interest** — the degree to which the stakeholder's objectives, work or wellbeing are affected by the project: the size and nature of the stake.
- **Influence** (or power) — the stakeholder's capacity to affect decisions, resources, approvals or opinion, whether through formal authority or informal networks.
- **Attitude** — the stakeholder's current disposition towards the project, conventionally graded from *unaware* through *resistant* and *neutral* to *supportive* and *leading*.

The first two dimensions support classification, most familiarly through the power–interest grid associated with Mendelow. The third converts classification into strategy: a high-influence supporter and a high-influence resistor occupy the same cell of the grid yet demand entirely different treatment. Complementary lenses include the salience model of Mitchell, Agle and Wood, which ranks stakeholders by power, legitimacy and urgency, and the engagement assessment matrix, which records each stakeholder's *current* against *desired* engagement level so that the gap between the two defines where engagement effort must concentrate.

### 8.3 Governing frameworks and standards

Stakeholder engagement is an established practice area across the recognised bodies of knowledge. ISO 21502 treats it as an integral project management practice spanning identification, analysis and ongoing engagement; the PMBOK Guide addresses it as a dedicated performance domain interwoven with planning, delivery and measurement; PRINCE2 requires a documented communication management approach reviewed at each stage boundary; and the APM Body of Knowledge frames stakeholder engagement as a core interpersonal and professional discipline. Freeman's stakeholder theory supplies the underlying management thought: organisations create value for, and are accountable to, a constituency wider than their shareholders. The candidate is not examined on the clause structure of any one standard, but on the shared logic these frameworks encode — identify, analyse, plan, engage, monitor and adapt.

Engagement also operates *within* governance, not alongside it. The sponsor owns the business case and is simultaneously the project's most consequential stakeholder; boards and steering committees are standing engagement forums; and escalation routes defined in the governance framework are themselves engagement channels of last resort.

### 8.4 The engagement cycle

The elements of this chapter form a cycle, not a checklist. Identification and analysis produce a defensible picture of who matters, why, and with what disposition. That analysis drives a differentiated engagement strategy, which the communication plan operationalises. Delivery then generates evidence — attendance, responsiveness, sentiment, decision latency — against which effectiveness is evaluated and the approach adapted as attitudes and coalitions shift. Stakeholder analysis is perishable; the cycle is what keeps it current.

The discipline also integrates horizontally. Stakeholder attitudes are a recurrent source of both threat and opportunity in the risk register; benefits are realised only when stakeholders change how they work; and requirements, acceptance and adoption all depend on the quality of engagement.

### 8.5 Method: scoring stakeholders by interest, influence and attitude

The analysis that underpins every engagement decision can be performed as a repeatable, auditable procedure. The Institute expects candidates to be able to reproduce each step, including the arithmetic, under examination conditions.

1. **Identify.** Assemble the stakeholder register from the organisation chart, the contract and supplier structure, the benefits map and a facilitated workshop. Record individuals where influence is personal; group where it is collective.
2. **Score influence and interest.** Rate influence `I` (ability to affect the project) and interest `S` (degree to which the project affects them) on a 1–5 scale, then compute a priority score `P = I × S`.
3. **Assess attitude.** Rate current attitude `A` on a −2 (active resistance) to +2 (champion) scale, and set a *realistic* target attitude `T`. The engagement gap is `G = max(0, T − A)`: a stakeholder already at or above target has a zero gap.
4. **Weight the effort.** Compute an effort weight `W = P × G` and allocate the team's finite engagement capacity in proportion to `W`.
5. **Derive the strategy.** High `P` with a positive gap indicates *manage closely*; high `P` with no gap, *keep satisfied and protect*; lower `P`, *keep informed* where interest dominates or *monitor* where both dimensions are low — refined, always, by attitude.
6. **Re-score on a fixed cadence** and re-allocate as attitudes and coalitions move.

### 8.6 Worked example: allocating engagement effort on a warehouse-system replacement

**Setup.** A logistics company is replacing its warehouse management system over 14 months, affecting roughly 600 staff. The delivery leader, change manager and sponsor can jointly commit **190 hours per quarter** to deliberate engagement. Six stakeholder groups are scored.

| Stakeholder group | I | S | P = I × S | A | T | G | W = P × G |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Executive sponsor board | 5 | 3 | 15 | +1 | +2 | 1 | 15 |
| Warehouse operations managers | 4 | 5 | 20 | −1 | +1 | 2 | 40 |
| Warehouse shift staff (via representatives) | 2 | 5 | 10 | −2 | 0 | 2 | 20 |
| IT operations | 3 | 4 | 12 | 0 | +1 | 1 | 12 |
| Finance and controlling | 3 | 3 | 9 | +1 | +1 | 0 | 0 |
| Key logistics customers | 4 | 2 | 8 | 0 | +1 | 1 | 8 |

**Substitution.** `ΣW = 15 + 40 + 20 + 12 + 0 + 8 = 95`. Hours per group = `190 × W ÷ 95 = 2 × W`.

**Result.** Operations managers receive `2 × 40 = 80` hours; shift staff `40`; sponsor board `30`; IT operations `24`; customers `16`; finance `0` from this formula — but professional judgement overrides the arithmetic: a zero-gap group still receives baseline reporting so a supportive attitude does not decay unnoticed.

**Interpretation.** The largest single investment goes not to the most senior group but to the operations managers, whose combination of high priority (20) and a two-point attitude gap makes them the pivotal coalition: if they move from −1 to +1, they carry the shift staff with them. At the quarterly re-score, suppose they have moved to 0: `G` falls to 1, `W` falls to 20, and roughly 40 hours are released to redeploy — evidence, not intuition, drives the adaptation.

### 8.7 From analysis to the communication plan

Convert each register row into a plan row that fixes **message, channel, frequency and sender**. For the two highest-weight groups above: operations managers receive an operational-impact briefing (what changes on their shift floor, and what they control), face-to-face, fortnightly, delivered by the operations director — a trusted peer authority — not by the project office; shift staff receive short visual updates through their representatives, monthly, co-presented with a respected supervisor. Sender selection is a design decision of equal weight to message content: the same message from the wrong sender routinely hardens resistance.

### 8.8 Sustaining and adapting engagement

Evaluate effectiveness with observable evidence — attendance and contribution rates, survey attitude scores, issue-raising behaviour (silence from a high-interest group is a warning, not a success) — and re-run the scoring at least quarterly or after any major event such as re-baselining. Where AI-enabled tools contribute attitude ratings or drafts to this cycle, the disciplines of the following sections apply in full. Stakeholder attitude data is sensitive personal information and must be handled under the confidentiality standards of Chapter 2.

### 8.9 AI-enabled stakeholder intelligence

Artificial intelligence is changing stakeholder leadership from a periodically refreshed mapping exercise into a continuously informed discipline. AI-enabled tools can propose draft stakeholder maps from organisational data, and analyse large volumes of correspondence, meeting minutes, consultation responses and collaboration-platform activity to surface sentiment trends, emerging concerns and shifts in tone among key groups. Predictive models can flag early indicators of disengagement — declining meeting attendance, slower response times, reduced participation in reviews — before they harden into resistance. Generative tools can draft tailored communications for different audiences, propose engagement approaches for a given stakeholder profile, and summarise long consultation threads into decision-ready briefings. Used well, this augments the leader's situational awareness; it does not replace the judgement, empathy and political acumen that stakeholder leadership demands: trust cannot be delegated to a model.

### 8.10 What the leader automates — and what the leader validates

The certified Project Management Leader – AI draws a deliberate boundary between automation and validation. Suitable for automation are the mechanical layers of the competency: collation of stakeholder data, first-draft communication tailoring, sentiment and engagement analytics, distribution-list hygiene, and the routine tracking of actions arising from engagement events. Reserved for human validation are the layers where meaning and relationships are at stake: the interpretation of sentiment signals, the classification of any stakeholder's attitude or influence, the wording of sensitive messages, and every decision about who is engaged, when, and by whom. A model may suggest that a sponsor's confidence is declining; only the leader can test that inference in conversation, understand its causes and decide how to respond. No AI-generated communication should reach a stakeholder without review by an accountable professional, and no stakeholder should ever discover that a message concerning them was machine-authored and unowned.

### 8.11 Model risk, data protection and human accountability

Stakeholder analytics carries distinctive model risk. Sentiment models are trained on general language corpora and routinely misread irony, cultural register, professional politeness and multilingual nuance; a courteous refusal may score as positive while candid, committed challenge scores as negative. Engagement data is partial by construction — it captures written, digital interaction and is silent on corridor conversations, informal coalitions and the views of stakeholders who simply do not write things down. Treating such outputs as objective truth risks systematically misjudging precisely those stakeholders who matter most. There are also serious data-protection and ethical constraints: analysing individuals' communications and behaviour may engage privacy law, employment regulation and workforce-representation requirements, and covert sentiment monitoring can destroy the very trust engagement is meant to build. The leader ensures that any stakeholder analytics is transparent, proportionate, lawfully based and consistent with organisational policy — and records the provenance and limitations of the data on which conclusions rest.

Accountability for stakeholder relationships cannot be transferred to a tool. The professional who relies on AI-derived stakeholder insight must be able to explain, in plain language, what the tool analysed, what it inferred, how confident that inference is and why the resulting engagement decision was justified. Where an AI-informed judgement affects an individual's standing — for example, labelling a stakeholder a "blocker" — the leader treats the label as a hypothesis to be tested respectfully, never a verdict to be filed. Governance forums should be told when material stakeholder assessments are AI-assisted, and the engagement strategy remains a document owned, signed and defended by the project leader. In the examination, candidates are expected to demonstrate this boundary: knowing what to delegate to the machine, and what may never be.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI may map the stakeholder landscape, score sentiment, and draft the message. The professional validates the inference, owns the relationship, signs the communication and answers for the trust the project builds or loses. Insight can be automated; leadership cannot.

### 8.12 Common pitfalls

- **Static analysis.** Scoring once at initiation and never re-scoring; attitudes and coalitions move, and last quarter's plan quietly becomes wrong.
- **Confusing volume with influence.** The loudest stakeholder is scored highest while quiet, decisive influencers are underweighted.
- **Broadcast mistaken for engagement.** Communications *sent* is measured; attitude *moved* is not.
- **Neglecting supporters.** All effort flows to resisters while champions, receiving nothing, drift to neutral.
- **Unrealistic targets.** Setting `T = +2` for every group inflates gaps and spreads effort too thinly to move anyone.
- **Sender mismatch.** Peer-credibility messages issued from the project office rather than a trusted equivalent.
- **Careless register handling.** Candid attitude assessments left accessible to their subjects — or sanitised until analytically useless.
- **Letting the arithmetic decide.** Treating `W` as an instruction rather than a prompt for judgement, including accepting AI-derived sentiment scores without human validation.

### Key points

- A stakeholder is anyone who can affect, is affected by, or *perceives* themselves to be affected by the project; perception drives behaviour, and the population changes across the life cycle, so analysis is perishable.
- Engagement is two-way and broader than communication; stakeholder *leadership* — shaping attitudes, coalitions and sponsorship — distinguishes the delivery leader from the administrator.
- Analysis rests on interest, influence and attitude: the power–interest grid classifies, while attitude and the current-to-target gap convert classification into strategy.
- The scoring method `P = I × S`, `G = max(0, T − A)`, `W = P × G` allocates finite effort in proportion to weight, re-scored on a fixed cadence; the arithmetic prompts judgement, never replaces it.
- The communication plan fixes message, channel, frequency and sender for each group; sender credibility is as consequential as message content.
- AI can collate data, score sentiment, predict disengagement and draft communications, but carries model risk, partial data and privacy obligations; interpretation, attitude classification, sensitive wording and engagement decisions remain human.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: every AI-derived stakeholder insight must be explainable, validated and owned by an accountable professional.

### Practice questions

**1.** A residents' association believes, incorrectly, that a project will close its access road, and analysis confirms no such impact will occur. According to the definition of a stakeholder, the delivery leader should:

A. Exclude the association from the register, since no real impact exists
B. Record the association but take no action until it escalates formally
C. Engage the association, because perceived impact drives behaviour whether or not it is real
D. Refer the association to the legal team to correct the misunderstanding

**Answer: C.** The definition explicitly includes those who *perceive* themselves affected, because perception drives behaviour regardless of the underlying facts.

**2.** Using the Institute's scoring method, a stakeholder group is rated influence 4, interest 3, current attitude −1 and target attitude +1. Its effort weight `W` is:

A. 12
B. 14
C. 24
D. 48

**Answer: C.** `P = 4 × 3 = 12`, `G = (+1) − (−1) = 2`, so `W = 12 × 2 = 24`.

**3.** Under the doctrine "AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable", which activity is appropriate to delegate to an AI-enabled tool?

A. Final classification of a sponsor's attitude in the stakeholder register
B. Releasing a tailored communication to a resistant group without human review
C. First-draft collation of engagement data and sentiment trends for human validation
D. Deciding which stakeholders are engaged, when, and by whom

**Answer: C.** Mechanical collation and first-draft analytics are suitable for automation; attitude classification, message release and engagement decisions require a validating, accountable human.

## 9. Procurement

Procurement is the discipline through which a project acquires the goods, services and works it cannot, or should not, produce itself. On most projects of any scale the majority of the budget is spent through suppliers, which makes procurement one of the principal channels through which cost, schedule and risk actually flow. A project management leader who cannot shape a procurement strategy, run a defensible tender and read a contract's risk allocation is managing only a fraction of the delivery system. This chapter establishes the concepts the examination expects a candidate to command, applies them through step-by-step methods and a worked example, and closes with the governance of AI-assisted sourcing.

### Core concepts defined

**Procurement** is the end-to-end process of obtaining goods, services or works from parties external to the delivering organisation, from identification of need through contract award to contract closure. It is broader than *purchasing* (the transactional act of ordering and paying) and narrower than *commercial management* (which also embraces revenue, claims and the wider business relationship).

A **procurement strategy** is the documented, approved approach that answers the structural questions before any supplier is engaged: what will be bought and what retained in-house; how requirements will be packaged into contracts; which sourcing route each package will use; what pricing and risk-allocation model each contract will carry; and how the supply market's capacity, competitiveness and appetite condition these choices. The strategy must be traceable to the project's objectives and risk appetite — maximising price competition on a schedule-critical package may be internally coherent yet wrong for the project.

The **make-or-buy decision** is the structured analysis that precedes strategy: a comparison of performing work internally against acquiring it externally, weighing relative cost, capacity and capability, control over intellectual property and sensitive data, the strategic importance of retaining the competence, and the transfer or retention of risk. Its output — the buy list — defines the scope over which the rest of the procurement process operates.

A **sourcing route** is the mechanism by which suppliers are approached: open competition, restricted (pre-qualified) competition, negotiated procedures, framework agreements with call-offs, or single- and sole-source awards. **Tendering** is the formal cycle of soliciting and receiving offers — typically through a pre-qualification questionnaire, an invitation to tender or request for proposal, and a request for quotation — conducted under published rules. **Supplier evaluation and selection** is the disciplined comparison of offers against pre-declared, weighted criteria, balancing price against quality, capability, financial standing and whole-life cost rather than lowest price alone. The resulting **contract** is the legal instrument that fixes scope, price mechanism and risk allocation; the choice among fixed-price, cost-reimbursable, time-and-materials and target-cost arrangements is, at root, a decision about which party carries which risk and what behaviour the incentive structure will produce.

**Ethical procurement** denotes the integrity conditions under which all of the above must be conducted: fairness, transparency, impartiality, equal treatment of bidders, managed conflicts of interest, and the absolute exclusion of bribery, collusion and bid manipulation. **Sustainable procurement** extends the evaluation lens to environmental, social and economic impacts across the supply chain and the asset's life.

### The governing frameworks

Procurement sits within a recognised professional and legal architecture. PMI's PMBOK Guide addresses the planning, conduct and control of procurements; the APM Body of Knowledge situates procurement within integrated commercial and contract management. ISO 20400 gives guidance on embedding sustainability in procurement, and ISO 37001 specifies anti-bribery management systems; legislation such as the UK Bribery Act 2010, the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention and modern-slavery disclosure regimes gives ethical obligations statutory force. Public-sector procurement is additionally governed by jurisdictional regimes — the UK's procurement legislation, the EU directives, the UNCITRAL Model Law — whose common principles of transparency, proportionality and non-discrimination this Body of Knowledge treats as good practice for any sector. Standard contract families such as NEC and FIDIC supply tested risk-allocation templates, and the codes of the procurement profession's chartered bodies define the conduct standards the examination assumes. Candidates are examined on principles, not on the clause-level content of any statute or standard form.

### How the elements relate

The elements form a deliberate sequence in which each decision constrains the next. Make-or-buy defines *what* is bought; the strategy decides *how* the buying is structured; the sourcing route and tender generate *offers* under conditions that keep competition genuine; evaluation converts offers into a *defensible selection*; and the contract converts selection into an *enforceable risk allocation* that the delivery team must then manage. Ethics and sustainability are not a final step but a property of the whole chain: a technically sound evaluation built on a conflicted panel is void of integrity, and integrity failures are rarely recoverable.

### Step by step: conducting a make-or-buy analysis

A defensible make-or-buy analysis proceeds in six steps.

1. **Specify the requirement in output terms.** Define what the project must obtain — capability, capacity, quality, timescale — without presuming how it will be produced.
2. **Establish whole-life cost for each option over a common period.** For the make option, include development, ongoing support, infrastructure and management overhead; for the buy option, include acquisition, implementation, recurring charges, contract management and exit costs.
3. **Risk-adjust both options.** Identify the principal risks of each route, estimate expected values (probability multiplied by impact) and add them to the base cost, so the comparison reflects exposure and not merely estimates.
4. **Assess non-financial factors.** Consider strategic fit and core competence, intellectual property, internal capacity and its opportunity cost, speed to delivery, supply-market maturity, and dependency or lock-in.
5. **Test sensitivity.** Vary the key assumptions across plausible ranges. If the ranking reverses within normal estimating uncertainty, the financial case alone is not decisive and the recommendation must rest on the qualitative factors.
6. **Recommend, document and seek approval.** Record the recommended strategy — route to market, contract type and risk allocation — in the procurement management plan and obtain governance sign-off before approaching the market.

### Step by step: designing the tender and evaluation

1. Select a sourcing route (open, restricted, framework call-off or negotiated) proportionate to value, risk and market conditions.
2. Fix the evaluation criteria, weightings, scoring scale and price formula **before** the invitation to tender is issued, and publish them to all bidders.
3. Separate mandatory pass/fail gates (financial standing, legal compliance, minimum technical thresholds) from scored criteria.
4. Provide narrative descriptors for each point on the scoring scale so that scores can be explained and audited.
5. Have panel members score independently, then moderate to consensus, recording the rationale for every score.
6. Manage integrity throughout: declared conflicts of interest, a single communication channel, equal information to all bidders, and a complete audit trail.
7. Where AI tools are used to summarise bids or flag anomalies, evaluators must still read, validate and own every score.
