### Worked example: from make-or-buy to contract award

A programme requires a document management capability for five years.

**Make option.** Internal build £480,000; support £60,000 per year × 5 = £300,000; base cost £780,000. Risk adjustment: a 30% probability of a 25% development overrun (£120,000) gives an expected value of 0.30 × £120,000 = **£36,000**. Risk-adjusted total: £780,000 + £36,000 = **£816,000**.

**Buy option.** Implementation £350,000; subscription £95,000 per year × 5 = £475,000; base cost £825,000. Risk adjustment: a 10% probability of a £50,000 integration overrun gives 0.10 × £50,000 = **£5,000**. Risk-adjusted total: £825,000 + £5,000 = **£830,000**.

The difference of £14,000 is about 1.7% of the make option — well inside estimating tolerance, so the financial case is not decisive. Because the internal team is fully committed to core delivery and the supply market is mature, the recommended strategy is **buy**, and the analysis, including this reasoning, is recorded for governance approval.

The tender attracts two compliant bids with five-year whole-life prices of £825,000 (Supplier A) and £760,000 (Supplier B). Published weightings: technical 40%, whole-life cost 30%, delivery capability 20%, sustainability and social value 10%. The published price formula awards 10 to the lowest price, others pro rata: Supplier A scores 760,000 ÷ 825,000 × 10 = **9.21**.

| Criterion (weight) | A raw | A weighted | B raw | B weighted |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Technical (40%) | 8.00 | 8.00 × 0.40 = 3.20 | 7.00 | 2.80 |
| Whole-life cost (30%) | 9.21 | 9.21 × 0.30 = 2.76 | 10.00 | 3.00 |
| Delivery capability (20%) | 7.00 | 1.40 | 8.00 | 1.60 |
| Sustainability (10%) | 6.00 | 0.60 | 8.00 | 0.80 |
| **Total** | | **7.96** | | **8.20** |

Supplier B is recommended for award at 8.20 against 7.96. The moderation record explains each score against the published descriptors, and the unsuccessful bidder receives a structured debrief — the process can withstand challenge because every element was fixed in advance and evidenced throughout.

### Common pitfalls

- **Comparing acquisition price rather than whole-life cost**, omitting support, contract management and exit costs from the buy option.
- **Ignoring risk adjustment and optimism bias**, which systematically flatters the make option.
- **Treating small financial margins as decisive** when the difference lies within estimating uncertainty.
- **Setting or altering weightings after bids are opened**, which destroys the defensibility of the award and may be unlawful in regulated regimes.
- **Vague scoring descriptors**, producing scores that evaluators cannot explain under challenge.
- **Untested price formulas**, where an outlier bid distorts relative scores; model the formula before publication.
- **Integrity breaches during a live procurement** — undeclared conflicts, hospitality from bidders, or side-channel communications.
- **Delegating evaluation to AI outputs** — summaries and anomaly flags may inform the panel, but unvalidated machine scoring is indefensible.
- **Overlooking sustainability and lock-in** — awarding on price while ignoring exit barriers, supply-chain conduct and environmental impact.

### How AI changes the procurement competency

AI and predictive tooling now touch every stage of the procurement lifecycle. Market-intelligence engines scan supplier landscapes, price indices and capacity signals faster than any category team; generative tools draft specifications, tender schedules and contract clauses in minutes; analytical models flag anomalous pricing and predict supplier delivery risk or financial distress from historical performance data. The competency shifts accordingly, from assembling procurement information to interrogating it: framing the make-or-buy question correctly, testing what the models have assumed, and defending the recommendation before governance boards, auditors and, in regulated contexts, formal challenge.

Sensible automation targets high-volume, rule-bound work in which errors are detectable and reversible: spend classification, supplier data cleansing, compliance checks against mandatory criteria, first drafts of standard tender documentation and first-pass screening of pre-qualification responses. Judgement-bound decisions must remain human: setting the strategy and risk allocation, fixing criteria and weightings before tenders are invited, scoring qualitative bid content, deciding award, and any decision to exclude a supplier. A leader who allows an algorithm to rank bids without independent scrutiny has not accelerated the evaluation; they have abdicated it. The validation disciplines are standard: sample-check automated classifications, require the rationale behind every AI-generated score or red flag, and record in the procurement file which outputs were machine-assisted and how each was verified.

### Model risk, data caveats and human accountability

Procurement data is a hostile environment for naive models. Supplier performance histories encode incumbency bias: the suppliers an organisation has used most generate the most data and therefore the most confident predictions, quietly disadvantaging new entrants, smaller firms and diverse suppliers — an outcome that may breach equal-treatment obligations in public procurement. Price benchmarks decay rapidly in volatile markets, so a model trained on pre-shock data will mis-advise on both budget and negotiating posture. Generative drafting tools can produce plausible but legally defective clauses, or import terms from jurisdictions that do not apply. Confidentiality risk is acute: bid material, supplier pricing and evaluation deliberations must never be entered into tools that retain or train on user prompts. The leader should demand the provenance, coverage and refresh cycle of the data behind any predictive score, and treat an unexplainable ranking as inadmissible in an evaluation that must withstand scrutiny.

Fairness and transparency obligations do not relax because software performed the analysis. If an award is challenged, "the model scored it" is not a defence; the named evaluators and the accountable procurement authority own the outcome and must be able to explain it. Governance therefore requires disclosed use of AI where policy or regulation demands it; audit trails linking every automated output to a human reviewer; bias testing before screening tools touch live supplier data; and clear escalation routes when a professional disagrees with a machine recommendation. Candidates should expect examination scenarios that probe exactly this boundary — situations in which the AI output is convenient, confident and wrong, and the professionally defensible action is to overrule it and record why.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** In procurement, AI may scan markets, draft documents, screen submissions and score risk — but the strategy, the criteria, the award and the ethics of the deal belong to an accountable human being. Every machine-assisted judgement in the sourcing file must be explainable, validated and owned by a competent professional, because it is the professional, not the model, who answers to the supplier, the auditor and the tribunal.

### Key points

- Procurement is the principal channel through which cost, schedule and risk flow on most projects; the strategy must trace to the project's objectives and risk appetite, not merely to price competition.
- The elements form a constraining sequence — make-or-buy, strategy, sourcing route and tender, evaluation, contract — and ethics and sustainability are properties of the whole chain, not a final step.
- Make-or-buy comparisons use risk-adjusted whole-life cost over a common period; a margin within estimating uncertainty is not decisive, and the recommendation then rests on qualitative factors.
- Evaluation criteria, weightings, scoring descriptors and the price formula are fixed and published before the invitation to tender and never altered after bids are opened.
- Contract type — fixed-price, cost-reimbursable, time-and-materials, target-cost — is fundamentally a decision about risk allocation and the behaviour the incentives will produce.
- AI legitimately accelerates rule-bound work (market analysis, drafting, first-pass screening), but judgement-bound decisions — strategy, criteria, scoring, award, exclusion — remain human and must be explainable, validated and owned.
- Beware incumbency bias in supplier data, decayed price benchmarks, defective generated clauses and confidentiality leakage; an unexplainable ranking is inadmissible.

### Practice questions

**1.** A published price formula awards 10 points to the lowest compliant whole-life price, with other bids scored pro rata. The lowest bid is £760,000 and Supplier A bids £825,000. What is Supplier A's price score (to two decimal places)?

- A. 9.21
- B. 10.86
- C. 9.14
- D. 8.00

**Correct answer: A.** The pro-rata formula is lowest price ÷ bid price × 10 = 760,000 ÷ 825,000 × 10 = 9.21; option B inverts the ratio and the others do not follow the published formula.

**2.** Midway through scoring, the evaluation panel concludes that the published 30% weighting for whole-life cost favours one bidder and proposes increasing the technical weighting. What is the correct course of action?

- A. Amend the weightings and notify all bidders of the change
- B. Complete the evaluation under the published weightings and record the concern for future procurement strategy
- C. Amend the weightings only if doing so does not change the ranking
- D. Ask the AI screening tool to re-score the bids under the proposed weightings

**Correct answer: B.** Altering published criteria or weightings after bids are opened destroys the defensibility of the award and may be unlawful in regulated regimes, regardless of notification, effect on ranking, or the tool used.

**3.** An award decision is formally challenged, and it emerges that a predictive tool ranked the bids and the panel adopted the ranking without independent scoring. Which statement best reflects the Institute's doctrine?

- A. The award stands because the tool applied the published weightings consistently
- B. The award is defensible if the tool's developer certifies the model's accuracy
- C. The evaluation is indefensible because no accountable human validated, explained and owned the scores
- D. The evaluation is defensible provided the use of AI was disclosed to bidders

**Correct answer: C.** Under "AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable", disclosure, consistency and third-party assurance cannot substitute for named evaluators who validate and own every score — "the model scored it" is not a defence.

## 10. Contracts and Commercial Delivery

This chapter follows the commercial arc of a project from contract selection through administration, variation, claim and dispute to close-out. Its four disciplines form one causal chain: the contract type chosen at award determines what must be administered; the quality of administration and records determines whether variations and claims can be managed on evidence rather than assertion; and the discipline of all three determines whether commercial close-out is a confirmation or a negotiation.

### The contract as the instrument of delivery

A **contract** is a legally binding agreement under which one party (the **supplier**, contractor or service provider) undertakes to deliver defined scope, goods or services to another (the **client**, employer or buyer) in exchange for consideration, normally payment. In project delivery the contract is far more than a legal formality: it is the instrument that converts intent into enforceable obligation. It fixes what will be delivered, by when, to what standard, for how much and under which **payment mechanism**, and — critically — it allocates commercial **risk** between the parties and prescribes the remedies available when performance departs from promise. **Commercial delivery**, as this chapter uses the term, is the discipline of selecting, administering and closing contracts so that the commercial arrangement reinforces the project's delivery objectives rather than working against them. The delivery leader is rarely the drafting lawyer, but is accountable for ensuring that the contract can actually be delivered and that it is managed with discipline once signed.

### Contract types, payment mechanisms and risk allocation

Every contract type sits on a spectrum defined by its payment mechanism — the rule that converts work performed into entitlement to payment. At one pole sit **price-based** arrangements (lump-sum or fixed-price contracts, including turnkey and EPC forms), in which the supplier is paid a predetermined price and therefore carries cost, quantity and productivity risk. At the other sit **cost-based** arrangements (cost-reimbursable and time-and-materials contracts), in which the client pays actual cost plus a fee and therefore retains most delivery risk. Between them lie hybrids: **unit-rate and remeasurement** contracts, where rates are fixed but quantities are measured as built; **target-cost** contracts with pain/gain share, which split overruns and underruns to a formula; and **framework agreements** with call-off contracts for repeated or evolving demand. Supporting mechanisms — milestone and stage payments, retention, performance bonds, liquidated damages, key performance indicators and service credits — tune cash flow and incentive within any of these forms.

The governing principle of risk allocation is that risk should sit with the party best able to foresee, control and price it. The single most important variable in applying that principle is **scope maturity**: a stable, well-specified scope can responsibly be priced fixed; an immature or evolving scope priced fixed merely purchases an inflated risk premium and a pipeline of claims. This is why adaptive and agile delivery tends towards capacity-based or target-cost commercial models, while well-defined construction or supply scope tends towards fixed-price forms. Selecting the mechanism that matches scope maturity is the first examinable competence of this chapter.

### Selecting the commercial model: a five-step method

Selection proceeds in five steps. First, assess scope maturity honestly: determine how completely the requirement can be specified today and how stable it will remain through delivery. Second, identify the principal delivery risks and allocate each to the party best placed to manage it — risk transferred to a supplier who cannot control it returns as price premium, claims or failure. Third, shortlist payment mechanisms against that allocation: firm fixed price where scope is mature and stable; target cost with a pain/gain share where scope is broadly defined but likely to evolve; cost-reimbursable or time-and-materials where scope is immature, always paired with strong governance, capped commitments and break points; and unit or output rates where volumes rather than definition are uncertain. Fourth, stress-test the shortlist against scenarios — overrun, major change, supplier distress — and confirm that the supplier's commercial incentive points in the same direction as the client's objectives. Fifth, record the selection and rationale in the commercial strategy and obtain governance approval before tendering.

### Governing frameworks and standard forms

The chapter draws on established professional and contractual reference points, described at awareness level and always in the Institute's own words. Recognised project management bodies of knowledge treat procurement and contract management as an integral knowledge area, and ISO 21502 includes the management of suppliers within its project management practices. In engineering and construction, **standard forms of contract** — notably the FIDIC suite and the NEC family, alongside national forms — codify tested allocations of risk; the NEC forms are notable for embedding proactive administration through early-warning and compensation-event mechanisms. ISO 44001 addresses collaborative business relationships, relevant to alliancing and partnering models. Public-sector procurement regimes add rules on competition, transparency and remedies. Candidates are examined on principles and their application, not on the recall of clause numbers.

### Administering the contract during delivery

**Contract administration** is the systematic post-award management of both parties' obligations. It begins with contract start-up: build an obligations and deliverables register from the executed contract, baseline milestones and service levels, and agree communication protocols, notice periods and delegated authorities. Thereafter run a disciplined monthly cycle: verify deliverables against acceptance criteria before certifying milestones; apply the payment mechanism exactly as drafted, including retention and service-credit provisions; monitor service levels; and maintain contemporaneous records of instructions, notices and early warnings.

**Supplier performance management** builds on this foundation, measuring delivery against agreed obligations, milestones and service levels, and acting on shortfall through the contract's remedies and through structured performance reviews. The quality of records kept here is not clerical housekeeping: it decides whether later variations, claims and the final account are argued on evidence or on assertion. AI-enabled tools can propose obligation extractions, flag anomalies in cost submissions and draft performance summaries, but every such output must be validated and owned by the contract administrator.

### Worked example: settling a target-cost contract

A data-platform build is let as a target-cost contract with an original target cost of £4,000,000, a fixed fee of £400,000 (10 %), a 50/50 pain/gain share on any difference between adjusted target and actual allowable cost, and supplier pain capped at the fee.

During delivery, two compensation events are approved through the contractual change mechanism: £250,000 for a regulatory data-residency change and £150,000 for client-instructed additional interfaces.

Adjusted target cost = £4,000,000 + £250,000 + £150,000 = **£4,400,000**

At completion, audited allowable actual cost is £4,720,000.

Overrun = £4,720,000 − £4,400,000 = **£320,000**

Supplier painshare = 50 % × £320,000 = **£160,000** (well within the £400,000 cap, so no adjustment).

Final price payable = actual cost + fee − painshare = £4,720,000 + £400,000 − £160,000 = **£4,960,000**

The supplier's realised margin is £400,000 − £160,000 = £240,000, or £240,000 ÷ £4,720,000 = **5.1 %** of actual cost against the 10 % planned — a genuine but survivable commercial consequence. Contrast the alternatives: under a firm price of £4,400,000 plus fee, the supplier would have absorbed the full £320,000; under pure cost-reimbursement the client would have paid £5,120,000 and the supplier would have felt no consequence at all. The pain/gain mechanism shares the overrun £160,000/£160,000 — precisely the allocation intended for partially mature scope.

### Managing variations, claims and disputes

Because no contract survives delivery unchanged, every well-drafted agreement contains a **variation** (change) mechanism — the authorised route for instructing, pricing and incorporating change to scope, and the commercial counterpart of the change control discipline established in this Body of Knowledge's governance and planning chapters. Route every change through that single mechanism: capture the proposed variation in writing; confirm entitlement against the relevant clause; quantify cost and time effects before instruction wherever practicable; approve at the correct delegated authority; and update the contract baseline and registers immediately.

A **claim** is an assertion by one party of entitlement to additional time or money arising from events for which the other party bears contractual risk; a **dispute** is a claim or difference that the parties' ordinary processes have failed to resolve. For claims, observe notice periods strictly, insist on contemporaneous records, and evaluate merit dispassionately — entitlement first, quantum second. Where agreement fails, resolution routes form an escalating ladder — commercial negotiation, senior-executive escalation, mediation, adjudication or dispute boards, and only then arbitration or litigation — rising in cost, formality and relational damage. Select the route by weighing value at stake, speed, cost, confidentiality and the importance of preserving the relationship; most project disputes settle fastest and cheapest at the lowest rung both parties take seriously.

### Commercial close-out

**Commercial close-out** completes the cycle. Verify that every obligation in the register is discharged or formally waived; agree the final account, including all variations, claims and fluctuations; release retentions, bonds and guarantees on the contractual dates; document surviving obligations such as warranties, latent-defect liabilities and data-handling duties; record supplier performance for future sourcing decisions; and capture commercial lessons into organisational knowledge before the team disperses. Done on disciplined records, close-out confirms positions already evidenced; done late or on weak records, it becomes a negotiation.

### AI in contracts and commercial delivery

AI and predictive tooling now reach every stage of this chapter's arc. At formation, contract-analytics tools can compare a draft against a standard-form library, flag unusual clauses and departures from precedent, and simulate how alternative payment mechanisms would distribute cost outcomes under different scope-maturity scenarios — sharpening, though never making, the risk-allocation decision. During administration, analytics platforms monitor supplier performance continuously — milestone attainment, service-level trends, defect and invoice patterns — and surface early-warning signals of deterioration well before a periodic review would. In variations and claims, document-intelligence tools assemble chronologies from contemporaneous records, cross-reference correspondence against notice provisions, and estimate the likely cost and schedule effect of a proposed change; some offer predictive ranges for claim outcomes based on historical settlements. At close-out, extraction tools accelerate reconciliation of the final account against instructed variations. What changes is the speed and reach of commercial intelligence; what does not change is the locus of judgement, because the contract binds named parties and named certifiers, not models.

The delivery leader draws the automation boundary deliberately. Suitable for automation, with sampling checks: first-pass clause comparison and deviation flagging; obligation, milestone and service-level tracking; arithmetic verification of payment applications; assembly of variation and claims chronologies; close-out checklist reconciliation. Never delegable: selection of contract type and payment mechanism against actual scope maturity; whether a flagged clause matters commercially in this deal; whether a deteriorating supplier trend warrants relational intervention or contractual remedy; entitlement and quantum on any claim; the choice of dispute route; and certification of payment or of the final account. The leader validates an AI output as they would a junior analyst's work: what evidence supports this, what was assumed, and what would make it wrong?

Three caveats govern use of these tools. First, language models can hallucinate — inventing clause numbers, misquoting terms, or offering plausible but incorrect readings of entitlement — so every citation must be verified against the executed document itself. Second, predictive models trained on historical settlements embed the biases and jurisdictions of that history, and supplier-performance analytics inherit the quality of the site records feeding them: incomplete diaries produce confident but misleading signals. Third, contract terms, claims positions and settlement strategy are commercially sensitive and may be legally privileged — confirm a tool's data-handling and retention terms before any contract document is processed, and recognise that AI-generated analysis relied upon in a dispute may become disclosable and must therefore be explainable.

Contractual powers — instructing a variation, serving or responding to a notice, certifying payment, agreeing a final account — vest in named individuals, and no AI output transfers or dilutes them. An AI-drafted notice served out of time is still out of time; a certificate issued on unvalidated analytics is still the certifier's certificate. The examination expects candidates to demonstrate not only commercial competence but the governance of these tools: every AI output that informs a commercial position must be explainable, validated against the contract and the records, and owned by a competent, accountable professional.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** A model may draft the comparison, flag the slipping service level, assemble the claims file and predict the settlement range — but the contract knows nothing of models. Only a competent, named professional allocates risk, certifies payment, serves notice, agrees the variation and settles the account — and that professional answers for every AI-informed decision as fully as for their own analysis.

### Common pitfalls

- Transferring risk the supplier cannot price or control, then paying for it anyway through contingency, claims or insolvency.
- Selecting a fixed price for immature scope and converting delivery into a variations negotiation from month one.
- Certifying milestones on effort or optimism rather than evidenced acceptance criteria.
- Issuing instructions informally, then discovering they constitute uncontrolled variations with cost and time entitlement.
- Missing contractual notice periods and forfeiting otherwise valid entitlements.
- Allowing the obligations register and contract baseline to drift from the change log.
- Accepting AI-generated contract summaries or claim valuations without human validation of clause references and arithmetic.
- Escalating prematurely to formal proceedings and destroying a relationship the programme still depends upon.
- Treating close-out as administrative housekeeping and releasing securities before obligations are verifiably discharged.

### Key points

- The contract converts intent into enforceable obligation: it fixes scope, time, standard, price and payment mechanism, and allocates commercial risk between the parties.
- Payment mechanisms sit on a spectrum from price-based (supplier carries cost risk) through unit-rate, target-cost and framework hybrids to cost-based (client retains risk); risk belongs with the party best able to foresee, control and price it.
- Scope maturity is the decisive selection variable: mature, stable scope suits fixed price; evolving scope suits target-cost or capacity-based models; fixed-pricing immature scope buys a risk premium and a pipeline of claims.
- Disciplined administration — an obligations register, evidenced certification and contemporaneous records — is what allows variations and claims to be resolved on evidence rather than assertion.
- Route all change through the single contractual variation mechanism; on claims, observe notice periods strictly and establish entitlement before quantum; escalate disputes deliberately up the ladder from negotiation to litigation.
- Target-cost pain/gain mechanisms share overrun and underrun to a formula, keeping both parties' incentives aligned where scope is partially mature.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: first-pass extraction, tracking and chronology assembly may be automated with sampling checks, but risk allocation, entitlement, dispute-route choice and every act of certification remain with a named, accountable human.

### Practice questions

**1.** A client must contract for a system integration whose requirement is broadly defined but certain to evolve as regulation develops. Which commercial model best matches the principle that risk should sit with the party best able to control it?

- A. Firm fixed price with liquidated damages
- B. Target cost with a 50/50 pain/gain share
- C. Uncapped cost-reimbursable with no break points
- D. Remeasurement against a fixed bill of quantities

**Correct answer: B.** Target cost with pain/gain shares the cost consequences of partially mature, evolving scope, whereas A transfers unpriceable risk, C leaves the supplier with no consequence, and D addresses quantity uncertainty rather than definitional uncertainty.

**2.** A target-cost contract has an original target of £2,000,000, a fixed fee of £200,000, a 50/50 pain/gain share and one approved compensation event of £100,000. Audited allowable actual cost is £2,300,000. What is the final price payable?

- A. £2,300,000
- B. £2,400,000
- C. £2,450,000
- D. £2,500,000

**Correct answer: B.** Adjusted target is £2,100,000, so the £200,000 overrun yields a £100,000 painshare, and the final price is £2,300,000 + £200,000 − £100,000 = £2,400,000; the distractors omit the painshare, the fee, or misapply the share percentage.

**3.** Under the Institute's AI doctrine, which of the following may never be delegated to an AI tool, however capable?

- A. First-pass comparison of a draft against a standard-form library
- B. Assembly of a claims chronology from contemporaneous records
- C. Certification of a payment application
- D. Arithmetic verification of a cost submission

**Correct answer: C.** Certification is a contractual power vested in a named individual and cannot be transferred to a model, whereas A, B and D are first-pass or mechanical tasks suitable for automation with human sampling checks and ownership of the output.

## 11. Integrated Cost, Schedule and Risk Management

Cost, schedule and risk are commonly taught as three disciplines; on a real project they are one system. The baseline is a prediction of cost and time; risk is the reason that prediction may prove wrong; earned value management measures how the prediction is faring; and integrated forecasting reconciles all three into a defensible estimate of where the project will finish. This chapter treats them as that single system. It fixes the vocabulary, sets out the integrated method step by step, works a full example, and examines how AI-enabled tooling changes — and does not change — the delivery leader's accountabilities.

### Performance measurement baselines and the earned value vocabulary

A **baseline** is the approved, version-controlled plan against which performance is measured; it changes only through formal change control. The **performance measurement baseline (PMB)** is the time-phased budget for the authorised scope, built up through the work breakdown structure into **control accounts** — the management points at which scope, budget, actual cost and schedule integrate. The total of the PMB is the **Budget at Completion (BAC)**.

Earned value management (EVM) rests on three measures, all expressed in currency at a common data date: **Planned Value (PV)**, the budget for the work scheduled to date; **Earned Value (EV)**, the budget for the work actually performed; and **Actual Cost (AC)**, what that performed work actually cost. From these follow the variances (**CV = EV − AC**; **SV = EV − PV**), the performance indices (**CPI = EV ÷ AC**; **SPI = EV ÷ PV**), and the forecasting measures — **Estimate at Completion (EAC)**, **Estimate to Complete (ETC = EAC − AC)**, **Variance at Completion (VAC = BAC − EAC)** and the **To-Complete Performance Index (TCPI)**, the cost efficiency required on remaining work to meet a stated target. The foundational point is that EVM converts physical progress into money, so that scope, schedule and cost become directly comparable.

### The vocabulary of risk

A **risk** is an uncertain event or condition that, if it occurs, has a positive or negative effect on objectives; ISO 31000 frames this generally as the effect of uncertainty on objectives. Negative risks are **threats**; positive risks are **opportunities**, and a competent delivery leader manages both. Individual risks are distinct from **overall project risk**, the combined uncertainty of the whole endeavour. The **risk register** records each identified risk with its cause, probability, impact, priority, a single named **risk owner** and an agreed response — avoid, transfer, mitigate (reduce) or accept for threats; exploit, share, enhance or accept for opportunities. Risk remaining after response is **residual risk**; a response that creates a new risk creates a **secondary risk**.

Two reserves fund uncertainty, and the distinction is examinable. **Contingency reserve** funds identified residual risk: it sits within the project budget, is drawn down under the project's own authority strictly against realised risks, and every drawdown is logged. **Management reserve** covers genuinely unidentified work: it sits outside the PMB, is controlled by the sponsor or governance tier, and is never used to disguise overspend. Frameworks differ on whether contingency sits inside or alongside the PMB; the examinable principle is that reserves are held distinct from work-package budgets, controlled at the right level of authority, and traceable in drawdown.

### From deterministic estimates to stated confidence

A single-point estimate of cost or duration is **deterministic**: it carries no statement of how likely it is to be achieved. Quantitative cost risk analysis (QCRA) and quantitative schedule risk analysis (QSRA) replace single points with distributions — typically three-point estimates on activities and cost elements, plus discrete risk events from the register — and combine them, most commonly by Monte Carlo simulation, into a probability distribution of project outcomes. The outputs are cumulative probability curves (S-curves), confidence levels such as **P50** and **P80** (the value with a 50% or 80% probability of not being exceeded), and sensitivity or tornado analyses showing which risks drive the spread. Contingency is then set as the difference between the funded confidence level and the base estimate — a governance decision expressed in probabilistic language, not a percentage plucked from habit.

### Governing frameworks and standards

This chapter aligns with the internationally recognised bodies of guidance: the PMBOK Guide and PMI's practice standards for earned value management and risk management; ISO 21502 on project management, ISO 21508 on earned value management and ISO 31000 on risk management; the EIA-748 EVM system guidelines used in government-regulated environments; AACE International's Total Cost Management Framework and recommended practices on risk analysis and contingency determination; and the APM Body of Knowledge. Candidates are examined on the concepts and calculations as defined here, not on the clause structure of any single standard.

### The integrated method, step by step

1. **Establish the PMB.** Decompose the scope into a work breakdown structure, assign each work package a budget and an earned value measurement technique, and time-phase the budget to form the planned value curve. Work-package budgets exclude reserves.
2. **Record performance data each period.** At control-account level, capture PV, EV and AC as at a single data date.
3. **Calculate and interpret the metrics.** Compute CV, SV, CPI and SPI, and investigate root causes before reporting: a number without an explanation is data, not analysis.
4. **Maintain the risk register.** Identify risks systematically, assess probability and impact, prioritise, assign a single named owner to each risk, and agree responses. Fund agreed responses within the baseline through change control, and quantify the residual exposure that remains.
5. **Run quantitative risk analysis on the remaining work.** Replace single-point estimates of remaining cost and duration with three-point ranges, add risk events from the register, model correlation between related items, and simulate to produce probability distributions for cost and completion date.
6. **Set contingency at a stated confidence level.** Contingency equals the value at the percentile mandated by governance minus the deterministic estimate. Draw it down only against realised risks, with an auditable log.
7. **Forecast and report on an integrated basis.** Reconcile the earned-value EAC with the QRA percentiles, and present variances, forecast, contingency status and top risks as one coherent picture — because a healthy CPI alongside a deteriorating risk register is not a healthy project.

The loop then closes: execution generates the next period's EV, AC and risk data, and the analysis repeats against a baseline that changes only through change control.

### Worked example: a data platform programme at month six

A twelve-month data platform programme has a budget at completion (BAC) of £4,800,000, time-phased evenly for simplicity. At the month-six data date:

- PV = £2,400,000 (work scheduled to date)
- EV = £2,100,000 (budgeted value of work actually performed)
- AC = £2,300,000 (actual cost incurred)

**Variances and indices.**

- CV = EV − AC = £2,100,000 − £2,300,000 = **−£200,000** (over cost)
- SV = EV − PV = £2,100,000 − £2,400,000 = **−£300,000** (behind schedule)
- CPI = 2,100,000 ÷ 2,300,000 = **0.913** (£0.91 earned per £1 spent)
- SPI = 2,100,000 ÷ 2,400,000 = **0.875** (progressing at 87.5% of the planned rate)

**Forecasts.** Assuming current cost efficiency persists:

- EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 4,800,000 ÷ 0.913 = **£5,257,000** (rounded)
- ETC = EAC − AC = 5,257,000 − 2,300,000 = **£2,957,000**
- VAC = BAC − EAC = **−£457,000**

If schedule pressure is also expected to drive cost (for example, through extended time-related overheads), the combined-index formula gives EAC = AC + (BAC − EV) ÷ (CPI × SPI) = 2,300,000 + 2,700,000 ÷ 0.799 = **£5,679,000**. The credible forecast range is therefore roughly £5.26m to £5.68m.

**Risk and contingency.** The register's top residual risk — migration rework, probability 40%, cost impact £350,000 — carries an expected monetary value of 0.40 × £350,000 = £140,000. A Monte Carlo simulation of the remaining work, combining three-point estimates with the register's risk events, yields a cost-to-complete distribution with P50 = £3,120,000 and P80 = £3,410,000 against the deterministic ETC of £2,957,000. With governance requiring 80% confidence, cost contingency is set at 3,410,000 − 2,957,000 = **£453,000**, giving a funded completion position of 2,300,000 + 3,410,000 = £5,710,000 — consistent with the upper end of the EVM forecast range. The schedule simulation shows a P80 completion at month fourteen, so two months of schedule contingency are proposed. The practitioner reports the variances with causes, recommends the P80 funding position to the board, and initiates change control rather than quietly absorbing the overrun.

### Common pitfalls

- **Confusing spend with progress.** AC or elapsed time is not EV; earned value must derive from the measurement technique assigned to each work package.
- **Trusting SPI late in the project.** SPI converges on 1.0 as work completes, however late; corroborate with critical path analysis of the schedule.
- **Anchoring the forecast to the budget.** Persistent CPI below 1.0 rarely self-corrects; "it will come back" is hope, not forecasting.
- **Unowned or uncosted risks.** A register without named owners, response actions and dates is a list of worries, not a management instrument.
- **Ignoring correlation in QRA.** Independent distributions produce unrealistically narrow ranges and understate the contingency required.
- **Treating contingency as a scope fund.** Drawdown must trace to realised risks, never to unapproved scope growth.
- **Rebaselining to erase variance.** Baselines change only through change control, with history preserved.
- **Accepting AI outputs unvalidated.** Machine-generated forecasts and risk scores enter reports only after a competent human has tested, understood and taken ownership of them.

### How AI changes integrated performance management

Integrated cost, schedule and risk management is the most data-rich competency in the delivery leader's portfolio, and therefore the one most visibly reshaped by predictive tooling. Machine-learning models now generate estimates at completion by pattern-matching a project's performance trajectory against historical portfolios, rather than relying solely on index-based formulae. Anomaly detection flags implausible progress claims, out-of-sequence schedule logic and misbooked actual costs before period close. Natural-language processing mines risk registers, lessons-learned repositories, contracts and site reports to propose candidate risks the team has not yet articulated. Simulation engines run quantitative risk analysis continuously, so confidence levels and contingency adequacy can be re-examined at every reporting cycle instead of only at stage gates. The net effect is a shift from periodic, backward-looking variance reporting towards continuous, forward-looking inference — provided the leader governs what the machinery produces.

### What the leader automates — and what the leader validates

The working rule is that computation is automated and judgement is validated. A competent leader will routinely automate data assembly and cleansing, the arithmetic of earned value, threshold-based variance flagging, first-pass risk identification prompts, Monte Carlo execution and the drafting of performance narratives. What the leader must personally validate is different in kind: the earning rules and progress claims behind every earned value figure; the completeness, ownership and response quality of the risk register; the probability distributions and correlation assumptions inside any quantitative model; and every AI-generated EAC before it reaches a governance forum. The discipline the examination expects is reproducibility — the candidate who cannot hand-calculate a CPI, or explain why a P80 sits where it does, cannot meaningfully validate a model that claims to have done it for them.

AI forecasting also carries specific, examinable failure modes. Models trained on historical projects inherit the optimism bias, scope patterns and delivery context of that history; applied to a materially different project, they can be confidently wrong. Earned value inputs are only as reliable as the progress measurement behind them, and automation amplifies poor data faster than it amplifies good data. Simulation tools that default to independence between risks understate the spread of outcomes, producing contingency figures that look precise and are systematically thin. Machine-generated forecasts quoted to spurious precision imply a confidence the underlying data cannot support. Finally, models drift as the project or portfolio changes character, so the leader must insist on periodic revalidation against realised outcomes.

### Human accountability

Institute doctrine is explicit. Every AI output that enters a performance measurement baseline, a forecast or a report to a sponsor must have a named, competent human owner who can explain and defend it. Setting contingency at a stated confidence level is a management decision informed by analysis, never a number transcribed from a model. Drawdown of contingency, and any change to the baseline, passes through formal change control regardless of what a predictive tool recommends. Assurance functions should expect an audit trail showing what the model proposed, what the professional accepted, amended or rejected, and why. Any output that cannot be explained — its inputs, assumptions and sensitivity — must not be used to set a baseline, a forecast or a contingency figure. Candidates are examined on precisely this posture: interpreting AI-assisted outputs, challenging their assumptions, and owning the resulting decision.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** A model may propose an estimate at completion, a risk mined from project records, or a P80 contingency drawn from ten thousand simulations. The delivery leader verifies and decides: checking the data behind the earned value, interrogating the distributions and correlations, deciding the confidence level at which contingency is set, and signing the forecast that goes to the sponsor. No algorithm holds the baseline; the accountable professional does.

### Key points

- Cost, schedule and risk form one system: the baseline predicts, risk explains why the prediction may fail, EVM measures, and integrated forecasting reconciles all three.
- EVM converts physical progress into money: CV = EV − AC, SV = EV − PV, CPI = EV ÷ AC, SPI = EV ÷ PV, and EAC = BAC ÷ CPI when current cost efficiency is expected to persist.
- Contingency reserve funds identified residual risk under project authority; management reserve funds the unidentified and sits with the sponsor, outside the PMB.
- Quantitative risk analysis replaces deterministic estimates with distributions; contingency is set at a governance-mandated confidence level (for example P80) minus the base estimate.
- SPI loses meaning late in a project and must be corroborated with critical path analysis; persistent CPI shortfalls rarely self-correct.
- Correlation matters in simulation: assuming independence between risks narrows the spread and understates contingency.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: every machine-generated forecast, risk or contingency figure must be explainable, validated against project evidence and owned by a competent human before it informs a decision.

### Practice questions

**1.** At its data date a project reports BAC £2,000,000, PV £900,000, EV £800,000 and AC £1,000,000. Assuming current cost efficiency persists, what is the estimate at completion?

A. £2,000,000  B. £2,222,000  C. £2,250,000  D. £2,500,000

**Answer: D.** CPI = 800,000 ÷ 1,000,000 = 0.80, so EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 2,000,000 ÷ 0.80 = £2,500,000; option C wrongly divides by SPI, and option A anchors to the budget.

**2.** A governance board mandates that cost contingency be funded at P80. This means the base estimate plus contingency gives a total cost with:

A. an 80% probability of not being exceeded  B. an 80% probability of being exceeded  C. a value equal to 80% of the worst simulated outcome  D. a guaranteed 20% margin above the P50

**Answer: A.** P80 is the value on the cumulative distribution with an 80% probability that the actual outcome will not exceed it; the other options misread the percentile.

**3.** An AI tool produces an EAC materially lower than the index-based forecast, but cannot show which inputs or assumptions drove its prediction. The delivery leader should:

A. adopt the AI forecast, since it draws on more historical data  B. average the two forecasts to balance the evidence  C. report the index-based EAC and exclude the AI figure until it can be explained and validated  D. report both figures and let the sponsor choose

**Answer: C.** Under Institute doctrine an output that cannot be explained and validated must not inform a forecast — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

## 12. Predictive Delivery

### The nature of the predictive approach

A predictive delivery approach — sometimes called plan-driven or, informally, waterfall — is one in which the scope, schedule and cost of a project are defined and approved substantially before execution begins, and in which delivery performance is thereafter measured against those approved definitions. The defining commitment is not sequence but *predictability*: the sponsoring organisation invests early effort in specification, estimation and design precisely because it expects the requirement to remain stable enough for that early plan to stay meaningful.

Three terms anchor everything that follows. A **baseline** is the approved, version-controlled statement of scope, schedule or cost against which performance is measured; it changes only through formal change control, never by silent revision. A **stage** (or phase) is a discrete, bounded portion of the project, planned in detail one stage at a time within an outline whole-project plan. A **stage gate** is a formal decision point at the boundary between stages at which a governance body reviews evidence of performance and viability before authorising further work. Predictive delivery is therefore best understood as a control system: baselines supply the reference, stages bound the commitment, and gates supply the decision mechanism.

### Frameworks and standards context

The candidate should be able to locate these ideas within the recognised professional canon. The *PMBOK® Guide* describes the predictive life cycle and its planning, executing and monitoring-and-controlling work, including the scheduling techniques examined in this chapter. PRINCE2® contributes the most fully developed treatment of stage-based governance: its principles of *manage by stages* and *manage by exception* formalise stage-limited authorisation and tolerances — permitted deviations from baseline within which the project manager may act without escalation. ISO 21502 provides international-standard guidance on governance and phase-based direction, and the APM Body of Knowledge treats deployment approaches, scheduling and gated life cycles in comparable terms. The Institute's examination does not test allegiance to any single framework; it tests the candidate's command of the common conceptual machinery these frameworks share.

### Suitability: when prediction is a rational bet

Because the predictive approach spends heavily before it delivers, its suitability turns on whether early definition is *achievable* and *valuable*. It is achievable where requirements are stable and specifiable, the solution technology is well understood, and comparable work provides a reliable estimating basis. It is valuable where the cost of late change is high (physical construction, embedded systems), where regulation or contract demands demonstrable, documented control (safety cases, fixed-price procurement, licensed industries), or where multiple parties must coordinate against a common integrated plan. Conversely, where requirements are emergent, feedback is cheap and the product can be delivered incrementally, an adaptive or hybrid approach will usually dominate; Chapter 13 develops that comparison.

In applied form, the screen tests three criteria: requirement stability (can scope be specified substantially in full before build?), risk profile (is rework intolerably expensive or unsafe?) and regulatory context (must evidence of a complete, approved design exist before execution?). The judgement and its rationale are recorded and revisited at every gate. Suitability assessment is thus a risk judgement, not a preference: the candidate is examined on weighing these factors against one another for a given scenario.

### The predictive method, step by step

Applied predictive delivery is a repeatable sequence, and the examination expects the candidate to execute every step, not merely name them.

1. **Screen for suitability**, as above, and record the rationale.
2. **Structure the work.** Decompose scope into a work breakdown structure, then group the work into management stages, each ending in a gate with defined entry evidence and exit criteria.
3. **Build the network.** Identify precedence relationships, estimate durations, and run the forward pass (earliest start ES, earliest finish EF) and backward pass (latest start LS, latest finish LF). The critical path is the longest path; total float is LS − ES.
4. **Baseline.** Freeze scope, schedule and cost as the approved baseline, version-controlled and altered only through formal change control.
5. **Measure, forecast, decide.** Each period, compare performance against baseline, forecast completion, and bring the evidence to the gate for a proceed, amend or stop decision.

### The scheduling engine: critical path and critical chain

Within a stage, the schedule baseline rests on network logic. A **network diagram** (in modern practice, activity-on-node precedence form) represents activities and their dependencies — finish-to-start and its variants, with leads and lags. The **forward pass** yields each activity's earliest dates and hence the minimum project duration; the **backward pass** yields latest dates; the difference yields **float**, total float being the delay an activity can absorb without delaying project completion. The **critical path** is the longest path through the network — the sequence of activities with zero total float — and it, not the activity list, determines project duration.

Schedule compression operates on this logic: **crashing** adds resource to critical activities at increased cost, while **fast-tracking** overlaps activities at increased risk, and both can shift which path is critical. **Critical chain** scheduling, derived from the theory of constraints, extends the model by recognising resource contention as well as logical dependency: it schedules the resource-constrained longest chain, strips padding from individual estimates, and concentrates protection in a project buffer and feeding buffers whose consumption is then the primary progress signal.

### Worked example: compressing a regulated laboratory upgrade

**Setup.** A pharmaceutical company upgrades a quality-control laboratory. Requirements are stable and validation is mandatory, so a predictive approach is confirmed. Six activities, durations in weeks:

| Activity | Description | Predecessors | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | Requirements and regulatory scoping | — | 4 |
| B | Detailed design | A | 6 |
| C | Long-lead equipment procurement | A | 10 |
| D | Build and configuration | B | 8 |
| E | Installation and commissioning | C, D | 5 |
| F | Validation and handover | E | 4 |

**Passes.** The forward pass gives A: ES 0, EF 4; B: 4–10; C: 4–14; D: 10–18; E starts at the later of its predecessors' finishes, max(14, 18) = 18, so E: 18–23; F: 23–27. The backward pass from week 27 returns:

| Activity | ES | EF | LS | LF | Total float |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A | 0 | 4 | 0 | 4 | 0 |
| B | 4 | 10 | 4 | 10 | 0 |
| C | 4 | 14 | 8 | 18 | 4 |
| D | 10 | 18 | 10 | 18 | 0 |
| E | 18 | 23 | 18 | 23 | 0 |
| F | 23 | 27 | 23 | 27 | 0 |

Two paths exist: A–B–D–E–F = 4 + 6 + 8 + 5 + 4 = **27 weeks** (critical, zero float) and A–C–E–F = 4 + 10 + 5 + 4 = 23 weeks, giving C total float of 8 − 4 = **4 weeks**.

**Compression.** The sponsor requires 25 weeks to meet a site shutdown window. Crash options: B by 1 week at USD 14,000; D by up to 2 weeks at USD 9,000 per week; C by 2 weeks at USD 6,000 per week. C is not on the critical path — crashing it buys nothing. The cheapest critical-path option is D: crashing 8 → 6 weeks costs 2 × 9,000 = **USD 18,000** and yields 27 − 2 = **25 weeks**. Always recheck for path shift: the secondary path remains 23 weeks, still 2 weeks shorter, so the critical path is unchanged — but C's float falls from 4 to 2 weeks and must be re-flagged as a schedule risk.

**Interpretation.** The professional brings this to the design gate as an **amend** recommendation: rebaseline to 25 weeks, authorise USD 18,000 of crash cost, and record the reduced float as a new risk. The fast-track alternative — overlapping design and build — was rejected because rework risk in a validated environment outweighs the saving.

### Measurement, forecasting and the gate

Progress is measured objectively against baseline — through milestone completion, physical per cent complete and earned-value measures — and converted into forecasts of completion date and cost. At each stage boundary the delivery leader presents evidence, not assertion: cumulative progress against planned value, forecast completion date and cost, risk exposure, and the health of the business case. That evidence is what makes a gate a genuine decision rather than a ceremony: the board confronts a choice on demonstrated performance and forecast viability, not intention. Three outcomes are open — **proceed** (performance and business case sound), **amend** (rebaseline under change control, as in the worked example) or **stop** (the business case no longer holds — a legitimate control outcome, not a failure of nerve). A gate without a genuinely available stop option is ceremony, not control.

### AI in predictive delivery: automate the mechanics, validate the meaning

Predictive delivery is the most heavily instrumented of the delivery approaches, and therefore the one in which AI tooling has the most to offer — and the most to distort. Modern tools can draft a first-pass logic network from a scope statement, run schedule-quality diagnostics, recalculate the critical path and float continuously as progress is recorded, generate probabilistic completion forecasts, enumerate compression scenarios with their cost–time trade-offs, and assemble draft evidence packs for a stage gate. None of this changes the discipline itself; it changes its economics. Analyses that once justified a monthly reporting cycle can now run daily, and the delivery leader's competency shifts accordingly: from producing the analysis to interrogating it.

A defensible division of labour follows. **Automate the mechanics:** the forward and backward pass, float calculation, schedule-health checks (dangling activities, missing predecessors, excessive constraints and lags), variance arithmetic, first-draft reporting narrative, and the enumeration of crash and fast-track options. These are deterministic or near-deterministic tasks in which tooling is faster and less error-prone than manual work. **Validate the meaning:** whether AI-proposed logic reflects the real physical, contractual and resource dependencies of the project rather than statistical habit; whether a recommended compression option is actually executable given resource, quality and safety constraints; whether the assumptions beneath a probabilistic forecast still hold after change; and — above all — the suitability judgement itself, which no tool can own.

### Model risk and accountability at the gate

Forecasting models are only as trustworthy as the progress data feeding them. Subjective percentage-complete assessments, lagging actuals and optimistic status reporting propagate directly into machine-generated forecasts, now delivered with a confidence the underlying data does not warrant. Historical reference data may embed the optimism bias of past estimates, derive from projects that are not genuinely comparable, or rest on samples too small to support the probability statements displayed. Models also drift: a forecast calibrated against one baseline is not automatically valid after a rebaseline, and every approved change requires the leader to confirm that the model's reference frame has moved with the plan. Finally, an opaque output is inadmissible as gate evidence: a forecast whose data sources, assumptions and sensitivities cannot be explained to the project board cannot support a proceed, amend or stop decision, however accurate it may later prove to have been.

Stage gates exist precisely to concentrate accountability, and AI must not be allowed to dilute it. AI-generated analysis may inform a gate, but the decision is made and owned by named, competent humans — the sponsor and project board, advised by the delivery leader. The leader who presents machine-assisted evidence signs for it as their own: they must be able to state what the tool produced, what they changed or rejected, and why. That audit trail is good practice everywhere and an obligation in regulated environments, where the choice of a predictive approach is often driven by exactly this need for demonstrable control. The examination tests this division of responsibility directly: knowing which steps of critical-path analysis, forecasting and gate preparation may be automated is inseparable from knowing which judgements may never be delegated.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** In predictive delivery, AI may draft the network, recompute the path and forecast the finish; the delivery leader validates the logic against reality, owns the baseline and answers for the gate decision. A forecast nobody can explain is not evidence, and a proceed, amend or stop recommendation is never delegated to a model.

### Common pitfalls

- **Spending float as if it were free.** Total float belongs to the path, not the activity; consuming it early strips protection from every successor.
- **Crashing non-critical activities.** Money spent off the critical path shortens nothing.
- **Compressing without rechecking the network.** Near-critical paths can become critical; recompute both passes after every compression step.
- **Confusing crashing with fast-tracking.** Crashing trades cost for time; fast-tracking trades risk for time. State which trade is being made.
- **Baseline drift.** Absorbing scope change informally destroys the meaning of every variance reported afterwards.
- **Gate theatre.** Gates that always return "proceed" measure optimism, not performance.
- **Unvalidated AI schedules.** A machine-generated network with plausible but wrong logic fails exactly when it matters; the planner who signs it owns it.

### Key points

- Predictive delivery is a control system: baselines supply the reference, stages bound the commitment, and gates supply the decision mechanism. Baselines change only through formal change control.
- Suitability is a risk judgement, not a preference: stable, specifiable requirements, expensive or unsafe late change, and regulatory demand for demonstrable control all favour prediction.
- The critical path — the longest path through the network, with zero total float — determines project duration; total float (LS − ES) belongs to the path, not to individual activities.
- Crashing trades cost for time; fast-tracking trades risk for time. After every compression step, recompute both passes, because the critical path can shift.
- Critical chain scheduling adds resource dependency to logical dependency and uses buffer consumption as the primary progress signal.
- A gate is a genuine decision — proceed, amend or stop on evidence — and stop is a legitimate control outcome; a gate without a real stop option is ceremony.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: automate the deterministic mechanics, validate the meaning, and never delegate gate decisions, baseline ownership or the suitability judgement to a tool.

### Practice questions

**1.** In a precedence network, an activity has ES 6, EF 12, LS 9 and LF 15. What is its total float?

A. 0 weeks
B. 3 weeks
C. 6 weeks
D. 9 weeks

**Answer: B.** Total float = LS − ES (equivalently LF − EF) = 9 − 6 = 3 weeks.

**2.** To recover two weeks of schedule, a project manager overlaps detailed design with build rather than adding resource to build. Which trade-off has been chosen?

A. Cost for time, by crashing
B. Risk for time, by fast-tracking
C. Scope for time, by de-scoping
D. Float for time, by resource levelling

**Answer: B.** Fast-tracking overlaps logically sequential activities and accepts increased rework risk; adding resource at extra cost would have been crashing.

**3.** Under the Institute's doctrine on AI-enabled planning, which of the following may properly be delegated to a scheduling tool?

A. The proceed, amend or stop decision at a stage gate
B. Ownership of the schedule baseline after a rebaseline
C. Computation of the forward and backward passes and float
D. The judgement that the project remains suitable for a predictive approach

**Answer: C.** Deterministic mechanics such as network calculation may be automated; gate decisions, baseline ownership and suitability judgements must remain with accountable humans — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

## 13. Agile Delivery

This chapter defines agile delivery, situates it within professional standards, sets out the practice sequence from suitability decision to governance forecast, and examines how AI-enabled tooling changes — and does not change — the delivery leader's accountabilities. Its sections form one control loop: suitability analysis determines whether, and how far, agile applies; backlog and timebox practice generates increments and evidence; roles and cadence institutionalise inspection and adaptation; and metrics turn the accumulated evidence into forecasts a governance body can rely on.

### 13.1 Core definitions and the empirical basis

Agile delivery, as this Body of Knowledge uses the term, is a family of **iterative and incremental** approaches in which requirements and solutions evolve through short, structured feedback cycles between an empowered delivery team and engaged stakeholders. An **iteration** is a fixed-duration cycle of planning, building and reviewing work; an **increment** is the usable, verified addition to the product that each cycle produces.

Agile is not an absence of discipline but a different mechanism of control. Instead of measuring conformance to a detailed upfront plan, the leader inspects working output frequently and adapts scope, priority and process on the evidence. This is **empirical process control**: because complex work cannot be fully specified in advance, control is achieved through **transparency** (all parties see the same real state of the work), **inspection** (frequent examination of output and progress) and **adaptation** (prompt adjustment when deviation appears). The project remains fully accountable for its business case, expenditure and benefits; only the evidence of progress changes.

### 13.2 Frameworks and standards context

The intellectual foundation is the Manifesto for Agile Software Development (2001), which records four value preferences — favouring individuals and interactions, working product, customer collaboration and responsiveness to change, without dismissing the items each is weighed against — supported by twelve principles emphasising early and continuous delivery of value, sustainable pace, technical excellence and regular reflection. The most widely used instantiations are Scrum, defined in the Scrum Guide, and the Kanban method, with Lean thinking supplying the underlying flow and waste-elimination logic.

Contemporary standards, including ISO 21502 and current editions of the PMBOK Guide, recognise predictive, adaptive and hybrid life cycles as equally legitimate. Agile therefore sits within professional project management, not outside it, and the PCI PML-AI examination assesses the candidate's ability to select, apply and govern these approaches, not allegiance to any one of them.

### 13.3 The delivery-approach decision

The delivery approach is an explicit leadership decision, recorded in the delivery approach statement, discussed with the sponsor and revisited at stage gates rather than treated as a one-off choice. Three determinants dominate.

- **Requirement volatility.** Where requirements are stable, well understood and contractually fixed, predictive planning is efficient; where they are emergent, or expected to change materially as users learn from working increments, iterative discovery reduces the risk of building the wrong thing.
- **Stakeholder availability.** Agile presumes that a person with authority to make value and priority decisions — an empowered product owner — and users who can attend reviews are genuinely available at every iteration boundary. Without that commitment, feedback loops decay into assumption.
- **Organisational readiness.** Funding models, procurement arrangements, audit expectations and culture must tolerate incremental releases and evolving scope within a fixed envelope of time, cost and quality.

The work must also decompose into increments that are individually verifiable and, ideally, valuable. Where the determinants point in different directions, **hybrid delivery** — a predictive governance envelope of business case, stage gates and budget containing agile execution — is the professionally defensible answer.

### 13.4 Backlogs, refinement and prioritisation

The **product backlog** is the single, ordered list of everything currently known to be needed from the product — the sole source of work, never complete while the product lives. Candidate scope is captured as user-centred backlog items, each with acceptance criteria. **Backlog refinement** is the continuing activity of decomposing, clarifying, estimating and re-ordering items — progressive elaboration under another name. Refinement is rolling: items near the top of the backlog are small, estimated and ready for an iteration; items further down remain deliberately coarse.

Prioritisation is value-driven. Examinable techniques range from categorical schemes — MoSCoW's must have, should have, could have and won't have — to economic ordering by cost of delay relative to duration. Effort is estimated in relative units (story points) calibrated by the team itself; the consequences of that team-local calibration for measurement are developed in section 13.6.

AI tools can usefully draft acceptance criteria, cluster duplicate items and flag dependencies. Consistent with Institute doctrine — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable — the product owner validates and owns every ordering decision a tool suggests.

### 13.5 Timeboxes, roles and cadence

A **timebox** fixes duration and quality while scope flexes; its output is an increment meeting an agreed **definition of done**, applied uniformly so that "complete" means the same thing in every report to governance. Iteration length — commonly two weeks — is fixed and never extended: stretching the timebox to "finish" work destroys the empirical basis of every metric derived from it. Each iteration is planned against demonstrated velocity, not aspiration, and coordinated through a short daily event.

Two events close each timebox. In the **review**, stakeholders inspect the working increment against acceptance criteria and give evidence-based feedback on the product. In the **retrospective**, the team inspects its own way of working and commits to at least one specific, concrete improvement. Reviews adapt the *what*; retrospectives adapt the *how*.

Three role archetypes recur across frameworks: a product-ownership role accountable for value and priority; a self-organising, cross-functional team accountable for the increment; and a facilitation role accountable for process health and impediment removal. **Cadence** — the fixed rhythm of planning, daily coordination, review and retrospective — converts these events into a reliable governance interface: sponsors receive demonstrated working output on a predictable schedule rather than narrative status reports.

### 13.6 Metrics, forecasting and scale

Agile measurement is empirical, extrapolating from observed delivery data — never intuition alone.

- **Velocity** is the amount of work, in relative estimation units, that a given team completes per timebox. It is a planning signal for that team, never a productivity comparator between teams.
- **Throughput** counts completed items per unit of time; **cycle time** measures elapsed time from starting an item to finishing it; **work in progress (WIP)** links the two, since in stable flow cycle time rises with WIP.

Forecasts are presented as ranges with stated confidence — optimistic, central and pessimistic outcomes — never a single date, and burn-up charts make both progress and scope change visible to governance bodies. Forecasts are revalidated every iteration as new data arrives.

At scale, multiple teams working from one backlog demand explicit dependency management, consistent definitions of done and aggregated metrics. Published scaling frameworks exist, but practices should scale only as far as coordination genuinely requires.

### 13.7 Worked example — velocity-based release forecasting

**Setup.** A team delivering a customer-portal release works in two-week iterations. The refined backlog for the release totals **264 story points**. Velocity over the last six iterations was 28, 24, 31, 26, 30 and 29 points.

**Method and arithmetic.**

1. *Central velocity* — the six-iteration mean:
   28 + 24 + 31 + 26 + 30 + 29 = 168; 168 ÷ 6 = **28 points per iteration**.
2. *Optimistic velocity* — mean of the best three iterations:
   (31 + 30 + 29) ÷ 3 = 90 ÷ 3 = **30 points per iteration**.
3. *Pessimistic velocity* — mean of the worst three iterations:
   (24 + 26 + 28) ÷ 3 = 78 ÷ 3 = **26 points per iteration**.
4. *Iterations required* (always round up, since a partial iteration is a whole timebox):
   - Central: 264 ÷ 28 = 9.43 → **10 iterations**
   - Optimistic: 264 ÷ 30 = 8.80 → **9 iterations**
   - Pessimistic: 264 ÷ 26 = 10.15 → **11 iterations**
5. *Calendar conversion* at two weeks per iteration: 9 × 2 = 18 weeks; 10 × 2 = 20 weeks; 11 × 2 = 22 weeks.

**Result.** The release is forecast at **18–22 weeks, with a central estimate of 20 weeks**.

**Interpretation.** The delivery leader reports the range, not the single figure, and states the assumptions: stable team composition, no material backlog growth and a consistent definition of done. If refinement typically adds scope, the pessimistic case should be re-run with an explicit growth allowance. Governance receives a defensible confidence statement — "very likely within 22 weeks, plausibly by 18" — that can be revalidated every iteration as new velocity data arrives.

### 13.8 AI and the agile competency

Agile delivery generates precisely the kind of data on which predictive tooling thrives: a continuous stream of small, dated, comparable records — backlog items, state transitions, cycle times, throughput counts. AI-enabled tooling now sits across the whole iterative cycle. Backlog assistants cluster duplicate items, flag ambiguous acceptance criteria and draft first-pass user stories from stakeholder inputs; flow-analytics engines detect ageing work in progress, growing queues and unstable arrival rates before they surface in a review. Most significantly, probabilistic forecasting has displaced single-point extrapolation: simulation over historical throughput yields a distribution of completion dates ("85 per cent confidence of delivery by the June release") — a materially more honest instrument for governance communication than a burndown line drawn to a hoped-for endpoint.

**What the leader automates, and what the leader validates.** The dividing line follows judgement. Sensible candidates for automation include data capture from workflow tools, backlog hygiene (duplicates, stale items, missing fields), flow-metric dashboards, first drafts of iteration summaries and governance reports, and the mechanical generation of probabilistic forecasts. What must remain validated, and never delegated, includes: the suitability assessment, since requirement volatility, stakeholder availability and organisational readiness are contextual judgements no model observes directly; prioritisation, because value ordering is an accountability of the product owner however persuasive an AI-suggested ranking may appear; any commitment communicated to sponsors or steering groups; and the interpretation of metrics, where the leader must guard against Goodhart's law — velocity that is targeted, or optimised by a tool, ceases to measure anything. Retrospective analytics deserve particular caution: mining team discussions for sentiment or themes may be technically feasible, but deploying it without consent corrodes the psychological safety on which honest inspection depends.

**Model risk and data caveats.** Agile data flatters models and misleads the unwary. Story points are relative, team-local currency: any tool that compares velocity across teams, or treats points as absolute effort, produces analytically invalid output. Historical samples are small — a dozen iterations is thin ground for a confident distribution — and the history is perishable: changes in team composition, a tightened definition of done, scope rebaselining or process improvement all invalidate the baseline a model learned from. That tension is inherent, since improvement is the very purpose of empiricism; the leader must know when a forecast's foundations have moved. Casually maintained boards — batch closures, tickets left open across iterations, inconsistent workflow states — poison cycle-time and throughput data at source. A forecast is only as sound as the board discipline beneath it, and the leader is accountable for both.

**Human accountability before governance.** When an AI-derived forecast reaches a governance body, accountability is undiminished: the delivery leader must be able to explain what data the forecast rests on, what assumptions it embeds, why its confidence range is credible, and what would invalidate it. "The tool predicted it" is not a defence available to a certified professional. Examination candidates should expect to be tested on interpreting and challenging AI-generated agile metrics and forecasts — including recognising when an output is unreliable — not merely on operating the tooling.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** An AI forecast of a release date is a proposal, not a commitment; a suggested backlog ranking is an input, not a decision. The delivery leader validates the data and assumptions, the product owner owns the priority order, the team owns its improvement actions — and the human professional owns every number presented to governance.

### 13.9 Common pitfalls

- **Treating velocity as a productivity target.** Velocity is a planning input local to one team; setting targets or comparing teams inflates estimates and corrupts the forecast.
- **Extending the timebox** to "finish" work, which destroys the empirical basis of every metric.
- **Forecasting from too few data points**, or from iterations affected by holidays and team changes without adjustment.
- **Presenting a single-date forecast** to governance instead of a range with stated assumptions.
- **A weak definition of done**, so increments carry hidden rework and reported progress overstates reality.
- **A proxy product owner** without authority, delaying decisions and hollowing out reviews.
- **Skipping retrospectives under schedule pressure**, forfeiting the improvement loop on which agile depends.
- **Accepting AI-generated backlogs or forecasts unexamined.** Every AI output must be explainable, validated and owned by an accountable professional before it informs a delivery commitment.

### Key points

- Agile delivery is a disciplined family of iterative and incremental approaches controlled empirically — through transparency, inspection and adaptation — with accountability for business case, expenditure and benefits unchanged.
- The delivery approach is an explicit, revisited leadership decision driven by requirement volatility, stakeholder availability and organisational readiness; hybrid delivery is a professionally legitimate outcome.
