- The ordered, continuously refined product backlog is the sole source of work; timeboxes fix duration and quality while scope flexes, closing with a review that adapts the product and a retrospective that adapts the process.
- Velocity, throughput and cycle time are team-local empirical signals for planning — never inter-team comparators, and never targets.
- Forecasts are ranges with stated assumptions and confidence, revalidated each iteration; partial iterations always round up to whole timeboxes.
- AI accelerates backlog hygiene, flow analytics and probabilistic forecasting, but the suitability decision, prioritisation, commitments to governance and the interpretation of metrics remain human accountabilities.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: every AI output must be explainable, validated and owned before it reaches a governance body.

### Practice questions

**1.** A team works in two-week iterations. The refined release backlog totals 180 story points and mean velocity over recent iterations is 24 points per iteration. What is the central estimate of release duration?

A. 14 weeks
B. 15 weeks
C. 16 weeks
D. 7.5 weeks

**Answer: C.** 180 ÷ 24 = 7.5, which rounds up to 8 whole timeboxes (a partial iteration is a whole timebox), and 8 × 2 weeks = 16 weeks.

**2.** A PMO proposes ranking all delivery teams by velocity and setting each team a velocity improvement target. What is the professionally correct response?

A. Agree, because velocity is the standard agile productivity measure
B. Agree, provided story points are first normalised across teams
C. Decline, because story points are relative, team-local units and a targeted velocity ceases to be a valid measure
D. Agree, provided the targets are set by the teams themselves

**Answer: C.** Velocity is a planning signal local to one team; comparing or targeting it invokes Goodhart's law, inflating estimates and corrupting every forecast built on the data.

**3.** An AI tool has produced a probabilistic release forecast and a re-ordered product backlog. Which action is consistent with Institute doctrine?

A. Present the forecast to the steering group unchanged, citing the tool as the source
B. Adopt the suggested backlog order automatically, since the tool has analysed more data than any individual
C. Validate the forecast's data and assumptions before use, refer the suggested ordering to the product owner for decision, and own every number presented to governance
D. Reject both outputs, because AI-generated analysis may not inform governance decisions

**Answer: C.** AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable — AI outputs are inputs to accountable human decisions, neither adopted unexamined nor rejected outright.

## 14. Hybrid Delivery

### 14.1 What hybrid delivery is — and what it is not

Every project sits somewhere on a spectrum of development approaches. At one end lies the **predictive approach**, in which scope, schedule and cost are defined early, baselined and protected by formal change control; work proceeds through sequential phases and progress is measured against the approved plan. At the other end lies the **adaptive approach**, in which detailed requirements are deliberately allowed to emerge: work is organised into short, fixed-length iterations, scope is drawn from a continuously prioritised backlog, and the stable quantities are team capacity, cadence and quality rather than a fully specified scope. Between them sit **iterative** techniques (repeated refinement of an evolving product) and **incremental** techniques (delivery of usable functionality in successive slices), which either kind of project may employ.

**Hybrid delivery** is the deliberate, designed combination of predictive and adaptive approaches within a single project or programme, in which each component of the work is assigned the approach that best fits its characteristics, and the whole is bound together by one coherent governance framework. Three configurations recur in practice: *component hybrids*, where parallel workstreams run under different approaches — a construction package managed predictively alongside a software product built adaptively; *life-cycle hybrids*, where successive phases change approach — predictive definition and procurement, adaptive build, predictive commissioning and handover; and *governance hybrids*, where an adaptive delivery engine operates inside a predictive stage-gate wrapper demanded by the funding or regulatory environment. The defining test in every case is intent. A hybrid model is a designed configuration, justified component by component; an accidental mixture of methods, adopted through drift or fashion, is not hybrid delivery but the absence of a delivery decision.

### 14.2 Tailoring as the governing principle

Hybrid delivery is the most visible product of **tailoring**: the deliberate adaptation of the delivery approach, governance arrangements and management processes to the context of a specific project. Three principles govern it. First, tailoring is decided against assessable characteristics of the work — requirements stability, technical novelty, cost of change, regulatory or contractual constraint, criticality of safety or compliance, and the feasibility of releasing value incrementally — not against team preference. Second, tailoring is a governance act: the chosen configuration is recorded, justified and approved by the sponsoring authority, so that divergence from organisational method is visible rather than silent. Third, tailoring is revisited: the assignment of approaches is reviewed at stage boundaries and adjusted as uncertainty resolves.

The principal published sources of professional guidance converge on this position: international standards require the management framework to be tailored across the predictive-to-adaptive range; the major bodies of knowledge treat the development approach as a design choice made per deliverable; the leading structured-governance methods combine stage-based direction and management by exception with the expectation that delivery within a stage may be iterative; and widely used agile frameworks supply the cadences, backlog disciplines and empirical review mechanisms on which adaptive components rely. References to such standards are contextual: the Institute does not claim accreditation by, affiliation with, or endorsement from their publishers, and the PML-AI examination assesses reasoning with these concepts, not recital of any proprietary framework.

### 14.3 Core vocabulary and the operating model

Four terms carry the analytical weight of this chapter. A **component** is any separable element of the project — a workstream, deliverable, sub-project or phase — to which a development approach can be assigned. A **cadence** is the rhythm at which a component plans, delivers and reviews: gate-to-gate for predictive components, iteration-by-iteration for adaptive ones. A **stage gate** is a governance checkpoint at which the sponsoring authority reviews evidence and authorises, redirects or halts continued investment. A **baseline** is the approved reference against which performance is measured: in predictive components, scope, schedule and cost are baselined in detail; in adaptive components, the baseline is properly set at the level of capacity, cadence, release milestones and outcome targets, while detailed scope remains intentionally fluid beneath it.

These pieces interlock as follows. The hybrid **operating model** maps components to approaches on justified grounds; governance then reconciles the resulting cadences by requiring each gate to consume evidence in the form each component naturally produces — earned value and milestone data from predictive components; throughput, burn-up and increment demonstrations from adaptive ones — while preserving a single point of accountability for the integrated result. Dependencies concentrate at the **interfaces** between components, where one approach must make firm commitments to another, and there the characteristic failure modes of hybrid delivery arise: cadences that never synchronise, controls duplicated across two management systems, and accountability that dissolves between them.

### 14.4 Designing the hybrid model: a step-by-step method

Hybrid delivery is designed, not declared. The Institute expects candidates to follow a repeatable method and defend every assignment decision on evidence.

**Step 1 — Decompose and profile.** Break the project into components at the level where a single delivery approach can genuinely apply, and profile each against the tailoring criteria of section 14.2 — in particular requirements volatility, cost of change, regulatory or contractual constraint, and integration criticality.

**Step 2 — Assign an approach per component, with written justification.** Stable requirements, high cost of change and hard external constraints indicate predictive control; volatile requirements and cheap, reversible change indicate adaptive delivery. Record the rationale — an unjustified assignment is indistinguishable from habit.

**Step 3 — Establish the governance spine.** Place stage gates on the predictive backbone and define, for each gate, what evidence the adaptive stream must present. Gates consume evidence of working product and validated forecasts, not documents produced for the gate's sake.

**Step 4 — Baseline each stream appropriately.** The predictive stream carries a conventional cost and schedule baseline measured by earned value. The adaptive stream carries a capacity-and-outcome baseline: a fixed team run rate, a prioritised backlog and a velocity-based completion forecast. Do not baseline the backlog item by item.

**Step 5 — Synchronise cadences.** Set the reporting period to a whole number of sprints and place every gate on a sprint boundary. Enter every cross-stream dependency in a register with a named owner, a need-by date and the sprint or stage that satisfies it.

**Step 6 — Integrate the forecast and assign single accountability.** Combine the earned value forecast and the velocity forecast into one programme position, reported side by side. Where AI-enabled tools propose completion dates or risk flags, the doctrine of section 14.7 applies without exception: the delivery leader validates the inputs, explains the arithmetic and owns the published forecast.

### 14.5 Worked example: reconciling a stage gate with an adaptive forecast

A logistics operator is delivering an automated distribution centre with a total budget of £8.40m. Component A — building fit-out and conveyor installation — is predictive (£6.00m, 40 weeks). Component B — the warehouse management software — is adaptive (£2.40m budget; a stable team costing £150,000 per two-week sprint; a backlog estimated at 480 story points). Gate 3, integrated commissioning, is fixed at week 32 by the mechanical completion of the conveyors.

At the week-20 review, six software sprints are complete, 216 points are delivered and £0.90m is spent. Velocity over the last three sprints was 38, 42 and 40 points, so the rolling mean is (38 + 42 + 40) ÷ 3 = **40 points per sprint**.

*Adaptive forecast.* Remaining backlog = 480 − 216 = 264 points. Sprints required = 264 ÷ 40 = 6.6, rounded up to **7 sprints = 14 weeks**, giving forecast completion at week 20 + 14 = **week 34** — two weeks beyond the gate. The correct hybrid lever is scope, not schedule: 80 points of features not required for commissioning are moved to a post-gate release. Revised pre-gate backlog = 264 − 80 = 184 points; 184 ÷ 40 = 4.6, rounded up to **5 sprints = 10 weeks**; completion at week 30, restoring a two-week buffer before the gate.

*Predictive forecast.* Component A at week 20 shows planned value £3.00m, earned value £2.70m and actual cost £2.85m. CPI = 2.70 ÷ 2.85 = **0.947**; SPI = 2.70 ÷ 3.00 = **0.90**. Estimate at completion = 6.00 ÷ 0.947 = **£6.33m**, a £0.33m overrun.

*Integrated position.* Component B's forecast cost — including the post-gate release of the deferred 80 points, seven sprints in all — is £0.90m + (7 × £0.15m) = **£1.95m**, £0.45m under budget. The programme forecast is £6.33m + £1.95m = **£8.28m** against £8.40m. The delivery leader reports both components separately: the programme is within budget only because the software underspend masks the construction overrun, and netting the two without governance visibility would conceal a control problem. If an AI forecasting assistant produced the £8.28m figure, the leader must still reproduce this arithmetic, test the velocity assumption and own the number presented at the gate.

### 14.6 Common pitfalls

- **Hybrid by indecision** — attaching agile ceremonies to an unchanged predictive plan ("water-scrum-fall"), gaining the overhead of both approaches with the flexibility of neither.
- **Misaligned cadences** — gates or reporting cut-offs landing mid-sprint, forcing the adaptive team to fabricate percent-complete figures between demonstrations.
- **Duplicated control** — measuring the adaptive stream with both earned value task tracking and velocity, producing two contradictory forecasts and doubled reporting effort.
- **Freezing the backlog** — baselining adaptive scope item by item, which converts every reprioritisation into formal change control and defeats the purpose of the assignment.
- **Netting variances across streams** — presenting only the combined forecast, so one component's underspend hides another's overrun.
- **Interfaces with two owners or none** — dependencies logged without a single accountable name and a need-by date.
- **Treating rounded-up forecasts as certainties** — reporting "7 sprints" without the confidence range implied by velocity variation.
- **Unowned AI output** — publishing machine-generated completion dates or risk scores that no competent professional has validated or can explain.

### 14.7 AI in this competency: reading two data regimes as one project

Hybrid delivery is the setting in which AI-enabled project management offers the greatest assistance and the greatest scope for error, for the same reason: the project generates two dissimilar data regimes. Predictive components produce baseline-referenced data — planned value, milestone status, gate criteria, change-log entries — while adaptive components produce cadence-referenced telemetry such as throughput, velocity, cycle time and backlog movement. Translating between the two for governance purposes has traditionally been the delivery leader's burden; it is precisely what modern tooling now automates. Probabilistic forecasting models can convert throughput history into a distribution of completion dates expressed against the stage-gate plan; classification and language tools can assemble gate documentation from sprint records, backlog items and decision logs; anomaly detection can flag when reported gate status and underlying delivery telemetry diverge — often the earliest warning of the "watermelon" report, green outside and red within.

The candidate should distinguish mechanical translation from delivery judgement. Suitable for automation: consolidating adaptive metrics into the integrated reporting calendar; first-draft mapping of completed backlog items to baseline scope; monitoring dependencies across workstreams on different cadences; drafting gate submissions from the delivery record. Requiring validation before use: any probabilistic forecast presented to a gate or funding decision; every scope mapping the tooling proposes, because whether an increment genuinely discharges a baseline commitment is an acceptance judgement, not a data join; inferred cross-workstream dependencies, which models deduce from correlation rather than knowledge of the technical interface; and any suggested rebaseline. One decision is never delegated: the assignment of predictive or adaptive approaches to components. That is the tailoring judgement on which the whole operating model rests, and a model trained on past projects will reproduce past habits, not reason about this project's uncertainty profile.

Three model-risk caveats are examinable. First, adaptive telemetry is behavioural, not contractual: velocity is team-relative, unstable under team change and susceptible to gaming, and forecasts trained on it inherit every distortion, usually from a small sample of sprints. Second, the two regimes rarely share definitions — of "done", of dates, of scope granularity — and a model that fuses them without a governed data dictionary produces confident, precisely wrong forecasts; the integration work of section 14.4 is a data-governance precondition, not merely a reporting nicety. Third, generative summarisation introduces a seam-specific hazard: a fluent AI-drafted gate paper can smooth over exactly the ambiguity the gate exists to expose. Tooling must also not recreate the duplicated-control failure of section 14.6 in digital form: two dashboards, drawn from differently filtered data, telling the sponsor two different stories.

### 14.8 Human accountability

Every AI output entering the governance record — a forecast, a mapping, a drafted gate submission — must be explainable to the board that relies on it, validated against source data, and owned by a named, competent professional. Gate decisions remain human decisions taken by identified role-holders, and AI-assisted artefacts should be marked as such, with the reviewing owner recorded. Where accountability for a hybrid component is already contested, tooling amplifies the ambiguity rather than resolving it: assign the single accountable owner first, then instrument.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** In hybrid delivery, AI may translate adaptive telemetry into stage-gate language, forecast completion probabilistically and draft the governance record — but the professional decides which components are predictive and which adaptive, judges whether an increment truly discharges a baseline commitment, and answers to the gate. The seam between the two delivery worlds is held together by accountable human judgement, never by the tooling that reports across it.

### Key points

- Hybrid delivery is a designed, justified combination of predictive and adaptive approaches under one governance framework; an accidental mixture of methods is the absence of a delivery decision, not a hybrid.
- Tailoring is decided per component against assessable characteristics — requirements volatility, cost of change, regulatory constraint, integration criticality — then recorded, approved and revisited at stage boundaries.
- Baseline each stream in its own currency: detailed scope, schedule and cost for predictive components; capacity, cadence, release milestones and outcomes for adaptive ones — never the backlog item by item.
- Synchronise cadences: set reporting periods to a whole number of sprints, place gates on sprint boundaries, and give every cross-stream dependency a single named owner and a need-by date.
- When an adaptive forecast misses a fixed gate, the correct lever is scope — defer features not needed for the gate — not schedule; and report the streams' variances side by side, never netted.
- AI may translate telemetry between the two regimes, forecast probabilistically and draft the governance record, but approach assignment is never delegated, and every AI output entering the record must be explainable, validated and owned by a named, competent human: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Practice questions

**Question 1.** A programme runs a predictively managed construction package alongside an adaptively built software product under a single stage-gate framework. What makes this genuine hybrid delivery rather than an accidental mixture of methods?

A. The presence of both a critical-path schedule and a product backlog
B. The deliberate, justified assignment of an approach to each component under one governance framework
C. The use of sprints inside a formally gated stage
D. The approval of a single combined budget by the sponsor

**Correct answer: B.** The defining test of hybrid delivery is designed intent — approaches assigned and justified component by component under coherent governance — not the mere co-existence of techniques.

**Question 2.** An adaptive stream has 264 story points remaining and a rolling mean velocity of 40 points per two-week sprint. How many weeks should the delivery leader forecast to complete the backlog?

A. 12 weeks
B. 13.2 weeks
C. 14 weeks
D. 20 weeks

**Correct answer: C.** 264 ÷ 40 = 6.6 sprints, rounded up to 7 whole two-week sprints because partial sprints cannot be planned.

**Question 3.** Which decision may never be delegated to an AI-enabled tool in hybrid delivery?

A. Drafting a first-cut gate submission from the delivery record
B. Consolidating adaptive metrics into the integrated reporting calendar
C. Flagging divergence between reported gate status and delivery telemetry
D. Assigning predictive or adaptive approaches to components

**Correct answer: D.** Approach assignment is the tailoring judgement on which the operating model rests; a model trained on past projects reproduces past habits rather than reasoning about the current project's uncertainty.

## 15. Quality and Change Management

This chapter treats four disciplines as one system of control. Quality planning defines what a conforming output is; quality assurance and quality control demonstrate that conformance is being achieved; integrated change control protects the baselines that give "conformance" its meaning; and organisational change management carries the conforming output into use, where benefits are realised. AI-enabled tools now assist every strand, within the governance discipline that closes the chapter.

### Quality as a defined, managed property of project outputs

Quality, in the project context, is the degree to which a project's outputs conform to stated requirements and satisfy the needs for which they were commissioned. It is not a synonym for excellence or high specification: a low-grade output can be of high quality if it meets its defined requirements reliably, and a high-grade output can be of poor quality if it does not. Candidates must hold this distinction between quality and grade precisely, because it anchors every subsequent decision about standards, acceptance criteria and cost.

Quality management comprises three related but distinct activities. **Quality planning** determines which standards and regulatory obligations apply to the project's outputs, translates requirements into measurable acceptance criteria, and specifies how conformance will be demonstrated, by whom and at what points in the delivery lifecycle; its principal product is the quality management plan. **Quality assurance** is process-facing and forward-looking: it provides independent confidence that the methods, competences and controls in use are capable of producing conforming outputs, through audits, peer reviews, method-compliance checks and gate reviews — most credible when performed independently of the work being assured. **Quality control** is product-facing and evidence-based: it inspects, tests and measures actual outputs against the defined acceptance criteria and identifies non-conformances for correction.

The governing principle across all three is prevention over inspection: building quality in almost always costs less than failure, a relationship the cost-of-quality model formalises by setting the costs of conformance (prevention and appraisal) against the costs of non-conformance (internal and external failure).

### The governing frameworks

Project quality management sits within a wider standards landscape. The ISO 9000 family articulates the quality management principles — customer focus, leadership, engagement of people, process approach, improvement, evidence-based decision-making and relationship management — from which project-level practice derives. ISO 10005 provides guidance on quality plans, ISO 10006 addresses quality management in projects specifically, and ISO 21502 treats quality as an integrated component of project management guidance. The continuous-improvement tradition associated with Deming and Juran, expressed in the plan–do–check–act (PDCA) cycle, underpins both predictive and adaptive delivery: in agile contexts it surfaces as built-in quality, a maintained definition of done, and retrospectives as a structured improvement mechanism. The PML-AI examination tests the candidate's ability to apply these frameworks to delivery situations, not to recite them.

### Producing the quality management plan

A quality management plan is built deliverable by deliverable, not written as a general essay. The Institute expects candidates to follow six steps. First, identify the standards that apply to each output — contractual specifications, regulatory obligations and the organisation's own quality management system. Second, convert each standard into measurable acceptance criteria: quantified thresholds a competent reviewer could test, never adjectives. Third, define assurance activities — peer reviews, stage-gate reviews and independent audits — specifying timing, ownership and independence from the delivery team. Fourth, define control measures: the inspections, tests and sampling regimes applied to outputs, with the metrics and tolerances that trigger corrective action. Fifth, estimate the cost of quality, separating prevention and appraisal costs from internal and external failure costs, and carry the figures in the budget. Sixth, assign responsibilities and the escalation route for non-conformances.

Continuous improvement runs on the same data. Plot recurring metrics such as defect escape rate on a control chart to distinguish common-cause variation from genuine signals; use Pareto analysis to isolate the vital few causes; test remedies through the PDCA cycle; and return confirmed lessons to the quality management system.

### Integrated change control

A **baseline** is an approved reference — for scope, schedule, cost or a product's configuration — against which performance is measured and from which no departure is made without authorisation. A **change request** is a formal proposal to depart from a baseline, whatever its origin: a stakeholder requirement, a defect, a regulatory development or a corrective action. **Integrated change control** is the governed process by which such proposals are captured, assessed, dispositioned, implemented and communicated. The word *integrated* is load-bearing: before disposition, each proposal is assessed for its impact across scope, cost, schedule, quality, risk, resources and — critically — the benefits case, because a change that is affordable in cost and schedule terms may still erode the outcome the project exists to deliver.

Disposition authority rests with a defined change authority, commonly a change control board, operating within tolerances delegated by the project's governance tier; changes exceeding those tolerances are escalated. The available dispositions are approval, rejection or deferral, and every disposition is recorded with its rationale. Configuration management is the companion record-keeping discipline: it keeps the approved version and status of every product known at all times, so the baseline being changed is unambiguous and each approved change is traceable.

The method has seven steps: capture the request on the change log; screen it for completeness; assess impact on scope, cost, schedule, risk and benefits; recommend a disposition; route the decision to the correct authority level; update baselines, registers and the benefits case on approval; and communicate the outcome. The worked example below shows the assessment arithmetic.

### Worked example: assessing change request CR-041

A 40-week systems project has an approved budget of £1,200,000 and a benefits case worth £10,000 per week from go-live, under a contract whose benefit window ends on a fixed date. Change request CR-041 proposes extending the compliance module to meet newly published data-retention rules.

**Cost.** The estimate is 280 hours of design, build and test at £95 per hour = £26,600, plus £6,000 of additional test-environment costs = £32,600. A 10 per cent estimating allowance adds £3,260, giving a total of £35,860 — 3.0 per cent of the approved budget (£35,860 ÷ £1,200,000 = 2.99 per cent).

**Schedule.** The work adds four weeks to the build activity, which holds one week of float, so the critical path extends by 4 − 1 = 3 weeks.

**Benefits.** Because the benefit window closes on a fixed date, a 3-week deferral of go-live permanently forgoes 3 × £10,000 = £30,000.

**Risk.** Approval retires the non-compliance risk, assessed at 40 per cent probability of a £250,000 regulatory penalty — an expected monetary value of 0.40 × £250,000 = £100,000. It introduces a new integration risk of 0.20 × £25,000 = £5,000. Net risk exposure falls by £100,000 − £5,000 = £95,000.

**Net appraisal and disposition.** Expected value of approving = £95,000 − (£35,860 + £30,000) = **+£29,140**, so the assessment recommends approval. Because £35,860 exceeds the project manager's £25,000 delegated tolerance, the disposition belongs to the project board, which approves the change funded from the change budget. The cost and schedule baselines are re-versioned from v1.2 to v1.3, the risk register and benefits profile are updated, and future performance is measured against the new baseline. An AI assistant may draft this assessment from the schedule model and risk register, but the professional validates every figure and owns the decision presented to the board.

### Organisational change and adoption

Integrated change control governs changes *to* the project; organisational change management governs the change the project causes *in* its host organisation. The distinction matters because outputs generate benefits only when their intended users adopt them into working practice, so adoption is planned, resourced work — led visibly by the sponsor, supported by business change roles close to the affected users — never an assumed consequence of delivery.

Recognised models give this work structure, and the adoption plan applies one of them rather than a communications calendar alone. Using ADKAR: assess each user group against Awareness, Desire, Knowledge, Ability and Reinforcement; target interventions at the weakest element first (sponsor-led messaging for awareness and desire; training for knowledge; coached practice and floor-walking for ability; measurement and recognition for reinforcement); then sequence these against the release plan. Kotter's eight-step model, building urgency, coalition, vision and reinforcement, and Lewin's unfreeze–change–refreeze serve equally where the examination scenario suits them. Define adoption metrics in advance — for example, percentage of active users at week four and error rates against the pre-change baseline — and track them in the benefits realisation plan.

### How AI changes quality and change management

AI reaches this competency along all three of the chapter's threads. In quality management, machine learning extends the classical toolset: models trained on inspection and test histories predict where defects will concentrate, so assurance effort can be risk-weighted rather than spread evenly; anomaly detection watches process data continuously instead of at sampling intervals; and language models check requirements and acceptance criteria for ambiguity, contradiction and untestability before they are baselined — a new capability, since poor criteria historically surfaced only at acceptance. In integrated change control, AI accelerates the analytical core of the process: tracing a proposed change through the scope, schedule, cost, risk and benefits baselines, retrieving comparable past changes and their outturns, and triaging the change log so trivial requests do not consume board attention. In organisational change, adoption analytics — usage telemetry, training completion, support-ticket themes — give early, quantified sight of whether outputs are actually being taken up by their intended users.

The delivery leader may appropriately automate the clerical layer: administration of the change log; first-draft impact assessments assembled from the linked baselines; consistency checks between a change request and the artefacts it touches; control-chart signal detection and non-conformance trending; and first drafts of adoption communications and training materials — each removing latency and transcription error from a process whose value lies elsewhere.

What is never automated is disposition. A machine-drafted impact assessment is a hypothesis: before it informs a decision, a competent professional must trace its cost and schedule figures to their assumptions, confirm that the affected baselines were correctly identified, and test whether the retrieved "comparable" changes are genuinely comparable. Acceptance of deliverables remains a human judgement against the criteria in the quality management plan — an AI verdict that outputs "pass" is evidence to be weighed, not a decision taken. Likewise, adoption analytics inform the change plan but do not replace conversation with the people expected to change.

### Model risk, data caveats and human accountability

Quality data carries a specific inheritance problem: defect predictors learn where defects were *found*, which reflects where inspection was directed, not necessarily where failure lives. Sparse or inconsistently coded non-conformance records, screening thresholds tuned on one supplier or technology and reused on another, and impact estimates drawn from analogues that no longer apply can all be systematically wrong while appearing precise. Two behavioural hazards follow. Automation bias tempts a change authority to wave through a machine-scored "low impact" change without scrutiny — precisely the unexamined change that integrated change control exists to prevent. And adoption analytics process data about identifiable individuals, so privacy, proportionality and works-council or equivalent obligations apply before any such monitoring is deployed. Models, prompts, thresholds and training data belong under version control as governed artefacts, and the quality assurance function should audit the AI pipeline with the same independence it applies to any other process.

Accountability is untouched by the tooling. The change authority named in the governance framework — individual, board or sponsor — owns every disposition, and the record must show a named person approving, rejecting or deferring each change, whatever analysis supported them. The delivery leader owns the quality management plan and answers for the fitness of accepted outputs; sponsors and line managers own adoption. Where an AI-derived assessment informed a disposition or an acceptance, the record should disclose it, and someone competent must be able to explain it.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI may draft the impact assessment, flag the anomaly, predict the defect and chart the adoption curve; only an accountable person approves the change, accepts the deliverable and answers for the baseline. A change dispositioned on an explanation nobody can give is an uncontrolled change — however sophisticated the tool that scored it.

### Common pitfalls

- Acceptance criteria written as adjectives ("robust", "intuitive") that no reviewer can objectively test.
- Confusing quality assurance (process-focused, preventive) with quality control (product-focused, detective), and resourcing only one of them.
- Impact assessments that price cost and schedule but omit risk and benefits — in CR-041, the £95,000 exposure reduction that justified approval would have been invisible.
- Dispositions taken at the wrong authority level, or approved changes never re-baselined, so performance is measured against a superseded plan.
- Scope creep through "minor" changes accepted outside the change log, and gold-plating that adds unrequested refinement at the project's cost.
- Treating training as the whole adoption plan while neglecting desire and reinforcement, the elements where adoption most often fails.
- Presenting an AI-drafted impact assessment to a change authority unvalidated; the professional who signs the recommendation owns its figures.

### Key points

- Quality is conformance to stated requirements, not grade; quality planning, assurance (process-facing) and control (product-facing) are distinct activities, governed by prevention over inspection and priced by the cost-of-quality model.
- The quality management plan is built deliverable by deliverable in six steps, converting applicable standards into measurable acceptance criteria with named assurance and control activities, costs and escalation routes.
- Integrated change control protects approved baselines: every change request is impact-assessed across scope, cost, schedule, quality, risk and benefits before a recorded disposition by the correct authority, with configuration management keeping baselines unambiguous.
- Approved changes are re-baselined and carried into the risk register and benefits case; as CR-041 shows, a change affordable in cost terms can still destroy value through lost benefits.
- Organisational change management (Lewin, Kotter, ADKAR) turns delivered outputs into adopted outputs; adoption is planned, sponsored and measured work, because unadopted outputs yield no benefits.
- AI legitimately automates the clerical layer — drafting assessments, detecting anomalies, triaging the change log — but disposition and acceptance are never automated, and AI models, prompts and thresholds are governed, auditable artefacts.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: every AI-generated assessment must be explainable, validated against evidence and owned by a named, accountable person before it informs a decision.

### Practice questions

**1.** During execution, an independent reviewer audits whether the project team is actually following its documented peer-review procedure. This activity is best described as:

A. Quality control
B. Quality planning
C. Quality assurance
D. Configuration management

**Correct answer: C.** Quality assurance is process-facing and preventive — it independently confirms that the methods in use can produce conforming outputs, whereas quality control (A) inspects the outputs themselves.

**2.** A change request costs £18,000 and adds three weeks to an activity holding one week of float, on a project earning £8,000 per week of benefits within a fixed benefit window. Retiring an existing risk reduces expected exposure by £40,000 with no new risk introduced. The expected value of approving the change is:

A. +£6,000
B. −£2,000
C. +£22,000
D. +£14,000

**Correct answer: A.** The critical path extends by 3 − 1 = 2 weeks, forgoing 2 × £8,000 = £16,000 of benefits, so the appraisal is £40,000 − (£18,000 + £16,000) = +£6,000.

**3.** An AI triage tool scores a change request as "low impact" and recommends fast-track approval. Under the Institute's doctrine, the change authority should:

A. Approve the change, since the tool was validated at deployment
B. Treat the score as a proposal, validate the assessment against the baselines and evidence, and record a named human disposition
C. Reject the change, because AI-scored requests cannot be trusted
D. Defer the change until the tool's confidence exceeds a set threshold

**Correct answer: B.** AI proposes and the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable — machine output is evidence to be validated and owned by a named, accountable person, and waving it through unexamined (A) is automation bias.

## 16. Benefits Realisation

A project that delivers on time, on budget and to specification can still fail its investors, because schedules and budgets measure *delivery*, not *worth*. Benefits realisation is the discipline that closes this gap. This chapter sets out its concepts, the artefacts that operationalise them, the tracking regime that must outlive the project, and how AI-enabled tooling changes the economics of the discipline without changing where accountability sits.

### From outputs to benefits: the concepts, precisely

The vocabulary of benefits realisation must be exact, because the terms form a causal chain, and confusion between links in that chain is the single most common source of unrealised business cases.

An **output** is a deliverable the project produces — a system, an asset, a process, a trained team. A **capability** is what the organisation *can now do* because the outputs exist and have been adopted: outputs assembled, commissioned and embedded in working practice. An **outcome** is the changed operational state that results when the capability is actually used — faster order processing, fewer defects, shorter patient waits. A **benefit** is the measurable improvement resulting from an outcome that is perceived as an advantage by one or more stakeholders and that contributes to a strategic objective — typically expressed in money, time, quality, risk reduction or stakeholder value. A **disbenefit** is a measurable outcome perceived as negative by a stakeholder — for example, redundancy costs or increased workload elsewhere — and must be identified and managed with the same rigour, not hidden. The **strategic objective** is the organisational goal, stated outside the project, that the benefit serves.

The chain — outputs enable capabilities, capabilities enable outcomes, outcomes yield benefits, benefits serve objectives — is the logical backbone of this chapter. Each link is a *dependency*, not an automatic consequence: an output that is delivered but not adopted creates no capability; a capability that is not exercised produces no outcome; an outcome that no one measures yields no demonstrable benefit.

### The governing frameworks

The candidate should recognise the framework landscape in which this discipline sits. Programme-management practice — notably the Managing Successful Programmes (MSP) tradition — established benefits management as a core theme, including the benefits map, the benefit profile and the principle that programmes exist to deliver outcomes and benefits rather than outputs. PMI has published dedicated guidance on benefits realisation management, and the *PMBOK Guide* embeds benefits within the business case and project charter. The APM Body of Knowledge treats benefits management as integral to both project and programme domains, ISO 21502 frames benefits as the rationale that justifies initiating and continuing a project, and public-investment traditions such as the UK Treasury's Green Book add the discipline of appraising and then *evaluating* benefits against the original case. This Body of Knowledge draws on the shared logic of these sources in its own words and does not claim conformance with, or accreditation against, any of them; the PML-AI examination tests the principles as defined here.

Two framework principles recur everywhere. First, **the business case is the anchor**: benefits are forecast in the business case to justify investment, and realisation is always reported *against that forecast*, not against a conveniently revised one. Second, **benefits outlive the project**: most benefits accrue after handover, so accountability for them cannot end at project closure.

### The benefits map: built from right to left, read from left to right

Three artefacts operationalise the concepts — the benefits map, the benefit profile and the benefits realisation plan — and each answers a distinct question. The **benefits map** answers *"why does this work matter?"* It is a directed diagram reading left to right from outputs, through capabilities and outcomes, to benefits and strategic objectives. Read forwards, it demonstrates the investment logic; read backwards, it tests scope.

Although the finished map is read from outputs towards strategy, it is *constructed* backwards from strategy, not forwards from deliverables. The candidate should apply the following steps.

1. **State the strategic objective** the investment serves, in the sponsor's own words (e.g. "grow service revenue without growing headcount").
2. **Identify the measurable benefits** that would evidence progress against that objective, and any expected **disbenefits** to be managed openly.
3. **Trace each benefit back to the outcomes** — changes in the way the organisation operates — that must occur for the benefit to arise.
4. **Trace each outcome back to the capabilities** created when project outputs are combined with business change (training, revised processes, new roles).
5. **Trace capabilities back to project outputs**, then test every link for causality: "if this output is delivered and adopted, will that outcome plausibly follow?" Delete orphan outputs that support no benefit, and flag orphan benefits that no output supports — both are early warnings of a weak business case.

Where AI-assisted tools draft candidate maps or mine historical benefit data, the Institute's doctrine applies in full: every causal link must be explainable and owned by a named individual, not inferred by a model and left unexamined.

### The benefit profile: what exactly is this benefit, and how will we know?

For every benefit on the map, complete a one-page **benefit profile** recording: a precise description; a classification (cashable, non-cashable, strategic); the accountable **benefit owner** — a business manager, never the project manager; the **measure**, its unit and its data source; the **baseline** value, captured *before* delivery alters it; the **target** value and realisation timescale; the **realisation trajectory** (ramp-up and full run-rate dates); measurement frequency; attribution and assumptions; dependencies on business change; associated disbenefits; and links to the benefits map and business case.

Two elements are non-negotiable. Without a baseline there is no evidence of improvement — a baseline reconstructed after go-live is an estimate, not evidence, so baselining must precede delivery. Without a named owner there is no one answerable for the benefit; owners sit in the *business*, not the delivery team, because benefits are harvested in operations after the project team has disbanded.

### The benefits realisation plan: tracking beyond closure

The **benefits realisation plan** answers *"when, and by whose hand?"* It schedules measurement points, assigns tracking responsibility during delivery and — critically — beyond closure, and defines the reporting line through which realisation is compared with the business-case forecast. The candidate should design the regime as follows.

1. **Write the plan during delivery**, naming review points that extend past project closure (typically 6, 12, 24 and 36 months).
2. **Transfer accountability at handover**: each profile passes formally from the project to its benefit owner in operations, with the measurement method and data source tested and working. This transition is a transfer of live accountability, not an administrative filing.
3. **Report realisation against the business case**, benefit by benefit, at each review — actual versus profile, forecast at completion, and corrective actions where adoption is lagging.
4. **Give the regime a home**: a portfolio office, sponsor board or equivalent must own post-closure reviews, because the project board no longer exists to demand them.
5. **Close the loop**: feed realised-versus-promised results back into estimating assumptions for future business cases.

### Worked example: field-service scheduling capability

**Setup.** A project delivers a dynamic scheduling system plus retrained dispatch processes for a maintenance business with 120 technicians working 220 days per year. Baseline productivity is 3.9 completed jobs per technician per day; the target is 4.5. Contribution per additional job is USD 38. Incremental running costs (licences, support, model maintenance) are USD 150,000 per year. The profile assumes 40% of the productivity uplift is realised in year 1 and 100% in years 2 and 3. The business case commits to a net benefit of USD 950,000 over three years.

**Full-run-rate gross benefit.** Uplift = 4.5 − 3.9 = 0.6 jobs/technician/day.
Additional jobs per year = 120 × 0.6 × 220 = **15,840 jobs**.
Gross annual benefit = 15,840 × USD 38 = **USD 601,920**.

**Net benefit by year.**
Year 1: (0.40 × 601,920) − 150,000 = 240,768 − 150,000 = **USD 90,768**.
Years 2 and 3: 601,920 − 150,000 = **USD 451,920** each.
Three-year net benefit = 90,768 + 451,920 + 451,920 = **USD 994,608**.

**Comparison with the business case.** 994,608 − 950,000 = USD 44,608 of headroom (about 4.7%) — an acceptable but slim margin, so the tracking regime must detect slippage early.

**Tracking snapshot.** At the six-month post-closure review, the 13-week rolling average is 4.2 jobs/technician/day, an uplift of 0.3 against the profiled year-1 expectation of 0.40 × 0.6 = 0.24. Annualised realised gross benefit = 120 × 0.3 × 220 × 38 = **USD 300,960**, versus the profiled 240,768 — realisation is running at 300,960 ÷ 240,768 = **125% of profile**. The benefit owner reports this to the sponsor with the evidence trail, and the forecast at completion is revised upwards only after the owner confirms the uplift is attributable to the new capability and not to seasonal workload mix.

### How AI changes benefits realisation — and what it cannot change

Benefits realisation has always suffered a structural weakness: measurement effort peaks at business-case approval and collapses after handover — precisely when benefits begin to accrue. AI-enabled tooling changes the economics of that measurement. Analytics platforms can ingest operational data continuously and report movement against benefit baselines long after closure. Predictive models can forecast realisation trajectories from early adoption signals, flagging benefits at risk of shortfall months before a scheduled review would detect them. Natural-language tools can interrogate a benefits map, testing whether every strategic benefit traces back to a delivered output and whether claimed contributions are double-counted across a portfolio. None of this changes what a benefit *is*; it changes how early, how cheaply and how persistently the organisation can see whether one is emerging.

**What the leader automates.** Automation is appropriate where the work is mechanical and the data is owned: harvesting benefit measures from source systems; scheduled reporting of actuals against the benefit profile; alerting when a tracked measure breaches tolerance; consolidating realisation data across a portfolio; and drafting candidate benefits, leading indicators and measure definitions for professional refinement. These high-volume, low-judgement tasks can be delegated, freeing the leader for the work that matters.

**What the leader validates.** Validation cannot be delegated. The leader personally tests the **causal logic** of the benefits map, because correlation in operational data is not evidence that the project caused the movement; the **attribution** of benefit to this investment rather than to concurrent initiatives, market conditions or seasonality; the **integrity of baselines**, because a model that infers its baseline from contaminated pre-project data will misstate realisation permanently; and every **forecast of future realisation** before it reaches a sponsor or board. A predicted benefit is not a realised benefit, and governance reporting must never conflate the two.

**Model-risk and data caveats.** Benefits data is among the noisiest an organisation handles, and models inherit its defects. Business cases are systematically optimistic, so a model trained on historical cases and their outturns may normalise overstatement or learn from survivorship bias — organisations rarely keep honest records of benefits that quietly failed. Measure definitions drift after handover, silently breaking comparability with the baseline, and external confounders — price changes, regulation, competitor behaviour — move the very indicators a benefit is measured by. The discipline that answers these risks is explainability: every machine-produced realisation figure must be traceable to its source data, method and assumptions, and a figure that cannot be explained must not be reported. Disbenefits deserve equal instrumentation; a tracking regime that automates only the favourable measures is a governance failure, not a technical one.

**Human accountability.** Ownership is the non-negotiable centre of this competency. Every benefit profile names a benefit owner — a human accountable for realisation against the business case — and no tool, dashboard or model can occupy that role. Where AI output feeds a realisation report, a competent individual validates it and stands behind it; where tracking continues beyond closure, the tooling, its data pipelines and its known limitations are handed over to operational ownership as deliberately as any other product. The examination expects candidates to state plainly who answers for a benefit at every point in its life — during delivery, at transition and in sustained operation.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** An analytics platform may surface the trend, forecast the shortfall and draft the realisation report — but only a named benefit owner can attest that a benefit is real, attribute it honestly to the investment, and account to the sponsor for the business case. Machine measurement informs the judgement of realisation; it never constitutes it.

### Common pitfalls

- **Baselining after go-live**, leaving no defensible "before" measure.
- **Making the project manager the benefit owner** — benefits arise in operations, after the project team has disbanded.
- **Counting gross benefits** and ignoring incremental running costs and disbenefits.
- **Double-counting** the same saving across two projects' business cases.
- **Assuming full run-rate from day one** instead of profiling a realistic ramp-up.
- **Stopping measurement at closure**, when most benefit value typically arises afterwards.
- **Claiming unattributable benefits** — movement in the measure caused by external factors, not the new capability.
- **Accepting AI-generated benefit forecasts unvalidated** — projections drafted by a model must be checked, explained and owned by the accountable professional before they enter the business case or any realisation report.

### Key points

- Outputs enable capabilities, capabilities enable outcomes, outcomes yield measurable benefits, and benefits serve strategic objectives; every link is a dependency, not an automatic consequence.
- The business case is the anchor: realisation is always reported against the original forecast, never against a conveniently revised one, and disbenefits are managed with the same rigour as benefits.
- The benefits map is built backwards from strategy and tested for causality; orphan outputs are descoping candidates and orphan benefits signal a weak business case.
- Every benefit carries a profile with a measure, a baseline captured before delivery, a target, a realisation trajectory and a named owner drawn from the business, never the delivery team.
- Because most benefits accrue after handover, the benefits realisation plan schedules post-closure reviews, transfers live accountability to operations, and lodges the regime with a body that outlives the project board.
- AI-enabled tooling makes measurement continuous and cheap — harvesting actuals, forecasting trajectories, flagging shortfalls — but causal logic, attribution, baseline integrity and every forward forecast remain the professional's to validate.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: only a named human benefit owner can attest that a benefit is real and account to the sponsor for the business case.

### Practice questions

**1.** A project delivers a new rostering system and trains all shift supervisors to use it. Six months later, average overtime spend per depot has fallen by 12%. In benefits-realisation terms, the reduction in overtime spend is best described as:

A. An output
B. A capability
C. A benefit
D. A strategic objective

**Answer: C.** The 12% reduction is a measurable improvement, perceived as an advantage and expressed against a baseline — a benefit; the system is the output and the trained, embedded rostering practice is the capability.

**2.** A benefit profile assumes 80 engineers working 200 days per year, a productivity uplift of 0.5 jobs per engineer per day at USD 40 contribution per job, incremental running costs of USD 100,000 per year, and 50% realisation of the uplift in year 1. The profiled net benefit for year 1 is:

A. USD 60,000
B. USD 160,000
C. USD 220,000
D. USD 320,000

**Answer: A.** Full-run-rate gross benefit is 80 × 0.5 × 200 × 40 = USD 320,000; year 1 gross is 50% of that (USD 160,000), minus USD 100,000 running costs = USD 60,000.

**3.** An AI analytics platform tracking a benefit after handover forecasts a significant realisation shortfall and drafts the report for the next post-implementation review. Under the Institute's doctrine, what must happen before the report reaches the sponsor?

A. The platform submits the report automatically, since its data feed is authoritative
B. The project manager, as benefit owner, signs off the forecast
C. The named benefit owner validates the figures, confirms attribution and stands behind the report
D. The forecast is excluded, because AI output may never appear in governance reporting

**Answer: C.** Machine output may inform the report only after the accountable benefit owner — a business manager, not the project manager — has validated, explained and owned it; attestation is not delegable.

## 17. Sustainability in Project Delivery

### What sustainability means in project delivery

Sustainability, as the Institute uses the term, derives from the definition of sustainable development popularised by the Brundtland Commission's 1987 report *Our Common Future*: meeting the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own. Applied to project delivery, the concept operates at two levels that the candidate must keep distinct:

- **Sustainability *of* the output.** Whether the asset, product, service or change the project delivers is environmentally, socially and economically viable across its whole life — construction or build, operation, maintenance, and eventual decommissioning, disposal or reuse.
- **Sustainability *in* delivery.** Whether the temporary endeavour itself is conducted responsibly — its energy and material consumption, emissions and waste, supply-chain conduct, treatment of workers, and effects on the communities around it.

The distinction matters because of a structural asymmetry: delivery typically lasts months or a few years, while outputs endure for decades. Decisions taken during initiation and design lock in the great majority of an asset's whole-life impacts long before those impacts are felt. Two further terms anchor the chapter. **Whole-life perspective** means appraising an option over its entire life cycle rather than to handover, using techniques such as whole-life costing and life cycle assessment. **Project legacy** means the enduring consequences — beneficial and adverse — that persist after closure: benefits realised, capabilities and infrastructure left behind, environmental residues, and changes to livelihoods and communities.

### The triple bottom line

The **triple bottom line**, a framing introduced by John Elkington in the 1990s, holds that performance must be assessed on three dimensions — **environmental** (planet), **social** (people) and **economic** (prosperity) — rather than on financial results alone. The economic dimension is deliberately wider than project cost: it encompasses whole-life value to the owner and to society, including resilience and avoided future cost. The discipline it imposes is not that every project optimise all three dimensions equally, but that all three are appraised explicitly and trade-offs surfaced for the sponsor to decide, rather than resolved silently in favour of capital cost or schedule.

Two related constructs must not be conflated with it. **ESG** (environmental, social and governance) is the investor-facing disclosure lens through which organisations report; the triple bottom line is the decision lens through which delivery leaders appraise options. **Materiality** determines which sustainability topics warrant measurement and reporting: *financial materiality* asks what affects the organisation's value, while *double materiality* — the basis of current European reporting regulation — also asks what impact the organisation has on people and the environment.

### Governing frameworks and standards

Several published instruments give the field its common vocabulary. The **United Nations Sustainable Development Goals** (the seventeen SDGs of the 2030 Agenda) provide a widely used taxonomy for social and environmental outcomes to which many owners map project benefits. The **Greenhouse Gas Protocol** classifies emissions as Scope 1 (direct), Scope 2 (purchased energy) and Scope 3 (the value chain); on most projects, Scope 3 — embodied in purchased materials and subcontracted work — dominates. Management-system and method standards include **ISO 14001** (environmental management systems), **ISO 20400** (sustainable procurement), **ISO 14040/14044** (life cycle assessment) and **PAS 2080** (carbon management in buildings and infrastructure, with its hierarchy of avoid, switch and improve). On the reporting side sit the **GRI Standards** (impact-oriented disclosure), the **IFRS Sustainability Disclosure Standards** IFRS S1 and S2 issued by the ISSB (investor-oriented), and the EU's **Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive** with its European Sustainability Reporting Standards (double materiality). Profession-specific treatments, such as GPM's **P5 Standard**, extend the triple bottom line to the delivery process and product. These instruments are cited for orientation and study; the PML-AI credential is examination-based, and the Institute claims no accreditation by, or affiliation with, the bodies that publish them.

Together these pieces form a single chain of practice: triple-bottom-line principles supply the *criteria* for options appraisal; the selected option's commitments become *measures* baselined, reported and flowed to suppliers, so that corporate disclosures reconcile to verifiable project-level data rather than assertion; and whole-life and legacy assessment closes the loop after handover.

### A step-by-step method for sustainability-weighted option appraisal

Sustainability becomes real when it enters the option appraisal, the baseline and the contract. The Institute expects candidates to apply a repeatable method, not to append sustainability commentary to a business case.

**Step 1 — Frame the decision with the sponsor.** Agree the environmental, social and economic criteria that matter, the appraisal period (whole life, not the delivery phase alone) and any organisational commitments — a net-zero target, a social-value policy — that constrain the choice.

**Step 2 — Establish whole-life economic cost.** For each option, sum capital, operating, maintenance and end-of-life costs over the appraisal period.

**Step 3 — Quantify environmental impact.** Convert activity data (energy, fuel, materials, waste) into tonnes of carbon-dioxide equivalent (tCO2e) using published emission factors, covering both embodied carbon (construction) and operational carbon (use).

**Step 4 — Monetise what can credibly be monetised.** Apply the organisation's shadow carbon price to the tCO2e figures so carbon competes with cash in the same units. Keep factors that resist credible monetisation — biodiversity, community disruption, local employment — as scored criteria with defined scales and recorded evidence.

**Step 5 — Compare and recommend.** Present the monetised whole-life comparison alongside the scored criteria, state assumptions and sensitivities, and record the recommendation and its rationale in the decision log.

**Step 6 — Validate any AI contribution.** Where an AI tool proposes emission factors, energy forecasts or supplier sustainability ratings, the professional verifies source, vintage and applicability before the numbers enter the appraisal.

### Worked example: whole-life appraisal of two depot designs

A sponsor must choose between two designs for a regional distribution depot, appraised over 20 years. The organisation's shadow carbon price is USD 80 per tCO2e; electricity costs USD 0.14 per kWh; the grid emission factor is 0.40 kgCO2e per kWh. Figures are undiscounted for clarity; in practice, apply the organisation's stated discount rate to future costs and test sensitivity.

| Input | Option A (conventional) | Option B (low-carbon) |
|---|---|---|
| Capital cost | USD 4,200,000 | USD 4,650,000 |
| Annual electricity | 620,000 kWh | 380,000 kWh |
| Embodied carbon | 2,900 tCO2e | 2,450 tCO2e |

**Operational energy cost (20 years).** Option A: 620,000 × 0.14 = USD 86,800 per year; × 20 = USD 1,736,000. Option B: 380,000 × 0.14 = USD 53,200 per year; × 20 = USD 1,064,000.

**Operational carbon.** Option A: 620,000 × 0.40 = 248,000 kg = 248 tCO2e per year; × 20 = 4,960 tCO2e. Option B: 380,000 × 0.40 = 152,000 kg = 152 tCO2e per year; × 20 = 3,040 tCO2e.

**Monetised carbon at USD 80 per tCO2e.** Operational: A = 4,960 × 80 = USD 396,800; B = 3,040 × 80 = USD 243,200. Embodied: A = 2,900 × 80 = USD 232,000; B = 2,450 × 80 = USD 196,000.

**Whole-life comparison.**
Option A: 4,200,000 + 1,736,000 + 396,800 + 232,000 = **USD 6,564,800**.
Option B: 4,650,000 + 1,064,000 + 243,200 + 196,000 = **USD 6,153,200**.

Option B is USD 411,600 (6.3 %) cheaper on a whole-life basis and avoids 7,860 − 5,490 = 2,370 tCO2e. Note the shape of the result: on cash alone, B's extra USD 450,000 of capital buys energy savings of only 86,800 − 53,200 = USD 33,600 a year — a 13.4-year simple payback (450,000 ÷ 33,600) that many sponsors would reject. It is the monetised carbon, together with B's stronger scored social criteria (apprenticeship and local-labour commitments), that carries the recommendation. This is precisely why criteria, carbon price and appraisal period must be agreed with the sponsor *before* scoring, never fitted afterwards to a preferred answer.

### Embedding the measures in baselines, reporting and the supply chain

An approved appraisal becomes delivery discipline in three moves. First, **baseline the measures**: Option B's 2,450 tCO2e embodied-carbon figure becomes a carbon budget alongside the cost baseline, decomposed to work packages and placed under change control — a design change that adds carbon is a change, not a footnote. Second, **report against them**: the routine performance report shows carbon and agreed social measures (for example, local-labour hours) as planned versus actual, with variance and forecast at completion, on the same cadence as cost and schedule. Third, **flow them to suppliers**: tender evaluation carries a stated sustainability weighting; contracts carry the KPIs and — critically — an obligation to supply the underlying data (material quantities, delivery mileage, metered energy) in a defined format, without which no KPI is verifiable. At handover, the whole-life measures transfer to the benefits and legacy register with a named operational owner, so the 20-year case remains accountable after the project closes.

### Common pitfalls

- **Capital-only appraisal.** The cheapest option to build is often the dearest to run and to decommission.
- **Double-counting.** Monetising carbon and then scoring it again as a weighted criterion.
- **Unstated assumptions.** Emission factors, carbon price and appraisal period left implicit, making the appraisal unrepeatable and unauditable.
- **Discount-rate abuse.** A high rate quietly extinguishes long-term benefits; state it, justify it and sensitivity-test it.
- **Measures without baselines.** Objectives never baselined or reported — brochure, not baseline, and a greenwashing risk.
- **KPIs without data clauses.** Supplier targets with no contractual right to the evidence needed to verify them.
- **Unvalidated AI inputs.** AI-proposed emission factors, forecasts or supplier ratings adopted without a competent human confirming source, vintage and applicability.
- **Orphaned legacy.** Closing the project with no named owner for whole-life measures, so the benefits case evaporates at handover.

### How AI changes the sustainability competency

Sustainability has historically suffered from a data problem: environmental and social impacts are diffuse, accumulate across long supply chains and asset lifetimes, and are recorded — if at all — in systems never designed for project reporting. AI and predictive tooling change this in three practical ways. First, **estimation**: machine-learning models linked to design data, materials libraries and emissions-factor databases can generate carbon, energy, water and waste estimates for competing options far earlier in the life cycle than manual assessment allows — when the leader's influence is greatest. Second, **monitoring**: analytics platforms can ingest metered consumption, logistics records and site data to track sustainability performance continuously against the baseline, flagging variances in the same cadence as cost and schedule. Third, **disclosure support**: language models can screen supplier declarations, certifications and sustainability reports at a scale no project team could match, and can draft the project's own sustainability reporting.

None of this changes what the competency *is*. The triple-bottom-line trade-offs remain judgements, and AI's contribution is to make those judgements better informed, faster and more consistently evidenced.

### What the leader automates — and what the leader validates

The delivery leader may sensibly automate data aggregation, emissions-factor lookups, option-comparison calculations, supplier-document screening and the first draft of routine sustainability reporting. The leader must personally validate: the system boundary and scope of any footprint calculation; the emission factors and assumptions applied; any AI-derived ranking used in a recommendation to the sponsor; any social-impact conclusion, which rests on stakeholder evidence no model can substitute; and every sustainability claim that leaves the project. Whole-life and legacy assessments deserve particular scrutiny: their outputs are dominated by assumptions (asset life, usage profiles, discount treatment) the model inherits rather than tests.

### Model risk, data quality and human accountability

Sustainability data is among the weakest an AI system will encounter on a project. Scope 3 and supply-chain figures are frequently estimated, self-reported or missing; emission factors vary by region and vintage; third-party ESG ratings of the same supplier can disagree materially. Models trained on such data can present fragile numbers with unwarranted precision, and generative tools can fabricate plausible figures, factors or references outright. Bias is a live risk: datasets over-represent large, well-documented suppliers and under-represent local and social impacts, skewing comparisons against exactly the options the appraisal exists to surface. The competent leader therefore demands provenance for every input, an uncertainty range on every estimate, and reproducibility for any figure that enters a baseline or report.

Sustainability disclosure is increasingly regulated, and overstated environmental claims — greenwashing — carry legal, commercial and reputational consequences. Accountability for them cannot be delegated to a tool or its vendor. Every AI-assisted sustainability figure used in governance must be explainable to the sponsor and to an auditor, validated by a competent person, and formally owned by a named individual — normally the project management leader. "The model calculated it" is not a defence the Institute recognises, in the examination or in practice.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI can estimate a footprint, rank the options and draft the disclosure — but it cannot weigh an environmental gain against a social cost, and it cannot answer for a claim. The delivery leader who signs the recommendation, the baseline and the sustainability report owns every number in them, and must be able to explain, evidence and defend each one without reference to the tool that produced it.

### Key points

- Sustainability operates at two distinct levels — sustainability *of* the output across its whole life, and sustainability *in* the conduct of delivery — and early design decisions lock in most whole-life impacts.
- The triple bottom line (environmental, social, economic) is a decision lens for appraising options; ESG is a disclosure lens; double materiality adds the organisation's impact on people and planet to financial materiality.
- Scope 3 (value-chain) emissions dominate most project footprints; ISO 14001, ISO 20400, ISO 14040/14044 and PAS 2080 supply method, while GRI, IFRS S1/S2 and CSRD govern disclosure.
- Appraise options with sponsor-agreed criteria fixed in advance: cost the whole life, quantify carbon, monetise credibly at the shadow carbon price, score the rest, and state every assumption.
- Approved measures must be baselined under change control, reported on the same cadence as cost and schedule, and flowed to suppliers with contractual rights to the underlying data.
- AI accelerates estimation, monitoring and disclosure drafting, but sustainability data is weak and bias-prone: every AI figure needs provenance, an uncertainty range and reproducibility before it enters governance.
- Accountability for sustainability claims rests with a named professional, never with a tool: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Practice questions

**1.** A low-carbon option carries USD 450,000 more capital cost and a 13.4-year simple payback on energy savings alone, yet is correctly recommended. Which feature of the appraisal most directly justifies this?

A. Applying a lower discount rate to the low-carbon option only
B. Monetising carbon at the organisation's shadow price within the agreed whole-life comparison
C. Adjusting the criteria weightings after the results were known
D. Excluding embodied carbon so that operational savings dominate

**Correct answer: B.** Pricing carbon into the whole-life comparison, on criteria agreed with the sponsor before scoring, is what legitimately overturns the capital-only view; A, C and D are appraisal abuses.

**2.** Under the Greenhouse Gas Protocol, the emissions embodied in a project's purchased materials and subcontracted work are classified as:

A. Scope 1, because the project directly causes them
B. Scope 2, because they are procured
C. Scope 3, because they arise in the value chain
D. Outside the project boundary and excluded from its footprint

**Correct answer: C.** Value-chain emissions are Scope 3 and typically dominate a project's footprint — hence the contractual data obligations on suppliers.

**3.** An AI tool supplied the emission factors behind a project sustainability report that an auditor later challenges. Under Institute doctrine, accountability for the figures rests with:

A. The tool vendor, under the software licence
B. The named professional who validated and owns the figures
C. No one, provided the tool's methodology was published
D. The auditor, once the report has been accepted

**Correct answer: B.** AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable — every AI-assisted figure in governance must be explainable, validated and owned by a competent human, and "the model calculated it" is no defence.

## Chapter 18: Project Closure

### The nature and purpose of closure

Project closure is the final, governed phase of the project life cycle, in which the project is formally concluded: deliverables are accepted, obligations are discharged, resources are released, records are completed and archived, and the learning generated by the work is captured for the organisation. Two principles define the concept precisely. First, closure is a *process*, not an event — a structured sequence of verifications and authorisations, typically executed against a closure checklist. Second, projects end by *decision*, not by attrition. A project that simply fades away, with staff drifting to other work while contracts, cost accounts and access rights remain open, exposes the organisation to unmanaged commercial, financial and security risk. Formal closure completes the accountability loop opened at initiation: the sponsor who authorised the investment against a business case now confirms how it has been concluded.

### Planned closure and premature termination

Closure takes two forms. **Planned closure** occurs when the project has delivered its agreed scope and its outputs have been accepted. **Premature termination** occurs when governance decides, before completion, that continued investment is no longer justified — because the business case has become invalid, funding or strategic priorities have changed, a key party has failed, or a technical obstacle has proved unrecoverable. This Body of Knowledge treats termination as a legitimate governance outcome, not as managerial failure: continuing an unjustifiable project in order to avoid acknowledging sunk costs destroys more value than stopping it.

A *controlled* termination has three defining obligations: protect the value already created (part-finished deliverables, data, intellectual property, reusable designs); honour contractual, legal and ethical commitments to suppliers, staff and other stakeholders; and document the rationale for the decision so that it is auditable and instructive. Both forms of closure use substantially the same close-out machinery — termination compresses it and adds negotiation of early-exit terms.

### Administrative, contractual and financial close-out

Close-out comprises three interlocking strands. **Administrative close-out** completes and archives the project record, releases people, facilities and equipment in a planned manner, revokes system access, and formally disbands the temporary organisation. **Contractual close-out** verifies that every obligation under each contract has been discharged or formally settled: final claims and variations resolved, warranties and defect-liability arrangements activated, retentions released on the agreed terms, and closure confirmed in writing with each party. **Financial close-out** reconciles final cost against the approved baseline, clears outstanding commitments and accruals, closes the cost accounts so that no further expenditure can be booked to the project, and produces the final financial report. The strands are sequenced, not parallel: financial figures cannot be final until contractual liabilities are settled, and the administrative archive is complete only when it contains the evidence of both. Executing a closure checklist — this chapter's learning objective — rests on understanding these dependencies.
