### Handover, acceptance and transition to operations

**Acceptance** is the formal confirmation, by the authorised acceptor, that a deliverable meets its pre-agreed acceptance criteria. It is distinct from mere delivery and is the pivotal control of closure, since it triggers contractual settlement, warranty commencement and transfer of ownership. **Handover** is the transfer of the accepted deliverable to its operational owner, together with everything required to operate and sustain it: documentation, training, support arrangements and known-issue registers. **Transition** covers the managed period that follows — hypercare or early-life support — and, critically, the transfer of the benefits realisation plan to a named business owner, because most benefits accrue after the project has closed. Where deliverables include AI-enabled components, handover must additionally transfer model documentation, monitoring and retraining responsibilities, and the named human ownership that must survive the project team's departure.

### Executing the closure checklist

Closure is executed, not declared. The delivery leader works through a controlled sequence and evidences each step.

1. **Confirm completion of scope.** Reconcile the deliverable register against the baselined scope statement; every deliverable is either accepted, formally descoped through change control, or listed as an agreed exclusion.
2. **Obtain formal acceptance.** Test each deliverable against its pre-agreed acceptance criteria and secure written sign-off from the authorised acceptor — never rely on implied acceptance through use.
3. **Close the contracts.** Verify that all supplier obligations are discharged; agree final accounts; resolve or formally register outstanding claims; confirm warranty terms, defect-liability arrangements and retention release dates.
4. **Close the finances.** Process final invoices and accruals, close cost codes and control accounts to prevent late charges, reconcile the final cost outturn against the approved budget, and return unused contingency.
5. **Release resources deliberately.** Plan a staged demobilisation, complete performance feedback for team members, and transfer key-person knowledge before departure, not after.
6. **Archive and report.** Index the project records for retention, issue the closure report with final performance figures, and formally stand down the governance bodies.

Where AI drafts the closure report or reconciles records, its output is a proposal only: the delivery leader validates every figure and owns the submission.

### Managing a controlled premature termination

A premature termination is run as a project in its own right, with the same discipline as planned closure plus additional steps.

1. **Revalidate the business case** with current forecasts of cost to complete and expected benefits.
2. **Frame the decision on forward-looking figures only.** Costs already incurred are sunk in every option and must be excluded from the comparison.
3. **Present options to the sponsor or board** — complete, descope, pause or terminate — and record the rationale, the figures relied upon and the decision-maker in the decision log.
4. **Protect value.** Secure completed deliverables, intellectual property, data and licences that other parts of the organisation can reuse.
5. **Honour obligations.** Apply contractual termination clauses correctly, give proper notice, settle non-cancellable commitments and negotiate cancellation charges.
6. **Then execute the full closure checklist above,** including lessons learned — terminated projects are the richest source of them.

### Worked example: the termination decision

**Setup.** A systems project has a Budget at Completion (`BAC`) of USD 2,400,000. At the review gate, Earned Value (`EV`) is USD 1,080,000 and Actual Cost (`AC`) is USD 1,200,000. Benefits have been revalidated at USD 1,300,000 (down from USD 3,000,000 after a market change). Outstanding purchase orders total USD 300,000: USD 120,000 non-cancellable, USD 180,000 cancellable at a 10% charge. Demobilisation and close-out would cost USD 60,000; reusable design assets and licences are valued at USD 90,000.

**Formulae.** `CPI = EV ÷ AC`; `EAC = BAC ÷ CPI`; `ETC = EAC − AC`. Each option is compared on future cash flows only.

**Substitution and arithmetic.**

- `CPI = 1,080,000 ÷ 1,200,000 = 0.90`
- `EAC = 2,400,000 ÷ 0.90 = 2,666,667`
- `ETC = 2,666,667 − 1,200,000 = 1,466,667`
- **Option A — complete:** future benefit 1,300,000 − future cost 1,466,667 = **(166,667)**
- **Option B — terminate:** future cost = 120,000 + (10% × 180,000) + 60,000 = 120,000 + 18,000 + 60,000 = 198,000; less recovered value 90,000 = net **(108,000)**

**Result.** Termination is the better forward-looking outcome by 166,667 − 108,000 = **USD 58,667**.

**Interpretation.** Both options lose money, but the USD 1,200,000 already spent is sunk and irrelevant to the choice. The organisation should terminate, protect the USD 90,000 of reusable value, settle the USD 198,000 of obligations honourably, and record this rationale so the decision is defensible to audit and governance.

### Lessons learned and knowledge retention

A **lesson identified** is an observation about what helped or hindered performance; a lesson is **learned** only when it is embedded in changed organisational practice — an updated method, template, estimating dataset or behaviour. The lessons-learned review is therefore a structured, no-blame, evidence-based examination of the project record, and its output is a set of owned improvement actions, not a filed report. In practice:

1. **Prepare with evidence.** Assemble variance analyses, the risk register, the decision log and stakeholder feedback before the session, so discussion rests on data rather than recollection.
2. **Facilitate without blame.** Work through what was planned, what actually happened, why it differed, and what should be repeated or changed; attribute causes to process, not persons.
3. **Convert findings into actions.** Each lesson becomes a specific improvement action with a named owner, a deadline and a target artefact — an estimating norm, a checklist item, a template or a training change. A lesson without an owner is an anecdote.
4. **Retain the knowledge.** File lessons in a searchable organisational repository, tagged by theme, and route systemic items to the PMO or process owners for verification that the change was made.

**Knowledge retention** is the organisational discipline that converts these actions into durable assets. AI tools can mine project records, change logs and review narratives to cluster recurring patterns across an entire portfolio, converting anecdote into organisational evidence — but every proposed lesson must be explainable, validated against the evidence and tested with the people who lived the project, and adopted or rejected by a competent professional. A fluent summary of the wrong lesson is worse than no lesson at all.

### AI and predictive tooling in closure

Closure has historically been the most neglected and least instrumented phase of the life cycle; AI-enabled delivery environments change that. Predictive completion analytics can forecast the true end date of the punch list, flag deliverables at risk of failing acceptance, and estimate the cost-to-close with a discipline that manual trackers rarely achieve. Natural-language tooling can read the contract suite and extract outstanding obligations — warranties, retentions, licences, insurances, data-return clauses — into a close-out register, and can reconcile final accounts against commitment records to surface unmatched purchase orders, unreturned assets and dormant access rights that a human review would take weeks to find. For premature termination, portfolio analytics can provide early, dispassionate evidence that a benefits case has collapsed — often before political momentum allows anyone to say so aloud.

The certified professional therefore automates the mechanical substrate of closure: assembly and indexing of the documentation set, reconciliation of financial and commitment data, tracing of open contractual obligations, first drafts of close-out reports and lessons summaries, and archiving with searchable metadata. What is never automated is the judgement layer. Acceptance is a human decision made against agreed criteria, not a model score. The recommendation to terminate may originate in an algorithm; the decision, its rationale and its documentation belong to the sponsor and the delivery leader, who must be able to explain it without reference to an opaque output. Final accounts, claims positions, release of retentions and the closure certificate itself carry contractual and financial consequence and require validated human sign-off.

Closure-stage models also carry distinctive weaknesses that the examination expects candidates to recognise. Training data suffers from survivorship bias: organisations archive completed projects far more faithfully than terminated ones, so models systematically understate the signals that precede failure and can lend false comfort to a project that should be stopped. Cost-to-close forecasts degrade precisely when they matter most, because late-stage data is sparse, disputed and politically shaped. Obligation-extraction tools trained on standard forms can miss bespoke or negotiated clauses — and a missed clause at close-out is a liability, not an inconvenience. Generative summarisation can introduce plausible but unfounded statements into formal records; nothing machine-drafted enters a contractual close-out document, termination rationale or lessons register unverified. Finally, closure concentrates sensitive material — settlements, personal data, dispute correspondence — so feeding it into analytical tooling without data-governance controls creates a confidentiality exposure just as oversight winds down.

Accountability does not close when the project does. A named, competent individual must own every accepted deliverable, every financial settlement, every retained record and — above all — any decision to terminate. Where AI analysis contributed to that decision, its inputs, assumptions and limitations are documented in the closure record so that an auditor or a successor can reconstruct the reasoning years later. The delivery leader who signs the closure certificate is attesting to the whole of it, including every machine-produced element within it.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** In closure, AI may forecast the cost-to-close, extract the obligations, draft the report and cluster the lessons — but acceptance is granted, termination is decided, accounts are settled and lessons are declared learned only by an accountable human who can explain and defend each judgement. The algorithm surfaces the evidence; the professional closes the project.

### Governing frameworks

ISO 21502 addresses closing or terminating a project or phase as an integrated management practice; the PMBOK Guide treats closure within its project performance and life-cycle guidance; PRINCE2 defines a dedicated Closing a Project process with explicit acceptance and handover products; and the APM Body of Knowledge situates closure within governance and benefits management. Across all of them the architecture is the same, and acceptance is its hinge: acceptance triggers contractual and financial settlement; settlement enables administrative completion; handover carries the deliverable and the benefits plan into operations; and the lessons-learned review converts the whole experience into organisational capability. Closure done well is how a temporary endeavour leaves a permanent asset.

### Common pitfalls

- Treating handover to operations as acceptance — formal sign-off against agreed criteria is still required.
- Including sunk costs in a termination decision, biasing the board towards continuing ("we've spent too much to stop").
- Leaving cost codes open after closure, allowing unrelated charges to distort the final outturn.
- Releasing key people before knowledge transfer, so the transition to operations fails quietly later.
- Terminating abruptly without honouring notice periods and settlement clauses, converting a controlled exit into a dispute.
- Recording lessons "identified" but never assigning owners, so nothing is actually learned — and skipping the lessons review on terminated projects, precisely where the organisation has most to learn.
- Accepting an AI-drafted closure report or lessons summary unvalidated; every figure and finding must be explainable and owned by the accountable professional.

### Key points

- Closure is a governed process, not an event: projects end by decision, and the sponsor confirms how the investment concluded.
- Planned closure and premature termination use the same close-out machinery; a controlled termination additionally protects value created, honours obligations and documents its rationale.
- Close-out is sequenced: contractual settlement precedes final financial figures, and the administrative archive must evidence both.
- Formal acceptance against pre-agreed criteria is the pivotal control — it triggers settlement, warranty commencement and transfer of ownership, and is never implied through use.
- Termination decisions compare forward-looking cash flows only; sunk costs are excluded from every option.
- A lesson is learned only when embedded in changed practice, with a named owner, a deadline and a target artefact.
- AI may forecast, extract, reconcile and draft throughout closure, but acceptance, termination, settlement and lessons remain explainable, validated human decisions — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

### Practice questions

**1.** A project has a Budget at Completion of USD 2,400,000. At the closure review gate, Earned Value is USD 1,080,000 and Actual Cost is USD 1,200,000. Using performance-based forecasting, what is the Estimate at Completion?

A. USD 2,400,000
B. USD 2,666,667
C. USD 2,160,000
D. USD 1,466,667

**Answer: B.** CPI = 1,080,000 ÷ 1,200,000 = 0.90, so EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 2,400,000 ÷ 0.90 = USD 2,666,667; option D is the ETC, not the EAC.

**2.** When a board evaluates whether to terminate a project early, which figure must be excluded from the comparison of options?

A. The estimated cost to complete the remaining work
B. Cancellation charges on cancellable purchase orders
C. Costs already incurred on the project to date
D. The recoverable value of reusable assets and licences

**Answer: C.** Costs already incurred are sunk in every option and cannot be changed by the decision; only forward-looking cash flows are compared.

**3.** A closure-stage AI model trained predominantly on the organisation's archived completed projects is used to advise on whether a struggling project should be stopped. What is the most significant risk?

A. The model will overstate the cost of demobilisation
B. Survivorship bias understates the signals that precede failure, lending false comfort to a project that should stop
C. The model will automatically breach contract confidentiality clauses
D. The forecasts will be too pessimistic because terminated projects dominate the data

**Answer: B.** Completed projects are archived far more faithfully than terminated ones, so the training data under-represents failure precursors and biases the model towards recommending continuation.

## 19. AI-Enabled Project Management

### What AI-enabled project management is — and is not

AI-enabled project management is the disciplined use of artificial intelligence systems to support the work of initiating, planning, executing, monitoring and closing projects. The term covers a spectrum of capability, and precision matters: each class carries a different failure mode and therefore a different governance obligation. **Descriptive analytics** summarises what has happened in delivery data. **Predictive analytics and machine learning** infer patterns from historical data to produce forecasts, estimates or classifications — a completion-date probability, a cost-at-completion range, a risk likelihood score. **Generative AI**, most commonly delivered through large language models, produces drafts: schedules, risk registers, status narratives, stakeholder communications, lessons-learned syntheses. What none of these systems does is decide. However capable, an AI system produces a *proposal* — an output whose quality depends on the data it learned from, the prompt or parameters it received, and the fit between its training and the project's actual context.

Two boundaries define the discipline. First, AI-enabled project management is *assistive*, not autonomous: accountability for every plan, baseline, forecast, report and decision remains with a named, competent professional, exactly as it does when the supporting tool is a spreadsheet or a scheduling engine. Second, it is *evidence-bound*: an AI output enters project use only after it has been tested against independent evidence — never because it is fluent, fast or confident.

### Core concepts: outputs, explainability, validation and ownership

Four concepts recur throughout this chapter and are defined here once. An **AI output** is any artefact or judgement produced wholly or partly by an AI system and considered for use in delivery — a draft plan, an estimate, a forecast, a risk score, a summarised report, a recommended action. **Explainability** is the ability of the accountable professional to state, in terms a sponsor, governance board or auditor can follow, what the output is, what data and assumptions it rests on, and why it is credible; an output the owner cannot explain is not usable, whatever its apparent quality. **Validation** is the act of checking an AI output against independent evidence before adoption — reference-class data, parametric benchmarks, expert judgement, first-principles calculation or back-testing against known outcomes — and it must precede, never follow, incorporation into a baseline. **Ownership** is the assignment of each adopted output to a competent human who answers for it in governance forums and who carries the authority to reject it: the owner signs for the output as if they had produced it themselves, because in governance terms they have.

### The governing doctrine: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable

The PCI doctrine that runs through this Body of Knowledge applies here in its strongest form.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI may draft the plan, sharpen the estimate and sense the risk — but only a competent, named professional may commit a baseline, sign a report or accept a risk response. Every AI output used in delivery must be explainable, validated against independent evidence, and owned by the human who answers for it.

The doctrine is not a restraint on the use of AI; it is the condition that makes its use professionally defensible. It converts a probabilistic tool into a governable one by inserting the same control that project management has always applied to expert input from any source: independent scrutiny before reliance. A forecast from a model is treated exactly as a forecast from a consultant would be — interrogated for its basis, tested against other evidence, and adopted only under a named owner. In practice the doctrine imposes three tests on every AI output, corresponding to the concepts defined above: explainable, validated proportionately to its consequence, and owned. An output failing any test is advisory material at most. Accountability for scope, schedule, cost, risk and benefits cannot be delegated to a model, a tool or a vendor; a governance body should be able to trace every AI-influenced decision to the professional who disposed of it.

### Frameworks and standards context

No single standard yet codifies AI-enabled project management, so the professional draws on two converging bodies of guidance. From the project management side, established references — ISO 21502's guidance on project management, the PMBOK's treatment of tailoring, estimating and performance measurement, and ISO 31000 on risk management — supply the control framework into which AI outputs must fit: baselines are version-controlled, changes are governed, forecasts are evidenced, risks are owned. From the AI governance side, emerging instruments — ISO/IEC 42001 on AI management systems, ISO/IEC 23894 on AI risk management, the NIST AI Risk Management Framework, and risk-tiered regulation such as the EU AI Act — establish organisational expectations for transparency, human oversight, data governance and accountability. The delivery leader is rarely the author of the organisation's AI policy, but is always accountable for complying with it within the project. This chapter describes these frameworks generically, as context for professional judgement; the PCI credential is examination-based, and PCI claims no accreditation against, or endorsement by, any of the bodies named. Equally, this Body of Knowledge discusses tool *categories* — scheduling assistants, estimating models, risk-sensing analytics, generative drafting tools — never particular vendors.

### AI across the life cycle: use cases and triage

Artificial intelligence now touches every stage of the project life cycle. At initiation, natural-language tools summarise business cases, draft charters and surface comparable historical projects. In planning, machine-learning models propose work breakdown structures, dependency networks and reference-class estimates. During execution, predictive analytics flag schedule slippage, cost variance and emerging risk clusters before they appear in conventional reports; at closure, AI accelerates lessons-learned synthesis and benefits tracking. The delivery leader's first competence is therefore triage: identifying which activities genuinely benefit from AI support and appraising the value, limitations and risks of each use case. Pattern-rich, data-heavy, repetitive tasks — status aggregation, document drafting, anomaly detection — are strong candidates. Judgement-heavy tasks — stakeholder negotiation, trade-off decisions, benefits ownership — are not. The examination expects candidates to make this distinction explicitly, use case by use case, rather than treating "AI adoption" as a single decision.

### Appraising an AI use case: the five-step method

Before any AI capability enters the delivery environment, the delivery leader works through five steps. The sequence is examinable and should be applied exactly as given.

1. **Inventory candidate activities** across the life cycle — estimating, schedule development, risk identification, progress narrative drafting, forecasting, benefits tracking — and state, for each, the decision the AI output would inform.
2. **Appraise value against limitation.** For each candidate, record the expected value (time saved, wider option sets, earlier warning) alongside the specific limitations and risks: data quality, bias in the historical record, hallucination, confidentiality exposure and erosion of accountability.
3. **Confirm the data foundation.** An estimating or forecasting model is only as good as the organisation's project history; sparse, unrepresentative or uncleansed data disqualifies the use case until remediated.
4. **Assign ownership and explainability up front.** Name a competent human owner for every output category and set an explainability requirement proportionate to the decision at stake — a forecast feeding a governance gate demands a fuller account of drivers than a draft meeting summary.
5. **Pilot in parallel.** Run the AI method alongside the established method for a defined period; only demonstrated, documented performance earns the tool a place in live delivery.

### AI support for planning, estimating and forecasting

AI-generated plans and estimates are proposals, not baselines. Models trained on historical delivery data can counter optimism bias, generate probabilistic ranges rather than single-point estimates, and expose assumptions human planners leave tacit. But they inherit the defects of their training data: unrepresentative project histories, survivorship bias, and silent changes in delivery context that make the past a poor guide. Where a model cannot explain which factors drove its output, the output cannot be defended before a sponsor or steering committee, and it must not become a commitment. The rule is simple: automate the generation of options; never automate the acceptance of a baseline.

### Validating an AI output before adoption: the standing routine

When an AI tool proposes a plan, estimate or forecast, the owner validates it before it touches a baseline.

1. **Obtain the output with its drivers** — the inputs used, the assumptions made and the factors weighted most heavily. An output that cannot be explained cannot be validated.
2. **Verify the inputs** against the project's authoritative records (ledger, schedule status, risk register).
3. **Build an independent cross-check** using an established technique — parametric, three-point or earned-value based — from the same verified inputs.
4. **Reconcile.** Where the AI figure and the independent figure diverge, the divergence is diagnostic: interrogate what the model assumed that the professional would not, or vice versa.
5. **Dispose.** Adopt, adjust or reject; record the proposal, the validation evidence, the decision and the named owner in the decision log.

### Worked example: validating an AI-proposed estimate at completion

**Setup.** A workplace-technology programme has a budget at completion (BAC) of USD 2,400,000. At the end of month 8 the authoritative records show earned value EV = USD 960,000, actual cost AC = USD 1,090,000 and planned value PV = USD 1,120,000. An AI forecasting assistant proposes an estimate at completion (EAC) of USD 2,580,000 and a finish six weeks beyond baseline. The programme manager owns the forecast.

**Step 1 — verify inputs.** EV, AC and PV are reconciled to the cost ledger and schedule status; they agree.

**Step 2 — independent cross-check (earned value).**

- CPI = EV ÷ AC = 960,000 ÷ 1,090,000 = 0.88
- SPI = EV ÷ PV = 960,000 ÷ 1,120,000 = 0.86
- Performance-based EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 2,400,000 × 1,090,000 ÷ 960,000 = **USD 2,725,000** (unrounded CPI used)
- Budget-rate EAC = AC + (BAC − EV) = 1,090,000 + 1,440,000 = **USD 2,530,000**

The defensible bracket is therefore USD 2,530,000–2,725,000.

**Step 3 — reconcile.** The AI's USD 2,580,000 sits inside the bracket but near its optimistic end: it implies the remaining work will run at close to budgeted efficiency. The tool's explanation shows it weighted the last two months heavily, when a supplier delay was easing. The programme manager tests that assumption against independent evidence — the revised supplier schedule confirms only partial recovery. The six-week slip is separately checked against a critical-path reforecast rather than SPI alone, since SPI loses reliability late in a project.

**Step 4 — dispose.** The point figure is rejected. The reported forecast is EAC = USD 2,725,000, giving VAC = BAC − EAC = 2,400,000 − 2,725,000 = **(USD 325,000)**, a projected overrun of 13.5% of BAC, with the USD 2,530,000–2,725,000 range and the rationale disclosed to the board.

**Interpretation.** The AI added genuine value — it assembled the data and surfaced the schedule signal early — but its optimism was visible only because an independent cross-check existed. AI proposed; the professional disposed.

### AI support for monitoring, reporting and risk sensing

In execution, AI shifts the leader's posture from lagging to leading indicators. Predictive tooling can scan schedule networks, cost actuals, change logs and even communication metadata to sense risk earlier than periodic reporting cycles allow. The governance caveats are real. False positives erode attention; false negatives breed complacency; and models degrade as the project's context drifts from the data on which they were trained. Model risk must therefore be managed like any other delivery risk: known limitations recorded, performance monitored against outcomes, thresholds for human escalation defined, and data quality — completeness, timeliness, provenance — treated as a precondition rather than an afterthought. AI-drafted status reports become the reporting owner's words the moment they are issued: the professional who signs the report is accountable for every figure and every narrative claim in it, however it was drafted.

### Common pitfalls

- **Automation bias.** Adopting an output because it is fluent and precise-looking; confidence of presentation is not evidence of accuracy.
- **Baseline contamination.** Letting an unvalidated AI figure into the performance measurement baseline, after which every variance is measured against a fiction.
- **Validate-once syndrome.** Data drifts and models are updated; revalidation is required at defined intervals and on every material change.
- **Orphaned outputs.** No named owner — "the tool produced it" is never an acceptable answer to a governance board or an auditor.
- **Confidentiality leakage.** Feeding commercially sensitive or personal project data into tools outside the organisation's approved environment.
- **Unfit history.** Applying AI where the underlying record is sparse or biased and expecting the tool to compensate; it will instead reproduce the defect.
- **Cherry-picking.** Quoting whichever of several AI figures best supports the message the reporter wishes to send.
- **Disproportionate explainability — in either direction.** Explainability is scaled to the consequence of the decision, but it is never absent.

### Key points

- AI-enabled project management is assistive and evidence-bound: descriptive, predictive and generative capabilities produce proposals; none of them decides.
- The governing doctrine is "AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable": every AI output used in delivery must be explainable, validated against independent evidence, and owned by a named, competent human.
- Appraise use cases one by one with the five-step method: inventory, value-versus-limitation appraisal, data-foundation check, up-front ownership and explainability, and a parallel pilot.
- No AI-generated plan, estimate or forecast enters a baseline until an independent cross-check (parametric, three-point or earned-value based) has been built, reconciled and recorded with a named owner.
- Divergence between the AI figure and the independent figure is diagnostic, not a nuisance: it exposes assumptions that must be tested against evidence before disposal.
- In monitoring and risk sensing, AI enables leading indicators, but model risk is managed like any delivery risk — limitations recorded, performance tracked, escalation thresholds defined, data quality assured.
- Accountability is never delegable: the professional who commits a baseline, signs a report or accepts a risk answers for it, however it was drafted.

### Practice questions

**1.** A project has BAC = USD 1,200,000. At the status date, EV = USD 480,000 and AC = USD 600,000. An AI assistant proposes an EAC of USD 1,250,000. Using the cost-performance-index method, what independent cross-check figure should the owner build before disposing of the proposal?

A. USD 1,200,000
B. USD 1,320,000
C. USD 1,500,000
D. USD 1,680,000

**Correct answer: C.** CPI = 480,000 ÷ 600,000 = 0.80, so EAC = BAC ÷ CPI = 1,200,000 ÷ 0.80 = USD 1,500,000, exposing the AI proposal as materially optimistic; B is the budget-rate formula, not the CPI method asked for.

**2.** An owner's earned-value cross-check yields an EAC of USD 2,700,000; the AI tool proposes USD 2,550,000. What is the professionally correct next step?

A. Adopt the AI figure, because the model has processed more data than the owner can
B. Adopt the cross-check figure automatically, because human calculations always override machine output
C. Report the average of the two figures to the board as a balanced compromise
D. Treat the divergence as diagnostic and interrogate the differing assumptions against independent evidence before disposing

**Correct answer: D.** Divergence is information, not a tie to be split or a contest to be pre-judged; the owner tests the competing assumptions against evidence and then adopts, adjusts or rejects on the record.

**3.** A generative AI tool drafts the monthly status report and the project manager issues it unchanged. A material figure in the report later proves wrong. Who is accountable to the governance board?

A. The tool vendor, since the model generated the erroneous figure
B. The organisation's AI governance function, since it approved the tool
C. The project manager who issued the report
D. No one, because the error originated in an automated system

**Correct answer: C.** An AI-drafted report becomes the signing owner's words on issue; "the tool produced it" is never an acceptable answer, so accountability rests with the professional who disposed.

## 20. Decision Intelligence

Every stage of the delivery life cycle — the business case, the gate review, the baseline change, the supplier award, the decision to stop — converges on a moment of choice. Decision intelligence is the discipline of improving the quality of project decisions by combining structured decision-making methods, an evidence-based understanding of human judgement and its biases, and the governed use of data, analytics and artificial intelligence — while keeping a named, competent human accountable for every decision taken.

### What decision intelligence means

A **decision**, for the purposes of this Body of Knowledge, is a deliberate commitment of resources or direction that is costly to reverse: approving a business case, passing a gate, selecting a supplier, rebaselining a schedule, or stopping a workstream. The Institute distinguishes **decision quality** from **outcome quality**. Under uncertainty, a well-made decision can produce a poor outcome, and a careless decision can be rescued by luck. The examination therefore tests the candidate's command of decision *process* — framing, options, criteria, evidence, accountability and record — because process is what a professional can control at the moment of choice.

Decision intelligence sits inside the wider governance frameworks of this Body of Knowledge. ISO 31000 positions risk management as an input to decision-making, requiring decisions to reflect the best available information and its limitations. Organisational governance assigns **decision rights** — delegated authority levels, gate authorities and a single accountable decision-maker for each decision, however many parties are consulted.

### Decision-making models

Three families of model frame the chapter. **Normative (prescriptive) models** describe how an ideally rational actor should choose: expected-value reasoning, decision trees and multi-criteria decision analysis (MCDA), in which options are scored against weighted criteria. **Descriptive models** describe how people actually choose. Herbert Simon's **bounded rationality** holds that decision-makers satisfice — accept the first adequate option — because time, information and cognitive capacity are limited; the heuristics-and-biases programme of Tversky and Kahneman shows that intuitive judgement relies on mental shortcuts that are efficient but systematically error-prone. **Naturalistic models**, notably Gary Klein's recognition-primed decision model, explain how experienced practitioners decide rapidly by pattern-matching a situation to prior cases — powerful under time pressure, unreliable in genuinely novel conditions.

No family is "correct". The competent project management leader selects the approach the situation warrants — high-stakes, hard-to-reverse decisions justify formal structure; routine, reversible ones justify delegated, faster paths — deliberately matching method to stakes, reversibility and available time.

### Cognitive bias and debiasing

A **cognitive bias** is a systematic, predictable deviation of judgement from the standard a normative model would set; it differs from **noise**, which is random variability between judgements. The biases most consequential in project delivery are: optimism bias and the planning fallacy (underestimating cost and duration); anchoring (over-weighting an initial figure); confirmation bias (seeking evidence that supports the preferred option); availability (over-weighting vivid or recent events); sunk-cost reasoning and escalation of commitment (persisting because of past investment); groupthink; and strategic misrepresentation (deliberate optimism to win approval).

**Debiasing techniques** are countermeasures applied to estimates, forecasts and gate decisions: reference-class forecasting, which takes the "outside view" from the actual outcomes of comparable past projects and is recognised in public-sector appraisal guidance such as HM Treasury's Green Book treatment of optimism bias; the premortem, which imagines the decision has failed and works backwards to causes; independent estimate challenge and structured devil's-advocacy; calibration of estimators against realised results; and eliciting judgements independently before group discussion.

### Structuring a decision: the seven-step method

The Institute expects candidates to be able to run a structured decision process from a standing start. The steps are:

1. **Frame the question.** State the decision in one sentence, including what is *not* being decided, the decision deadline, and the accountable decision-maker. A mis-framed question invalidates everything downstream: a poor frame ("should we crash the schedule?") presupposes an option, whereas a sound frame asks "how do we recover the committed benefits date within the approved tolerance?"
2. **Generate options.** Produce at least three genuinely distinct options plus the do-nothing (or deferral) baseline. A single option presented for endorsement is not a decision but a ratification. AI assistants may be used to broaden the option set; every AI-generated option must be reviewed for feasibility by the professional.
3. **Set criteria and weights.** Agree criteria — benefit, cost, risk, deliverability, alignment — with the decision-maker *before* scoring, and weight them to sum to 1.00. Weighting before evaluation prevents weights being reverse-engineered to fit a favourite; the weights are a governance artefact expressing the sponsor's priorities, not the analyst's.
4. **Assemble evidence.** Attach a stated source and confidence to every score — a forecast, a benchmark, a consultation record. Unevidenced scores are opinions.
5. **Score and compute.** Score each option against each criterion (a 1–5 scale is standard), multiply by the weights, and sum. The result is a transparent, challengeable comparison rather than an unexplained preference.
6. **Test sensitivity and debias.** Vary the weights and the least-evidenced scores; if the ranking flips within plausible ranges, the decision hinges on that input and more evidence is needed. Apply at least one formal debiasing technique before committing.
7. **Decide, record, and set a review trigger.** The accountable individual decides — the matrix informs, it does not decide — and the record is completed the same day.

### Worked example: recovering a delivery date

A customer-onboarding programme is forecast to miss its contractual go-live by ten weeks. The delivery leader frames the question — "How do we protect the January benefits stream within a £250,000 change budget?" — and tables three options: **A**, crash the critical path with additional suppliers (£180,000); **B**, descope two non-contractual features to a later release (£40,000 rework provision); **C**, negotiate a three-month extension (£0 direct cost, deferred benefits of roughly £310,000).

The sponsor approves four weighted criteria: time to benefit (0.30), whole-life cost (0.25), delivery risk (0.25), stakeholder acceptance (0.20). Scores (1 = poor, 5 = strong) are evidenced by the cost model, a schedule risk analysis, and minuted customer soundings. An AI-supported schedule model proposed the P80 completion dates used in the time scores; the planner validated its logic links and calendars before use, and the model's role is noted in the record.

| Criterion (weight) | A: Crash | B: Descope | C: Extend |
|---|---|---|---|
| Time to benefit (0.30) | 5 | 4 | 1 |
| Whole-life cost (0.25) | 2 | 4 | 3 |
| Delivery risk (0.25) | 3 | 4 | 5 |
| Stakeholder acceptance (0.20) | 4 | 2 | 3 |

The arithmetic:

- **A** = (5 × 0.30) + (2 × 0.25) + (3 × 0.25) + (4 × 0.20) = 1.50 + 0.50 + 0.75 + 0.80 = **3.55**
- **B** = (4 × 0.30) + (4 × 0.25) + (4 × 0.25) + (2 × 0.20) = 1.20 + 1.00 + 1.00 + 0.40 = **3.60**
- **C** = (1 × 0.30) + (3 × 0.25) + (5 × 0.25) + (3 × 0.20) = 0.30 + 0.75 + 1.25 + 0.60 = **2.90**

B leads A by only 0.05, so the leader runs a sensitivity test. Raising stakeholder acceptance to 0.30 and lowering time to benefit to 0.20 gives A = 1.00 + 0.50 + 0.75 + 1.20 = 3.45 against B = 0.80 + 1.00 + 1.00 + 0.60 = 3.40 — the ranking flips. The decision therefore hinges on how much the sponsor truly values acceptance, and on the evidence behind B's acceptance score of 2. The leader commissions a two-day customer consultation, which confirms the descoped features are low-usage; the score is revised to 3, B rises to 3.80, and the sponsor selects Option B. The margin, the flip condition and the consultation evidence all go into the decision record.

Two debiasing techniques were applied before scoring was finalised. First, **reference-class forecasting**: the team's ten-week recovery estimate for Option A was compared with the last six comparable interventions, which overran their recovery plans by a median of 30 per cent; the time score therefore used thirteen weeks, not ten. Second, a **premortem**: the team assumed Option B had failed twelve months on and listed causes, surfacing a contractual notification requirement that became a mitigation action. For gate decisions, add an independent challenger who did not build the analysis, and record their dissent verbatim.

### AI in the decision cycle: automate the mechanics, validate the judgement

Decision intelligence is the competency in which AI's influence on project leadership is most direct, because predictive tooling does not merely inform decisions — it increasingly pre-shapes them. Machine-learning forecasts, anomaly detection, scenario simulation and generative assistants can assemble the evidence base for a gate review in a fraction of the traditional time: reference-class comparisons from historical portfolio data, probabilistic cost and schedule ranges, early-warning signals, and drafted options papers complete with weighted-criteria scoring. Used well, these capabilities counter several of the biases examined above — optimism bias and anchoring in particular — because a model's outside view is indifferent to a sponsor's enthusiasm. Used carelessly, they introduce a bias of their own: **automation bias**, the tendency to accept a machine-generated recommendation because it arrives quantified, formatted and confident.

The delivery leader may legitimately automate the mechanical layers of the decision cycle: gathering and normalising evidence, generating candidate options, running sensitivity analysis across weighted criteria, stress-testing estimates against reference classes, and drafting the decision record itself. What must be validated, never delegated, is the judgement layer: whether the decision has been framed correctly, whether the criteria and their weights reflect the organisation's genuine priorities, whether the option set is complete, and whether the model's evidence is fit for this decision. A leader presented with an AI-scored options matrix should be able to answer three questions before relying on it: what data the model drew upon, why it ranks the options as it does, and under what conditions its reasoning would break down. If no adequate explanation can be obtained, the output may inform exploration, but it must not determine the decision. Management-system thinking for AI, exemplified by ISO/IEC 42001, reinforces the same expectation of documented human oversight.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** AI may frame options, score criteria, forecast outcomes and draft the decision record — but it cannot own a decision. Every model-supported recommendation must be explainable, validated against independent evidence, and disposed of — accepted, adapted or rejected — by a named, competent decision-maker who remains accountable for the outcome.

### Model risk and data caveats

Model-supported decision-making carries risks the professional is expected to recognise. Models trained on historical portfolios inherit the biases of those portfolios: where past estimates were systematically optimistic, an uncorrected model will faithfully reproduce that optimism at scale. Data drawn from dissimilar projects, small samples or poorly governed sources yields confident-looking scores with wide, unstated error bars. Generative assistants can produce fluent options papers containing fabricated or unverifiable supporting claims, and models degrade silently as delivery context drifts away from the conditions on which they were trained. The leader therefore treats every model-supported input as an estimate with provenance — source data, known limitations, validation performed, and the model version and date — rather than as a fact. For significant decisions — a gate, a baseline change, a termination — model output is triangulated against independent evidence and expert judgement, never treated as sole arbiter.

### Accountability and the decision record

Governance requires that accountability for a decision is never transferred to a tool. The accountable decision-maker named in the decision record remains a competent human, however extensive the analytical or AI support employed.

The **decision record** binds the discipline together. It captures: the framed question; the options considered, including those rejected and why; the criteria, weights and scores with their evidence sources; the analytical and AI support used, the validation performed, and any point where human judgement departed from a model's recommendation and why; the sensitivity result; the accountable decision-maker, date and rationale; and a review trigger (in the worked example: "revisit if consultation feedback deteriorates or rework provision exceeds £40,000"). Records are reviewed at the next gate, and annually for pattern analysis across the portfolio.

This transparency serves three purposes. It makes decisions auditable and reviewable; it protects the organisation and the individual when decisions are later challenged; and it builds the institutional evidence base from which future models — and future decision-makers — learn, exposing recurring bias so that debiasing can be targeted. An unexplained model output is not evidence; it is an unverified claim, and the examination expects candidates to treat it as such.

### Common pitfalls

- **Retro-fitted weights** — adjusting weights after scoring until the preferred option wins; agree and freeze weights first.
- **Scoring without evidence** — matrices full of unsourced 3s and 4s that launder intuition as analysis.
- **Option scarcity** — evaluating one real option against two straw men.
- **Ignoring near-ties** — treating a 0.05 margin as decisive instead of triggering sensitivity analysis.
- **Anchoring on the first estimate** — failing to apply a reference class to single-point team estimates.
- **Unvalidated AI outputs** — pasting model-generated scores or forecasts into the matrix without checking assumptions, data currency and explainability.
- **Missing ownership** — records that name no accountable decision-maker, making later review impossible.

### Key points

- Decision quality is judged by process at the moment of choice — framing, options, criteria, evidence, accountability, record — not by outcome, which uncertainty can turn either way.
- Match the method to the decision: normative structure (MCDA, decision trees) for high-stakes, hard-to-reverse choices; recognition-primed and delegated paths for routine, reversible ones.
- Bias is systematic and predictable; counter it with reference-class forecasting, premortems, independent challenge, calibration and independent elicitation before group discussion.
- Criteria and weights are agreed with the decision-maker and frozen before scoring; every score carries an evidence source; a near-tie triggers sensitivity analysis, not a coin toss.
- AI may automate the mechanics — evidence gathering, option generation, sensitivity runs, record drafting — but framing, criteria, option completeness and evidence fitness are validated by the human.
- Every model-supported input is an estimate with provenance (data, limitations, validation, version), triangulated against independent evidence for significant decisions; automation bias is the failure mode to watch.
- The decision record names one accountable human, states what was AI-assisted and how it was validated, and sets a review trigger — making decisions auditable and organisational learning possible.

### Practice questions

**1.** A delivery leader runs a rigorous decision process — framed question, three evidenced options, pre-agreed weights, sensitivity test, completed record — yet the chosen option fails when a key supplier collapses without warning. How should the decision be judged?

A. As a poor decision, because outcome is the only objective test
B. As a good decision, because decision quality is assessed by the process followed at the moment of choice
C. As unjudgeable until the programme closes
D. As a poor decision unless the record is amended retrospectively

**Answer: B.** Under uncertainty the Institute assesses decision quality by process, not outcome; a well-made decision can still produce a poor result.

**2.** A weighted-criteria evaluation scores Option X at 3.60 and Option Y at 3.55. What should the leader do next?

A. Select X, because the highest weighted score wins
B. Re-weight the criteria until a clearer winner emerges
C. Test the sensitivity of the ranking to plausible changes in the weights and the least-evidenced scores
D. Discard the matrix and decide on judgement alone

**Answer: C.** A near-tie means the ranking may hinge on a single input; sensitivity analysis identifies which, and where further evidence is needed — re-weighting after scoring is the retro-fitted-weights pitfall.

**3.** An AI assistant produces a scored options matrix recommending termination of a workstream, but the team cannot obtain an explanation of how the ranking was derived. Institute doctrine requires the leader to:

A. Accept the recommendation, since the model has processed more data than any individual could
B. Use the output to inform exploration only, and not allow it to determine the decision
C. Adopt the recommendation provided the model exceeds a validated accuracy threshold
D. Transfer accountability for the decision to the owner of the model

**Answer: B.** An unexplained model output is an unverified claim, not evidence: AI proposes, but only a named, accountable professional verifies and decides.

## 21. Digital Delivery

This chapter examines how the delivery professional specifies, governs and leads through connected digital environments: the project management information system (PMIS) and its specification, the single source of truth and data quality, the leadership of distributed teams, the governing disciplines of security and information management, and the admission of AI into the toolchain. Candidates are examined on judgement in scenario, not on knowledge of any product.

### Digital delivery and the project management information system

Digital delivery is the conduct of project initiation, planning, execution and control through connected digital environments, such that the project's plans, records, communications and performance data exist primarily as structured, shareable information rather than as documents held by individuals. Its central instrument is the **PMIS**: the combination of tools, data stores, integrations, procedures and people that captures, validates, integrates, analyses and distributes delivery information across the lifecycle.

The Institute defines the PMIS **functionally, not as a product**. A candidate is examined on the ability to specify what a PMIS must do — the data it must hold, the integrations it must support, the controls it must enforce and the reporting it must enable — independently of any vendor's software. Guidance such as ISO 21502 treats the PMIS in the same way: as an organisational capability supporting governance and decision-making, configured to the project's scale, contracting environment and information-security obligations. A **toolchain** is the set of interoperating applications — scheduling, cost management, risk registers, collaboration, document control — that together realise the PMIS; the leader's concern is the coherence of the chain, not the merits of any single link.

### Specifying the PMIS

A PMIS specification is written from decisions backwards, never from a product feature list forwards. The examinable method has six steps:

1. **List the decisions the system must support** — baseline change approval, monthly forecasting, risk response drawdown, benefits tracking — and the questions each decision asks of the data.
2. **Define the data objects and their owners.** For each object (activity, control account, risk, change, benefit), name one accountable owner and the system in which it is mastered. Every object is mastered exactly once; all other systems consume it.
3. **Specify integrations, not re-keying.** State which interfaces move data between the schedule, cost, risk and document environments, at what frequency, and with what reconciliation control.
4. **Set data standards as requirements** — coding structures (WBS, CBS, OBS), naming conventions, update cadence and validation rules — because standards, not software, create the single source of truth.
5. **State the non-functional requirements**: least-privilege access control, audit trail, retention, availability, and exportability of the organisation's own data at contract end.
6. **Write acceptance criteria as tests** — "a change approved on day 1 appears in the period-end report of the same cycle" — so conformance can be verified whatever product is eventually procured.

### The single source of truth and data quality

A **single source of truth** exists when every controlled data item — a milestone date, a budget figure, a risk exposure, an approved change — has one authoritative location from which all reporting derives, so that two reports can never legitimately disagree. This is a property of governance and data architecture, not of buying one system: it is achieved by designating authoritative sources, integrating systems so data flows without re-keying, and version-controlling baselines. The concept parallels the **common data environment** formalised in the ISO 19650 series for built-asset information management; the Institute applies its logic generically to all delivery data.

Its foundation is **data quality**, assessed against recognised dimensions drawn from frameworks such as ISO 8000 and ISO/IEC 25012: accuracy, completeness, timeliness, consistency, validity and uniqueness. These dimensions matter because delivery data is consumed downstream — by status reporting, by cost and schedule forecasting, and increasingly by AI-supported analysis. Deficient data degrades each in turn: inaccurate progress data corrupts earned value and forecasts; incomplete risk data understates exposure; and models trained or prompted on poor data produce confidently wrong outputs.

### Auditing data quality before reporting and AI-supported analysis

Before each reporting cycle — and before any AI model is run against delivery data — the leader has the PMO score the data cut on four of the dimensions above: **completeness** (required fields populated), **validity** (values pass business rules), **timeliness** (records updated within the cut-off) and **consistency** (values reconcile across systems). Each dimension is measured as passing records divided by total records, combined into a weighted **data quality index (DQI)** and compared with a published threshold. Below-threshold data is remediated or the affected outputs caveated; an AI-generated forecast built on failed data is not released.

**Worked example.** A programme PMO audits the month-end cut of 1,200 live activities before an AI-supported estimate-at-completion run. Weights: completeness 0.30, validity 0.30, timeliness 0.25, consistency 0.15; release threshold 95.0%.

- Completeness: 1,146 ÷ 1,200 = 95.5%
- Validity: 1,092 ÷ 1,200 = 91.0%
- Timeliness: 1,020 ÷ 1,200 = 85.0%
- Consistency: 1,164 ÷ 1,200 = 97.0%

DQI = (0.30 × 95.5) + (0.30 × 91.0) + (0.25 × 85.0) + (0.15 × 97.0) = 28.65 + 27.30 + 21.25 + 14.55 = 91.75, i.e. **91.8%** to one decimal place — below the 95.0% threshold.

The materiality of the gap is then demonstrated. The late records conceal 1,740 unposted labour hours at a blended USD 110 per hour: 1,740 × 110 = USD 191,400 of understated actual cost. With earned value EV = USD 6,240,000 and recorded actual cost AC = USD 6,050,000, the reported cost performance index CPI = 6,240,000 ÷ 6,050,000 = 1.031 (1.03 to two decimal places). Correcting AC to 6,050,000 + 191,400 = USD 6,241,400 gives CPI = 6,240,000 ÷ 6,241,400 = 0.9998 (1.00). On a budget at completion of USD 15,600,000, the CPI-based estimate at completion (EAC = BAC ÷ CPI, calculated on unrounded values) moves from USD 15,125,000 to USD 15,603,500 — USD 478,500 of optimism created entirely by a data deficiency. The leader defers the forecast run until the timesheets post, and minutes the DQI, the correction and the decision.

### Leading distributed and virtual teams

A **distributed team** is one whose members work from different locations; a **virtual team** collaborates primarily through digital channels; most contemporary delivery teams are **hybrid**, mixing co-located and remote working across time zones and organisational boundaries. Distributed working changes the medium of leadership, not its substance: direction, motivation, conflict resolution and performance management remain the delivery leader's accountabilities, but they must be exercised deliberately through explicit **collaboration norms**. In practice the operating rhythm is:

1. **Agree a team charter**: working hours by time zone, expected response times, and which channel carries which class of traffic.
2. **Default to asynchronous; escalate to synchronous.** Reserve live meetings for decisions and problem-solving, and record every decision in the designated system of record — never only in a chat thread.
3. **Protect a core overlap window** and rotate recurring meeting times so the same location does not always absorb the unsociable hours.
4. **Apply information security by norm**: least-privilege access, approved platforms only, and no project data in personal channels or in public AI tools the organisation has not sanctioned.
5. **Check inclusion deliberately** — invite contribution from remote and quieter participants — because digital settings amplify, rather than remove, participation imbalances.

Two obligations underpin this rhythm. First, **inclusive digital working**: norms must not systematically disadvantage members by time zone, language, accessibility need or home circumstance, because exclusion degrades both fairness and the quality of project information. Second, **information discipline**: in a distributed team the written record in the PMIS is frequently the only shared reality, so insisting that decisions, assumptions and changes are recorded at source is a leadership behaviour, not an administrative preference.

### Security, information management and digital capability

Digital delivery is bounded by three governing disciplines. **Information security** protects the confidentiality, integrity and availability of project information; the reference framework is ISO/IEC 27001, which situates security controls within a managed system of risk assessment, policy and continual improvement rather than as isolated technical measures. **Information management** governs the lifecycle of project records — classification, retention, access control and disposal — including compliance with applicable data-protection legislation when personal data is processed. **Digital capability** is the demonstrated ability of the team and its stakeholders to use the toolchain competently and securely; a technically sound PMIS operated by an unprepared team still fails. These disciplines are the delivery leader's responsibility in their project context, exercised with specialist support, and they apply equally to AI-enabled tools: any AI capability admitted to the toolchain must satisfy the same security, records and explainability requirements as every other component.

### How AI changes digital delivery

AI is now a native layer of the digital delivery environment rather than an add-on to it. Within a well-architected PMIS, machine learning models can flag schedule slippage earlier than periodic reporting, detect anomalies in cost and progress data, classify and route documents, summarise meeting records and generate first-draft status narratives. Predictive tooling turns the single source of truth from a repository into an early-warning system — but only when the underlying data merits that trust. Every such capability is a projection from historical and current project data; where that data is incomplete, stale or inconsistently coded, AI does not fail loudly. It produces fluent, confident and wrong outputs. The leader's competency therefore shifts: less effort assembling information, more effort interrogating it.

A defensible division of labour treats automation and validation as complementary, not interchangeable.

- **Appropriate to automate:** data capture and consolidation from source systems; consistency and completeness checks; routine collation of dashboards and progress packs; document classification, version control and search; transcription and first-draft summaries of distributed-team meetings; alerting on threshold breaches.
- **Requiring human validation:** any forecast of cost, schedule or benefits before it informs a decision or a report to governance bodies; anomaly flags before escalation (a flagged anomaly may be a data defect, not a delivery problem); AI-generated narratives before issue to sponsors or clients; any recommendation affecting people — workload analysis, performance inferences or team-sentiment signals — which raises both ethical and legal obligations.

The rule is simple: automate the assembly of evidence; never automate the judgement, and never allow an AI-generated figure to reach a decision-maker without a named, competent owner who can explain it.

### Model risk, data caveats and human accountability

Model risk in digital delivery arises predictably. Models trained on past projects inherit the biases and estimating errors of those projects; a portfolio that habitually underestimated will train a model that normalises underestimation. Predictions degrade when the current project differs materially from the training population, and gradual drift in data definitions — a recoded activity structure, a changed progress-measurement rule — silently invalidates trained relationships. Leaders should require of any predictive tool, from any vendor: transparency about input data and its provenance; stated confidence ranges rather than single-point outputs; evidence of back-testing against known outcomes; and a documented boundary of applicability. Data caveats belong on the outputs themselves — a forecast built on unassured data should say so wherever it appears. Distributed working adds a security dimension: uncontrolled use of external AI services to summarise or draft project material can leak commercially sensitive or personal data outside the information-management boundary, so the acceptable-use position for AI tools is part of the information security regime, not an afterthought.

Accountability is not transferable to a model. The leader who presents an AI-supported forecast owns it exactly as if it had been produced manually, and must be able to explain its basis, assumptions and limitations to a sponsor, an auditor or a client. Governance arrangements should record which AI tools are in use, for what purpose, on what data, and who owns each class of output. An output that no competent person can explain has no place in a delivery decision, whatever its apparent precision.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** In the digital delivery environment, AI may assemble, reconcile, flag and forecast from the single source of truth — but every prediction, anomaly and generated narrative is a proposal. The delivery leader validates it against the quality of the data beneath it, explains it in their own words, and remains personally accountable for every decision and report that rests upon it.

### Common pitfalls

- Specifying the PMIS by copying a vendor's feature list instead of the organisation's decisions, data objects and standards.
- Tolerating multiple "sources of truth" — the same figure mastered in the schedule tool, the cost tool and a spreadsheet, reconciled by hand each month.
- Treating data quality as an IT concern, so deficiencies surface only when reports, forecasts and AI outputs are already wrong.
- Running AI analysis over an unaudited data cut and presenting the output with unwarranted precision.
- Measuring distributed team members by online presence rather than outputs, corroding trust and encouraging performative availability.
- Allowing decisions to live in chat threads: unretrievable, unauditable and invisible across time zones.
- Applying security controls to systems but not behaviours, leaving authorised platforms surrounded by unofficial workarounds.

### Key points

- The PMIS is defined functionally — by the decisions, data, integrations, controls and reporting it must support — and specified from decisions backwards, never from a vendor's feature list.
- A single source of truth is a property of governance and data architecture: every controlled data item is mastered exactly once, and all reporting derives from that source.
- Data quality is assessed on recognised dimensions (accuracy, completeness, timeliness, consistency, validity, uniqueness) and audited before each reporting cycle via a weighted DQI against a published threshold.
- Below-threshold data is remediated or its outputs caveated; an AI forecast built on failed data is deferred, and the audit, correction and decision are minuted.
- Distributed leadership works through explicit collaboration norms: a team charter, asynchronous-by-default working, protected overlap windows, deliberate inclusion and decisions recorded in the system of record.
- Information security (ISO/IEC 27001), information management and digital capability bound the whole environment and apply equally to any AI tool admitted to the toolchain.
- AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable: automate the assembly of evidence, never the judgement, and keep a named, competent owner accountable for every AI-supported output.

### Practice questions

**1.** A programme is procuring a new PMIS. Which starting point best reflects the Institute's examinable method for writing the specification?

- A. A comparison matrix of the leading scheduling and cost tools on the market
- B. The list of delivery decisions the system must support and the questions each asks of the data
- C. The feature set of the incumbent tool, extended with stakeholder wish-list items
- D. The IT department's standard infrastructure and licensing catalogue

**Answer: B.** The specification is written from decisions backwards — decisions, then data objects and owners, integrations, standards, non-functional requirements and acceptance tests — never from product features forwards.

**2.** A month-end data cut scores a DQI of 92.1% against a published release threshold of 95.0%, largely due to late timesheet postings. An AI-supported estimate at completion is scheduled to run tonight and the sponsor expects the forecast tomorrow. What should the delivery leader do?

- A. Run the forecast and present it, since the model will smooth out data gaps
- B. Run the forecast but round the outputs to reflect reduced confidence
- C. Defer the run until the data is remediated, minute the DQI and the decision, and tell the sponsor why
- D. Lower the threshold to 92.0% for this cycle so the run can proceed

**Answer: C.** Below-threshold data is remediated or the affected outputs withheld or caveated; releasing a forecast on failed data — or bending the threshold to fit — presents unwarranted precision the leader cannot validate.

**3.** An AI tool in the PMIS generates a schedule forecast that the delivery leader includes in a report to the project board. Who is accountable for the forecast, and on what condition may it be presented?

- A. The tool vendor, provided the model has been back-tested
- B. The PMO analyst who ran the model, provided the DQI passed
- C. No one, because a statistical output cannot carry personal accountability
- D. The delivery leader, provided they can explain its basis, assumptions and limitations

**Answer: D.** Accountability is not transferable to a model: the leader owns an AI-supported forecast exactly as if produced manually, and an output no competent person can explain has no place in a delivery decision.

## 22. Automation in Project Delivery

This chapter sets out the PML-AI competency of automation: what it is, when it is justified, how it is designed, and how it is governed once AI enters the workflow. Its concepts form one causal chain: the selection triad determines *what* may be automated; the checkpoint requirement, *how* it must be designed; testing discipline, *whether* it can be trusted; and monitoring with fallback, *how long* trust remains justified — with a named professional accountable for every link.

### What Automation Means in Project Delivery

Automation in project delivery is the execution of a defined project management process, or a step within one, by a machine rather than a person, according to rules established and owned by a competent professional. The Institute distinguishes automation from mere digitisation. Digitisation moves a manual activity onto an electronic medium — a spreadsheet replacing a paper log — while the judgement and the labour remain human. Automation transfers the labour itself: the system detects a trigger, applies a rule and produces an outcome without a person performing the intermediate steps. A status report compiled and distributed on schedule, or a risk register entry escalated when its exposure score crosses a threshold, is an automation because the process runs to completion, or to a designated pause point, without human effort.

The Institute recognises a continuum. At one end sits deterministic automation: rule-based workflows fully specified in advance, with predictable, repeatable output. At the other sits AI-enabled automation, in which a model classifies, summarises, drafts or predicts, and the output is probabilistic rather than guaranteed. The distinction matters for governance: deterministic automations fail in ways testing can largely anticipate; AI-enabled automations can fail plausibly, producing outputs that look correct while being wrong. The PML-AI doctrine — AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable — therefore binds most tightly at the AI-enabled end, though the accountability principle beneath it applies across the whole range: every automated output must be explainable, validated and owned by a named, competent human.

### The Selection Triad: Volume, Repeatability and Rule Clarity

Automation is justified by the character of the process, not the availability of the technology. The Institute's selection triad holds that a project process is a candidate for automation when it exhibits, together, sufficient **volume** (it occurs often enough to repay the cost of building and maintaining the automation), **repeatability** (successive occurrences follow substantially the same path, so one design covers all cases) and **rule clarity** (the decision logic can be stated completely and unambiguously, so the machine's behaviour can be specified rather than hoped for).

The triad is conjunctive. A high-volume process with unclear rules automates error at scale; a rule-clear process performed twice a year rarely repays its build cost; and a repeatable process embedded in a consequential judgement — approving a change, communicating with a client — may satisfy all three criteria mechanically yet still demand a human decision at its core.

### Checkpoints, Fallback and the Architecture of Control

A **human checkpoint** is a designed pause at which an automated workflow halts, presents its proposed action and reasoning, and proceeds only on the authorisation of an accountable person. The Institute's standard is categorical: a checkpoint is required at every consequential decision — one that commits resources, alters baselines, accepts risk or affects people — and at every external communication, because a message issued to a stakeholder cannot be recalled. Checkpoints are the workflow-level expression of the Institute's principle; they convert "the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable" from aspiration into architecture.

A **fallback arrangement** is the pre-defined route by which a process returns safely to human control when the automation fails, degrades or encounters a case outside its rules. Fallback presupposes **monitoring**: instrumented evidence that the automation is running and producing outputs within expected tolerances, with alerts when it is not. An automation without monitoring is invisible in failure; an automation without fallback leaves the underlying process ownerless when it stops. Both must exist before an automation is entrusted with live project work.

### Frameworks and Standards Context

These concepts sit within an established governance landscape. International project management guidance, including ISO 21502, treats tailoring and process discipline as the project manager's responsibility — automation is a tailoring decision and inherits that accountability. AI management system standards, notably ISO/IEC 42001, and emerging AI regulation converge on requirements the Institute's doctrine already embodies: human oversight, transparency of logic, documented accountability. Internal control traditions add the segregation principle that an automation's designer should not be its sole reviewer. The examination tests these sources as context only; candidates are assessed against the Institute's own doctrine, and PCI claims no accreditation by, or endorsement from, the bodies publishing them.

### A Three-Stage Method for Justified Automation

Candidates are examined on moving from intuition ("this feels automatable") to a defensible, quantified decision. The Institute's method proceeds in three stages: screen and score, design with checkpoints, then implement with monitoring and fallback.

**Stage 1 — Screen and score candidates.**

1. Inventory the recurring project management processes in scope (status consolidation, minutes distribution, register updates, timesheet chasing).
2. Score each against the triad on a 1–5 scale: **volume**, **repeatability** and **rule clarity**, the last tested by whether a competent professional could write the rules down unambiguously.
3. Apply agreed weights — a common convention is 0.4 for volume, 0.3 for repeatability, 0.3 for rule clarity — and set an admission threshold (for example, 3.5) below which candidates are rejected or deferred.
4. For candidates above the threshold, quantify baseline effort, expected automated effort, build cost and recurring maintenance, and compute payback before committing resources.

**Stage 2 — Design with human checkpoints.** Map the workflow end to end and classify every step as *mechanical* (data collection, formatting, calculation) or *consequential* (anything that commits the organisation: approvals, escalations, external communications, baseline changes). Mechanical steps may run unattended; every consequential step receives a named human checkpoint with authority to amend or reject the proposed output. No automated output leaves the project boundary without human disposition.

**Stage 3 — Implement, monitor, fall back.** Build in a controlled environment, test against historical cases with known correct outcomes, and run in parallel with the manual process for at least one full cycle. Define in advance the monitoring signals (exception rates, data-freshness checks, reviewer rejection rates) and the triggers on which the process reverts entirely to human execution.

### Worked Example: Automating Weekly Portfolio Status Consolidation

A PMO supports 15 projects. Each week an analyst collects status data, reformats it and assembles a portfolio pack.

**Step 1 — Score the candidate.** Volume scores 5 (15 instances weekly, every week), repeatability 4 (identical structure, occasional format drift), rule clarity 4 (consolidation rules are documented; RAG thresholds are defined numerically).

Weighted score = (5 × 0.4) + (4 × 0.3) + (4 × 0.3) = 2.0 + 1.2 + 1.2 = **4.4**, above the 3.5 threshold. The candidate is admitted to business-case analysis.

**Step 2 — Quantify the baseline.** Collection and reformatting take 30 minutes per project: 15 × 30 = 450 minutes = 7.5 hours. Assembling the portfolio pack adds 1.5 hours. Total manual effort = **9.0 hours per week**. Over 46 working weeks, annual effort = 46 × 9.0 = **414 hours**. At a fully loaded rate of £52 per hour, the baseline cost = 414 × £52 = **£21,528 per annum**.

**Step 3 — Quantify the automated state.** The automation extracts data from the scheduling and cost tools and drafts each project summary and the portfolio pack. Human checkpoints remain: the analyst reviews each draft summary (5 minutes × 15 = 75 minutes) and performs a final portfolio review before issue (45 minutes). Automated-state effort = 120 minutes = **2.0 hours per week**; annually 46 × 2.0 = 92 hours = 92 × £52 = **£4,784**.

**Step 4 — Compute the business case.** Gross annual saving = 414 − 92 = 322 hours = 322 × £52 = **£16,744**. Build and testing require 120 hours at £60 per hour = **£7,200**. Annual maintenance is estimated at 15% of build cost = **£1,080**. Net annual saving = £16,744 − £1,080 = **£15,664**. Payback period = £7,200 ÷ £15,664 = **0.46 years, roughly 5.5 months** — a justified automation, provided the checkpoints in Step 3 are preserved. Note that the review hours retained are not inefficiency; they are the cost of professional ownership of every issued report.

### How AI Changes the Automation Competency

AI-enabled automation extends the deterministic model in two consequential ways.
