First, predictive and generative components let workflows handle inputs previously too unstructured to automate — classifying incoming change requests, drafting status narratives from schedule and cost data, triaging risk register entries, routing correspondence. Second, agentic tooling can chain steps together, deciding at run time which action to take next. Both widen the pool of candidates but change the risk profile fundamentally: the triad's tests remain necessary yet are no longer sufficient, because an AI component may produce a plausible but wrong output on an input it has never seen. The PML-AI professional therefore evaluates AI-enabled candidates against an additional criterion — the cost of an undetected error — and reserves full automation for processes where that cost is demonstrably low.

### What the Leader Automates and What the Leader Validates

The defensible division of labour: AI may automate the assembly, transformation and first-draft interpretation of project information — data collection, log consolidation, draft minutes, candidate schedule updates, anomaly flags. The professional validates everything that carries consequence: baseline changes, forecasts communicated to sponsors, contractual or financial actions, escalations, and any message leaving the project boundary. The checkpoint standard applies with greater force where AI is in the loop, because AI failure modes are silent: a broken deterministic script usually stops, while a degraded model keeps producing confident output. Checkpoints for AI-enabled steps must therefore involve genuine review by a competent person able to explain why the output is correct; the examination treats perfunctory approval clicks as a governance failure, not a control.

### Model Risk and Data Caveats

AI-enabled automations inherit every weakness of the data and models beneath them. Predictive components trained on historical project data embed the biases and reporting habits of those projects; they degrade when the portfolio, methodology or economic context shifts (model drift), and they perform worst precisely on the novel situations where projects are most exposed. Generative components can fabricate detail that appears authoritative. For each AI-enabled automation the professional documents the data sources and their limitations, the validation conditions, the boundaries outside which output must not be trusted, and the cadence at which performance is re-tested against actuals. Monitoring must detect quiet degradation as well as hard failure — rising exception rates, drifting error distributions, outputs reviewers increasingly override — and fallback must return the process to fully manual operation without loss of records or audit trail.

### Human Accountability

No delegation of execution is a delegation of accountability. The leader who deploys an AI-enabled automation owns its outputs as personally as if they had produced them by hand. Every automated decision must be explainable to a sponsor, an auditor or a regulator; a named, competent individual must own each automation with authority to suspend it; and governance records must show who approved it, on what evidence, and when it was last validated. Where an output cannot be explained, it must not be relied upon. These expectations are examined behaviourally: the assessment tests whether the candidate places accountability, monitoring and fallback at the centre of automation practice, not at its periphery.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** An AI-enabled automation may gather, draft, classify and recommend — but every consequential action it initiates is released, and owned, by a competent human who can explain it. When the automation's behaviour can no longer be explained, validated and monitored, the professional's duty is to stop it and take the process back into human hands.

### Common Pitfalls

- **Automating a broken process.** Automation amplifies whatever it is given; standardise and stabilise first.
- **Counting gross rather than net savings.** Omitting maintenance, licence and checkpoint-review effort overstates the case at governance boards.
- **Removing checkpoints to "realise the full benefit".** Eliminating human review of consequential outputs breaches the disposition doctrine and converts efficiency into unmanaged risk.
- **Rule clarity assumed rather than tested.** If two experienced practitioners would decide an instance differently, the rules are not clear enough to encode.
- **Silent failure.** Monitoring must detect absence of output and degraded inputs, not merely errors — stopped or stale automations fail invisibly.
- **No rehearsed fallback.** A fallback that exists only on paper fails under pressure; the manual procedure must be documented, current and exercised.
- **Orphaned ownership.** Every automation requires a named owner accountable for its outputs, maintenance and retirement — an unowned automation is an unowned risk.

### Key Points

- Automation executes a process by machine under rules owned by a competent professional; digitisation merely changes the medium while the labour stays human.
- The selection triad — volume, repeatability, rule clarity — is conjunctive: a candidate must satisfy all three, and passing the screen admits it only to business-case analysis, not to build.
- Every consequential decision and every external communication requires a named human checkpoint; only mechanical steps may run unattended.
- A justified business case counts net savings — maintenance, licences and retained checkpoint-review effort included; the review hours are the cost of professional ownership.
- No automation goes live without monitoring (to make failure visible) and a rehearsed fallback (to return the process safely to human hands).
- AI widens the candidate pool but fails silently and plausibly, so candidates face a further test — the cost of an undetected error — and checkpoints must involve genuine review.
- Accountability is never delegated: a named owner holds authority to suspend each automation and must be able to explain every decision it releases.

### Practice Questions

**1.** A PMO identifies a daily, highly repeatable process, but two experienced practitioners handling the same instance would reach different decisions. Under the selection triad, the process is:

A. A strong candidate, because two of the three criteria are satisfied
B. A candidate, provided an AI model is used to infer the missing rules
C. Not a candidate until its rules are clarified and standardised, because the triad is conjunctive
D. A candidate, provided the payback period is under one year

**Correct answer: C.** The triad is conjunctive — automating unclear rules automates error at scale, and disagreement between competent practitioners is precisely the test rule clarity fails.

**2.** Using weights of 0.4 (volume), 0.3 (repeatability) and 0.3 (rule clarity) with a 3.5 threshold, a process scores volume 3, repeatability 5 and rule clarity 4. The weighted score and correct next step are:

A. 3.9; admit to business-case analysis
B. 4.0; admit to business-case analysis
C. 3.9; proceed directly to build
D. 3.0; reject, because the lowest criterion governs

**Correct answer: A.** The weighted sum is 3.9, clearing the threshold and admitting the candidate to business-case analysis, not directly to build; B is the unweighted mean and D misapplies the conjunctive principle to the arithmetic.

**3.** An AI model drafting weekly status narratives has degraded because the portfolio's context has shifted since validation. The most characteristic symptom, and its design implication, is:

A. The workflow halts with an error, so alerting on stopped jobs is sufficient
B. The model keeps producing confident, plausible narratives that are wrong, so checkpoints need genuine review and monitoring must detect quiet degradation
C. The model fails only in high-volume weeks, so capacity monitoring is the priority
D. The model refuses to generate output outside its validated boundary, so no further control is needed

**Correct answer: B.** A degraded model fails silently and plausibly rather than stopping; the control response is substantive review at checkpoints plus monitoring for drift signals such as rising reviewer override rates.

## 23. Responsible AI

### Responsible AI as a delivery discipline

Responsible AI is the discipline of using artificial intelligence in ways that realise its benefits without transferring unmanaged harm to individuals, to the organisation or to the project itself. For the Project Management Leader – AI it is not an abstract ethical debate but a delivery competence. Every AI use on a project — an estimating model, a schedule-risk predictor, a generative assistant drafting communications, a tool that shortlists suppliers or allocates people to work — creates obligations that must be identified, assessed and controlled with the same rigour the profession applies to cost, schedule and risk. The doctrine that governs this entire Body of Knowledge applies here in its strongest form: **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** An AI output remains a proposal until a competent, named human has understood it, validated it and taken ownership of the decision it informs. Responsible AI is the framework that makes that ownership real rather than rhetorical.

The chapter follows a single control loop: principles supply the criteria; regulation converts several of them into binding obligations; risk assessment determines what proportionate control looks like for a given use; and explainability, validation and assurance are how that control is exercised in practice.

### The four principles, precisely defined

Four principles anchor the discipline, and each has a precise meaning.

**Fairness** is the property that an AI system's outputs do not produce unjustified differential outcomes for individuals or groups. Unfairness enters through biased or unrepresentative training data, through proxy variables that stand in for protected characteristics, and through the context of deployment. On projects, fairness questions arise wherever AI touches people: resourcing and task allocation, performance assessment, recruitment into teams, and supplier or bid evaluation.

**Transparency** is openness about the system: that AI is being used, for what purpose, on what data, and with what known limitations. It is distinct from **explainability**, which is the ability to give an intelligible account of how a *particular output* was produced — a distinction this chapter maintains throughout, because a project may be transparent about using a tool whose individual outputs it still cannot explain.

**Privacy** is the protection of personal data throughout the AI lifecycle: a lawful basis for processing, purpose limitation, data minimisation, and control over personal data entering training sets, prompts and outputs. Privacy exposure on projects is often inadvertent — team data pasted into a generative tool, or personal details embedded in documents used as model context.

**Accountability** is the ability to attribute every AI-informed decision to a named person or governance body with the authority and competence to answer for it. Accountability can never be delegated to a system, a vendor or an algorithm; it can only be assigned to humans and evidenced through records.

The four principles interlock. Transparency is the precondition of accountability: no one can answer for a system whose use is undisclosed. Explainability is the precondition of validation: an output that cannot be accounted for cannot be meaningfully checked. Privacy constrains what data may lawfully fuel a system, and fairness can only be demonstrated — not merely asserted — where transparency permits testing.

### The governing frameworks

The principles are not free-floating; a body of law, regulation and standards gives them force. Data protection law — the UK GDPR and Data Protection Act 2018, the EU GDPR, and their counterparts in other jurisdictions — imposes binding obligations wherever AI processes personal data: lawful basis, data protection impact assessment for high-risk processing, data subject rights, and specific restrictions on decisions based solely on automated processing that significantly affect individuals. The EU AI Act establishes the first comprehensive AI-specific regulatory regime, classifying AI uses by risk tier: prohibited practices, high-risk systems subject to conformity and oversight requirements, limited-risk systems subject to transparency duties, and minimal-risk uses. Delivery leaders should note that employment-related AI, including workforce allocation and evaluation, falls within the high-risk tier.

Alongside binding law sit voluntary frameworks that shape professional expectations: the OECD AI Principles, the NIST AI Risk Management Framework with its govern–map–measure–manage functions, ISO/IEC 42001 for AI management systems, and ISO/IEC 23894, which extends ISO 31000 risk-management logic to AI. This Body of Knowledge draws on these frameworks as context for examination purposes; the credential does not confer, and PCI does not claim, certification or accreditation against any of them. The delivery leader's obligation is to recognise when a proposed AI use engages these regimes and to escalate to legal, data-protection or information-security specialists — not to substitute for them.

### Risk assessment, validation and assurance

Three further terms complete the working vocabulary. **AI risk assessment** is the structured evaluation of the harms a specific AI use could cause — bias, hallucination, data leakage, model drift, over-reliance and automation bias — and of their likelihood and impact, so that controls can be **proportionate**: calibrated to consequence, not applied uniformly. **Validation** is the point-in-time activity of establishing that an AI output is accurate and fit for the decision it will inform, through testing against known results, benchmarking, back-testing and structured human review. **Assurance** is the through-life counterpart: ongoing, suitably independent confirmation that controls continue to operate — monitoring, audit trails and periodic revalidation as data, models and usage change.

### The responsible AI assessment, step by step

Responsible AI on a project is not a policy statement; it is a repeatable assessment that the delivery leader runs before any AI output is allowed to inform a decision. The Institute's method has seven steps, applied per use — not per tool, since the same tool can be benign in one use and high-risk in another.

1. **Specify the use and its decision consequence.** State what the AI produces, which project decision it informs, who is affected, and what happens if the output is wrong — a drafting aid and a tool that influences payments or people carry very different risk profiles.
2. **Screen against the four principles.** For each of fairness, transparency, privacy and accountability, score inherent risk as likelihood (1–5) × impact (1–5). Any dimension scoring 10 or more triggers enhanced controls; any processing of personal data triggers a data protection assessment.
3. **Map the regulatory touchpoints.** Identify whether personal data is processed and whether AI regulation classifies the use as higher-risk; recognise the trigger and obtain specialist data-protection or legal advice rather than improvising a legal conclusion.
4. **Assign accountability.** Name the competent individual who owns every decision the AI informs. The professional must be identified before deployment, not after an incident.
5. **Design proportionate controls.** Match control depth to the screening score: disclosure to affected parties, defined human review points, data handling rules, and logging sufficient to reconstruct how any output influenced a decision.
6. **Validate before reliance.** Back-test the AI against historical cases with known outcomes. Compute error rates overall *and by relevant subgroup*, and test explainability: the owner must be able to explain any individual output in plain language.
7. **Decide, document and monitor.** Approve, reject or approve with conditions; set a re-validation cadence and the drift triggers that force early re-testing.

### Worked example: validating a delivery-risk flagging tool

**Setup.** A programme office proposes an AI tool that reads supplier progress data and flags work packages at risk of late delivery. Flagged packages receive escalated oversight, and milestone certification may be delayed pending review. Screening scores fairness at 4 × 3 = 12 and transparency at 3 × 4 = 12, so the use enters the enhanced-control tier and must be validated before reliance. The back-test uses 60 closed work packages from the previous year, of which 21 finished late — a base rate of 21 ÷ 60 = 35.0%.

**Overall validation.** The tool flags 25 of the 60 packages: 17 were genuinely late (true positives), 8 were on time (false alarms), and 4 late packages were missed.

- Precision = 17 ÷ 25 = **68.0%** — roughly two of every three escalations are warranted.
- Recall = 17 ÷ 21 = **81.0%** — the tool catches four of every five genuinely late packages.

For an attention-directing use, this performance is workable: 68.0% precision far exceeds the 35.0% base rate a random escalation would achieve.

**Fairness check by subgroup.** The 60 packages split into 40 delivered by tier-1 contractors (12 late) and 20 by SME suppliers (9 late). The tool flagged 12 tier-1 packages (12 ÷ 40 = 30.0%) but 13 SME packages (13 ÷ 20 = 65.0%). The decisive test is the false-positive rate — the chance that an *on-time* package is wrongly escalated:

- Tier-1: 3 false alarms ÷ 28 on-time packages = **10.7%**
- SME: 5 false alarms ÷ 11 on-time packages = **45.5%**

An on-time SME package is therefore 45.5 ÷ 10.7 ≈ **4.2 times** more likely to be wrongly flagged than an on-time tier-1 package — a material fairness defect, since a wrongful flag delays certification and harms the supplier commercially.

**Disposition.** The owner approves with conditions: flags are advisory only; the named owner reviews the evidence behind every flag before escalation; no payment consequence may follow from a flag alone; the use is disclosed to suppliers; and the tool is recalibrated and re-tested, with the SME false-positive rate to fall below an agreed 20% before the human review step may be lightened. Quarterly re-validation is scheduled. The AI proposed; the professional disposed.

### Common pitfalls

- **Judging on aggregate accuracy alone.** The headline figures concealed a fourfold subgroup disparity; always cut error rates by the groups the decision affects.
- **Assessing the tool, not the use.** A screening done once "for the tool" misses the risk added when the same output starts driving payments or appraisals.
- **Treating validation as a one-off.** Data and behaviour drift; a regime without re-validation cadence and drift triggers decays silently.
- **Confusing transparency with disclosure of the algorithm.** Stakeholders need a plain-language explanation of each output, not source code.
- **Accepting vendor assurances as assurance.** Supplier claims inform, but never discharge, the project's own validation and data protection obligations.
- **Automation bias.** Letting the AI's confidence substitute for evidence at the review point turns "human oversight" into a rubber stamp.
- **Uniform maximal controls.** Applying high-risk controls to trivial uses breeds workarounds; proportionality is itself a control objective.
- **The accountability gap.** "The model decided" is never an acceptable answer; if no named professional owns the output, the use is not ready for deployment.

### AI-enabled governance: automate the mechanics, validate the judgement

Responsible AI is the one competency in this Body of Knowledge that AI tooling transforms reflexively: the tools of governance are increasingly AI-enabled themselves. Contemporary platforms can maintain a live inventory of AI use across a project, run automated bias and data-quality scans, monitor deployed models for drift, and draft the documentation — impact assessments, model descriptions, transparency notices — that responsible-AI frameworks require. This shifts the leader's work from periodic, manual review towards continuous, instrumented oversight, but it does not change the object of the competency: a dashboard that reports a fairness metric is evidence to be interrogated, not a judgement already made.

The division of labour follows the Institute's standing doctrine. Suitable for automation are the mechanical substrates of governance: logging and audit trails of AI-assisted decisions, recurring data-quality and drift checks, first-pass triage of proposed uses against a risk taxonomy, and the assembly of draft assurance documents. Reserved for a competent human are the judgements those mechanics feed: the risk classification assigned to a use, any conclusion that a system is sufficiently fair or private, the adequacy of an impact assessment, the explanation offered to an affected stakeholder, and the decision to rely on an AI output at all. The test is simple: automation may gather and arrange evidence; it may never discharge an obligation.

Using AI to assure AI also creates second-order model risk that the examination expects candidates to recognise. Automated fairness scores depend on which metric was chosen and whether the reference data represents the affected population — a favourable score on unrepresentative data is false comfort. Post-hoc explanation tools produce accounts that are plausible rather than guaranteed faithful to the model's behaviour, and must be treated as hypotheses. Generative tools drafting compliance documents can fabricate regulatory citations, so every legal reference is verified against the source or referred to specialist advice. Project data entered into external tools carries its own confidentiality exposure, and monitoring thresholds tuned at deployment decay as context shifts. Proportionality governs throughout: control depth should match the consequence of the use, not the enthusiasm of the tooling.

### The non-delegable core: human accountability

No configuration of tooling transfers accountability. Each AI use has a named, competent owner; escalation routes to data-protection, legal and technical specialists are defined before they are needed; and the delivery leader can explain, in their own words and without reference to the tool, why each AI-informed decision was justified. Where an output cannot be explained, validated and owned, it is not used — however efficient it promises to be. Governance records must show a human decision at every consequential point, because that is what a sponsor, an auditor or a regulator will ask to see.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** Applied to responsible AI, the doctrine becomes self-referential and stricter for it: AI may scan for bias, monitor for drift and draft the impact assessment, but only a competent, named human may declare an AI use fair, lawful, transparent and fit to inform a project decision. The tools of assurance are themselves models — so the final assurance is never a tool's output, but a professional's owned, explainable judgement.

### Key points

- Responsible AI is a delivery competence, not an ethical abstraction: every AI use creates obligations managed with the same rigour as cost, schedule and risk, under the doctrine *AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable*.
- Four principles anchor the discipline — fairness, transparency, privacy and accountability — and transparency (openness about the system) is distinct from explainability (accounting for a particular output).
- Binding law (data protection regimes, the EU AI Act's risk tiers) and voluntary frameworks (OECD, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC 42001 and 23894) give the principles force; the leader's duty is to recognise triggers and escalate to specialists.
- The seven-step assessment is applied per use, not per tool, and calibrates controls proportionately to decision consequence.
- Validation must cut error rates by affected subgroup: aggregate precision and recall can conceal material fairness defects, as the worked example's fourfold false-positive disparity shows.
- Assurance is through-life, not point-in-time: re-validation cadence, drift triggers and audit trails keep controls operating as data, models and usage change.
- AI may automate the mechanics of governance — logging, scanning, monitoring, drafting — but every judgement, and all accountability, remains with a named, competent human.

### Practice questions

**1.** A back-test of an AI escalation tool shows 68% precision and 81% recall overall. Which additional check is most likely to reveal a fairness defect before the tool is relied upon?

A. Comparing precision with the historical base rate
B. Re-running the back-test on a larger aggregate sample
C. Comparing false-positive rates across affected subgroups
D. Obtaining the vendor's fairness certification for the model

**Answer: C.** Aggregate metrics can conceal differential error rates; the subgroup false-positive comparison exposed the 4.2-fold disparity in the worked example.

**2.** A project openly discloses that a generative tool drafts its stakeholder communications, but no one can account for how a specific draft was produced. Which statement is correct?

A. The use is transparent but not explainable
B. The use is explainable but not transparent
C. The use is neither transparent nor explainable
D. Disclosure makes the use both transparent and explainable

**Answer: A.** Transparency is openness about the system's use; explainability is the ability to account for a particular output — disclosure alone provides only the former.

**3.** Under the Institute's doctrine for AI-enabled governance tooling, which task may be automated without a competent human validating the result before it takes effect?

A. Classifying the risk tier of a proposed AI use
B. Maintaining the audit log of AI-assisted decisions
C. Concluding that a system is sufficiently fair to deploy
D. Approving the adequacy of a data protection impact assessment

**Answer: B.** Automation may gather and arrange evidence, such as logging and audit trails, but it may never discharge an obligation — classification, fairness conclusions and adequacy judgements are reserved for a named human.

## 24. Human Accountability

This chapter sets out the discipline that underpins every other competency in the PML-AI Body of Knowledge: however capable the tooling, every decision in AI-assisted delivery must be owned, explained and answered for by a named, competent human.

### Accountability and responsibility: two distinct obligations

The Institute draws a hard distinction between two terms that everyday usage blurs. **Responsibility** is the obligation to *perform* work: to produce an estimate, draft a schedule, analyse a risk exposure, prepare a stage-gate submission. Responsibility can be delegated, shared among team members and — critically for this chapter — assisted or partially automated by AI systems. **Accountability** is the obligation to *answer* for a decision and its consequences: to explain why it was taken, on what evidence, under what authority, and to accept the professional and organisational consequences that follow. Accountability can never be delegated, shared, diffused across a committee, or transferred to a tool.

From this distinction follows the doctrine that anchors the whole Body of Knowledge: **for every decision in AI-assisted delivery, accountability rests with a named, competent human**. An AI system may carry out the mechanical work that responsibility denotes — generating a forecast, drafting a change assessment, flagging a schedule anomaly — but it cannot bear responsibility in the professional sense, and it can hold accountability for nothing. In assignment-matrix terms (RACI and its variants), an AI system may contribute to the work behind the "R", but the "A" is always a single, identifiable person. A decision log entry that names a tool, a vendor or "the model" as the deciding agent records an accountability vacuum, not an assignment.

### AI concentrates accountability; it does not dilute it

When forecasts, risk scores, schedule diagnostics and drafted recommendations arrive machine-generated, the temptation is to let ownership blur — to treat "the model indicated" as though it were a decision-maker. The examination treats this as the central hazard of AI-assisted delivery. Predictive tooling changes the competency in two ways: it multiplies the decision inputs whose provenance the leader must be able to trace, and it creates a new failure mode — the **accountability gap** — in which an outcome has no named human owner because everyone deferred to the system. The competent leader closes that gap deliberately: every AI-assisted decision is owned, in advance and by name, by a human competent to challenge the recommendation they are adopting.

### The governing doctrine: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable

The Institute's doctrine — *AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable* — operationalises the principle. An AI output, however sophisticated, is a **proposal**: an input to professional judgement, of the same governance status as a junior analyst's draft or a consultant's recommendation. The **disposal** — accepting, amending, overriding or rejecting the proposal — is a human act, performed by a professional with the competence and the authority to make it. Three tests attach to every AI contribution before it may inform a project decision:

- **Explainable** — the accountable professional can articulate, in their own words, what the output says, what data and assumptions it rests on, and its material limitations. "The system said so" is never an explanation.
- **Validated** — the output has been checked against independent evidence, professional experience, or documented review appropriate to its consequence: proportionate scrutiny for routine outputs, rigorous challenge for decisions affecting baselines, contracts, safety or benefits.
- **Owned** — a named individual has adopted the output as their own professional position and is recorded as having done so. Ownership converts a machine proposal into a human decision; without it, no disposal has occurred.

The doctrine also settles what may legitimately be automated. The leader may automate the clerical substrate of accountability: capturing decision records as they are made, logging which inputs were machine-generated, assembling gate evidence packs, drafting minutes and actions, and flagging decisions that lack a recorded owner. These automations strengthen governance by making ownership visible at scale. What is never automated is the disposition itself, nor the three tests that precede it. Where a leader cannot obtain an adequate explanation, or lacks the competence to evaluate one, the obligation is escalation, not acceptance: signing what one cannot explain is an abdication, not a delegation.

### Frameworks and standards context

This doctrine reflects a broad convergence across governance and AI-assurance frameworks. Project governance standards — ISO 21502 and the governance chapters of the leading professional bodies of knowledge — locate decision authority in defined roles (sponsor, board, project manager) exercising delegated authority within tolerances; nothing in that architecture contemplates a non-human decision-maker. AI-specific instruments reinforce the point: ISO/IEC 42001 requires organisations to define human roles, competence and oversight for AI use; ISO/IEC 23894 and ISO 31000 treat AI as a risk to be owned and managed like any other; the NIST AI Risk Management Framework places a "Govern" function — accountability structures, defined roles, documented oversight — at the centre of trustworthy AI use; and the EU AI Act's human-oversight requirements make effective human supervision of consequential AI systems a legal expectation, not merely good practice. Professional codes of conduct, including the Institute's own Code of Professional Conduct, complete the picture: a professional remains answerable for work products issued under their name regardless of the tools used to produce them. These frameworks converge on one proposition: *the human in the loop is not a courtesy; it is the locus of accountability.*

### Applying the accountability method step by step

The Institute expects candidates to operate, not merely describe, this discipline. The method below applies to any delivery decision to which an AI system contributes analysis, a forecast or a recommendation.

**Step 1 — Register the decision.** Before any AI output is consulted, enter the decision in the project decision log: what is to be decided, by when, and against which delegated authority limit.

**Step 2 — Name the accountable individual.** Assign accountability to one named, competent person — never to a team, a committee or the tool. Responsibility for preparatory work may be distributed; accountability may not. Confirm that the named individual is competent to explain the method the AI has used; if not, either develop that competence or reassign before proceeding.

**Step 3 — Classify the AI contribution.** Record whether the AI input is advisory (an option set), analytical (a computed forecast) or generative (a drafted deliverable), and note the model or service version, the input data set and its cut-off date. This provenance record is created at the moment of use, not reconstructed later.

**Step 4 — Validate proportionately.** Set validation effort in proportion to consequence. As a minimum, re-perform the central calculation independently, test the inputs for completeness and currency, and challenge the assumption most capable of changing the answer.

**Step 5 — Dispose.** The accountable professional accepts, amends or rejects the recommendation, recording the disposition and a rationale in their own words. An unexplained acceptance is treated as no review at all.

**Step 6 — Complete the audit trail entry.** Each entry records: decision reference; AI contribution and provenance; validation performed; disposition (accepted, amended, rejected); rationale; the named accountable individual; and date. Rejections and amendments are recorded with the same rigour as acceptances.

**Step 7 — Close the loop.** At governance reviews, sample entries, compare AI recommendations with human dispositions and eventual outcomes, and apply consequences — corrective, developmental or disciplinary — to the named human, exactly as for any other professional decision.

### Worked example: dispositioning an AI schedule-recovery recommendation

A programme with a budget at completion (BAC) of £24,000,000 and a planned duration of 92 weeks is at its week-40 review. Planned value (PV) is £10,500,000 and earned value (EV) is £9,240,000. An AI scheduling assistant proposes a recovery package. The project manager, the named accountable individual, works through the method.

The AI's proposal (Step 3, analytical contribution) rests on this arithmetic:

- SPI = EV ÷ PV = £9,240,000 ÷ £10,500,000 = **0.88**
- Forecast duration = 92 ÷ 0.88 = **104.5 weeks**, a forecast delay of 12.5 weeks
- Liquidated damages exposure at £45,000 per week = 12.5 × £45,000 = **£562,500**
- Recommended action: a £340,000 acceleration package recovering 10 weeks, for a claimed net benefit of (10 × £45,000) − £340,000 = £450,000 − £340,000 = **£110,000**

Validation (Step 4): the project manager re-performs the SPI calculation and confirms it, then challenges the governing assumption — that SPI-based extrapolation reflects the critical path. Decomposing the schedule variance, SV = EV − PV = −£1,260,000, shows £890,000 of the shortfall sits in a fittings procurement package holding 15 weeks of float; only £370,000 lies on the critical path. A critical-path reforecast shows true slippage of **4 weeks**, not 12.5.

Disposition (Step 5): **amended**. Crashing two critical activities costs £128,000 and recovers the 4 genuine weeks; the benefit is 4 × £45,000 = £180,000, giving a net benefit of £180,000 − £128,000 = **£52,000**. The AI's package would have spent £340,000 to "recover" float that needed no recovery. The audit trail entry (Step 6) records the model version, data cut-off, the independent SV decomposition, the amended decision, the rationale and the project manager's name. The non-critical package is placed on watch, closing the loop at the next governance review (Step 7).

The examination presents scenarios of this kind and expects candidates to show both the arithmetic and the point at which judgement must depart from the AI's proposal.

### Model risk, data caveats and the audit trail

Model risk translates directly into accountability risk: the human owner answers for the model's failures. Models trained on unrepresentative portfolios reproduce historical bias; outputs degrade silently as delivery context drifts; generative assistants produce fluent text containing unverifiable claims; and opaque systems offer confidence without reasons. Because the accountable human cannot outsource these weaknesses, the audit trail must make them examinable. A defensible trail — the Step 6 entry, kept for every material decision — shows what the AI proposed and on what data, what the human examined against what independent evidence, and who disposed, how and why, particularly where judgement overrode the model. The trail serves governance in both directions: it protects the professional who exercised diligent judgement and exposes the professional who did not, and consequences — commendation, remediation or sanction — attach to the named human, never to the tool.

### Common pitfalls

- **Accountability diffusion** — attributing an outcome to "the model" or "the team"; every decision must trace to one named human.
- **Rubber-stamping** — a recorded review that involved no independent re-performance or challenge; automation bias is the most heavily examined failure mode.
- **Committee accountability** — naming a board where an individual is required; boards oversee, individuals account.
- **Provenance gaps** — logging the AI's answer but not its version, inputs and data cut-off, making the decision unexplainable later.
- **Selective trails** — recording acceptances but not amendments and rejections, which destroys the evidential value of the log.
- **Competence mismatch** — assigning accountability to someone who cannot explain the AI's method, as with SPI extrapolation above.
- **Retrospective reconstruction** — building the audit trail after the outcome is known rather than at the moment of decision.
- **One-off validation** — treating a model validated once as validated forever, ignoring drift in data and context.

### The standard the Institute examines

The elements of this chapter form a single chain: the accountability/responsibility distinction establishes what a human must hold; the disposal right names where it sits; competence and oversight supply the preconditions; and the audit trail makes the chain demonstrable. Break any link — an unnamed owner, an unexplained output, an unrecorded review — and accountability degrades into the diffuse "the system decided" posture that the examination requires candidates to recognise and reject.

Candidates are therefore expected to demonstrate that accountability structures survive contact with AI: that decision logs name humans rather than systems; that competence requirements are defined for anyone disposing of AI recommendations; that overrides of model output are recorded and reasoned rather than silent; and that post-incident reviews ask first who owned the decision, not what the tool suggested. An organisation whose delivery decisions cannot each be traced to a named, competent, informed human has not adopted AI — it has surrendered to it.

> **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** Applied to accountability, the doctrine is absolute: AI may draft, forecast, score and recommend, but it can be neither accountable nor responsible in the professional sense. Every AI contribution must be explainable, validated and owned; every decision must carry the name of a competent human who disposed of the recommendation and answers for the outcome. Where no such name exists, the decision is ungoverned — and the failure is human before it is technical.

### Key points

- Responsibility (performing work) can be delegated, shared and AI-assisted; accountability (answering for a decision) can never be delegated, shared or transferred to a tool.
- Every decision traces to one named, competent human: the "A" in any assignment matrix is a single identifiable person, never a system, vendor or committee.
- Every AI output is a proposal that must pass three tests — explainable, validated and owned — before it informs a decision; the disposal (accept, amend, reject) is always a human act.
- Validation is proportionate to consequence and includes independent re-performance and challenge of the governing assumption; an unexplained acceptance counts as no review at all.
- The audit trail is built at the moment of decision and records provenance, validation, disposition, rationale and the named individual — for rejections and amendments as rigorously as for acceptances.
- Automation bias (rubber-stamping) is the most heavily examined failure mode; where a professional cannot explain an output, the obligation is escalation, not signature.
- Governance and AI-assurance frameworks — ISO 21502, ISO/IEC 42001, ISO/IEC 23894, the NIST AI RMF, the EU AI Act — converge on the human in the loop as the locus of accountability; consequences attach to the named human, never the tool.

### Practice questions

**Question 1.** During a post-incident review, a delivery director explains a failed baseline change by stating that "the forecasting model recommended the revised dates and the steering board endorsed them." According to the Institute's doctrine, what is the fundamental defect in this position?

A. The steering board lacked the technical competence to endorse a model output
B. The model's recommendation was not validated against independent evidence
C. No single named human held accountability for the decision — it was diffused between a tool and a committee
D. The decision log failed to record the model version and data cut-off

**Correct answer: C.** Accountability can never rest with a tool or be diffused across a committee; B and D may also be failings, but the fundamental defect is the absence of a named accountable individual.

**Question 2.** In the worked example, what is the net benefit of the project manager's amended recovery decision, and why does it differ from the AI's claimed £110,000?

A. £52,000 — the critical-path reforecast shows only 4 weeks of true slippage, so £128,000 of crashing recovers £180,000 of liquidated damages
B. £110,000 — the amendment changes the actions but not the net benefit
C. £180,000 — the full liquidated damages saved by the amended package
D. £450,000 — the value of the 10 weeks the AI's package claimed to recover

**Correct answer: A.** Net benefit = (4 × £45,000) − £128,000 = £52,000; the AI's figure relied on SPI extrapolation that treated £890,000 of floating, non-critical variance as critical-path delay.

**Question 3.** A project manager records in the decision log: "AI cost forecast reviewed and accepted." No independent calculation, input check or assumption challenge is documented. Which pitfall does this best illustrate?

A. Retrospective reconstruction of the audit trail
B. Rubber-stamping driven by automation bias
C. Selective recording of acceptances only
D. Competence mismatch in the accountability assignment

**Correct answer: B.** A recorded review with no independent re-performance or challenge is rubber-stamping, the automation-bias failure mode; A, C and D concern when, what or by whom the record is made, none of which is evidenced here.



## Integrated Case Study: The Halcyon Operations Centre

### Purpose of this capstone

This closing chapter draws the competencies of the PCI PML-AI Body of Knowledge together into a single, realistic delivery. It introduces no new syllabus content; its purpose is integrative — to show how initiation, business-case discipline, governance, scope and requirements, planning and execution, stakeholder leadership, procurement, contracting and commercial delivery interlock on one live project, and how artificial intelligence is applied at every stage under the Institute's governing doctrine: **AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.** Every AI output is explainable, validated and owned by a competent, accountable human. Candidates should pause at each decision point and form a view before reading on: the examination routinely tests this kind of cross-competency synthesis, and while the figures are illustrative, the reasoning is the examinable substance.

### The scenario

Marston Vale Water, a fictional regional utility, operates three ageing control rooms whose technology is approaching end of support. A new resilience standard issued by its regulator must be met within four years. The executive board mandates **Project Halcyon**: consolidation of the three sites into a single operations centre — a new building on utility-owned land, and an integrated control platform configured from commercially available software. The Director of Operations is appointed sponsor; a project management leader is mandated to develop the business case. The delivery approach is hybrid: predictive for the civil and fit-out works, iterative for the platform configuration and migration.

### Stage one — initiation and the business case

The delivery leader's first act is not planning but justification. Four options are appraised: do minimum (remediate the worst site), refurbish all three, consolidate into a leased facility, or into a new build. Whole-life costs, benefits, risks and regulatory compliance are assessed for each. The new build carries the highest capital cost — **USD 78 million** against USD 52 million for the lease — but the strongest benefits case: consolidation savings, resilience compliance with margin, and an asset with a forty-year life. The business case recommends the new build, states its benefits in measurable terms (a 22.0% reduction in operating cost per site-year; incident-response performance at the regulator's threshold) and names benefit owners who will outlive the project.

**Applied AI.** An AI assistant drafts the options framework and generates comparative whole-life cost profiles from the utility's asset data and anonymised benchmark projects. The delivery leader finds one benchmark set unverifiable and excludes it, re-runs the comparison, and documents both the exclusion and its immaterial effect. The investment committee is told which figures are benchmark-derived and which are engineering estimates; the recommendation is the leader's, made explainable to the board without reference to any tool.

**Decision point 1.** A steering-group member urges skipping straight to design to protect the regulatory deadline. The sponsor declines: an unjustified project cannot be governed, because nothing has been agreed against which to judge change. The business case is approved at the first gate and becomes the standing test — *is this investment still worth making?* — applied at every later decision.

### Stage two — governance, scope and requirements

The sponsor chairs a project board comprising the senior user (head of network operations), senior supplier representation and an independent assurance voice. Tolerances are set — cost ±5.0%, schedule ±2 months, and zero tolerance on the regulatory compliance date — with escalation obligatory the moment a forecast breaches them.

Requirements are elicited from control-room staff, engineering, the regulator's standard and the emergency services with which the centre must interoperate. They are analysed, de-duplicated and prioritised: MoSCoW prioritisation for the platform, a baselined specification for the building. The delivery leader resists the senior user's wish to class every requirement "must have": a prioritisation in which nothing may drop is not a prioritisation but a hope.

**Applied AI.** Language-model tooling clusters 1,140 requirement statements into candidate themes and flags 63 apparent duplicates and 17 contradictions. The business analyst confirms 41 duplicates, rejects the remainder as genuinely distinct, and resolves the contradictions in facilitated workshops — the machine sorted; the humans decided. Traceability from every baselined requirement to a named stakeholder and an acceptance test is owned by the analyst, not the tool.

**Decision point 2.** Should the platform's "could have" requirements be contracted now or deferred? The board defers them to a post-migration release, keeping release one small enough to protect the compliance date — scope discipline exercised at the moment of commitment rather than discovered in delivery.

### Stage three — procurement, contracts and commercial structure

The procurement strategy splits the work into two lots. The building is let as a design-and-build contract under a **target-cost arrangement with a pain/gain share**, because ground conditions and fit-out interfaces carry genuine two-way uncertainty that a fixed price would merely convert into claims. The platform is let as a **fixed-price implementation with output-based acceptance criteria** and a rate-carded change mechanism, awarded through competitive dialogue. Evaluation criteria and weightings are published before tenders are opened; the panel scores independently and moderates openly.

**Applied AI.** During evaluation, AI tooling extracts each bidder's stated assumptions, exclusions and departures into a comparison matrix and flags one bid whose programme logic is internally inconsistent. The panel verifies the flag against the bid documents — it is real — and raises it in clarification. No score is generated by the machine: scoring is the panel's judgement, recorded with reasons, because a procurement decision that cannot be defended to an unsuccessful bidder or an auditor is not a decision but a liability.

**Decision point 3.** The lowest platform bid sits 18.0% below the next and below the utility's own should-cost estimate. Rather than banking the saving, the panel treats abnormally low pricing as a delivery risk, tests the bidder's resourcing assumptions in dialogue, and uncovers a scope misreading. The bid is corrected upward and loses; the contract is awarded on evaluated value for money, and the record states why.

### Stage four — planning, execution and commercial delivery

An integrated schedule links the civil works, fit-out, platform sprints and migration rehearsals, with the critical path running through fit-out into systems integration. Progress is measured through earned value for the works and validated, releasable increments for the platform. In month eleven, ground contamination is found: the contractor gives early warning, the compensation mechanism is applied as written, and the pain/gain share does what it was designed to do — the event costs money, not the relationship. Forecast cost moves to +4.6% of baseline: within tolerance, so reported, not escalated. When a later design change threatens +6.1%, escalation to the board is immediate, and the board funds it from contingency against a re-confirmed business case.

**Applied AI.** Run monthly, a schedule risk analysis tool proposes a probabilistic completion range and identifies the migration-rehearsal window as the emerging driver. The planner audits the model's logic links and duration distributions before any output reaches the board pack, and the delivery leader presents the forecast as a range with stated confidence — owning it as the project's forecast, not the software's.

**Decision point 4.** With the compliance date under pressure, the board weighs three recovery options: compress commissioning (a quality risk), migrate one control room at a time (slower benefits, protected compliance), or seek a short regulatory extension (an uncertain precedent). It selects phased migration — a deliberate, recorded trade of benefit timing for delivery certainty.

### Stage five — stakeholder leadership under pressure

Consolidation means relocation for 210 staff across three sites, and the recognised union signals formal opposition. The delivery leader treats this as leadership work, not communications work: the union is engaged as a stakeholder with legitimate interests, options for shift patterns and travel support are co-developed, and the senior user — not the project office — fronts the change with operational staff. An AI-drafted engagement pack is reviewed line by line before release; one sentence overstating job security is caught and corrected by its human owner — a small moment that illustrates the entire doctrine. Relocation terms are agreed two months before migration, and the first shift in the new centre is fully crewed.

### Stage six — closure and benefits

The centre enters service ahead of the compliance date. The deferred "could have" scope passes to a governed release-two decision, not an assumed one. The project closes formally: contracts are settled, lessons are recorded while memory is fresh, and the benefit owners begin measurement against the baselines the business case fixed. Twelve months on, the operating-cost benefit is tracking at 19.0% against the 22.0% forecast — reported honestly, with a recovery plan, because benefits realisation is a discipline, not a press release.

### Integrative discussion questions

1. At Decision point 1 the sponsor refused to begin design before the business case was approved. Which specific governance capabilities would have been lost had the project proceeded, and how would each later decision point have been weakened?
2. The case allocates ground-condition risk through a target-cost contract and platform delivery risk through a fixed price. Justify each allocation from first principles — then describe a change of circumstances that would reverse one of them.
3. Identify every point in the case where an AI output was corrected, excluded or overridden. For each, state the competence the responsible human needed to catch the flaw, and what would have failed had it been absent.
4. Decision point 4 traded benefit timing for delivery certainty. Reconstruct the decision as the board should have documented it: options, evaluation against the business case, tolerance implications and named ownership of the residual risk.
5. The union engagement succeeded because leadership, not process, was applied. Analyse what would probably have happened had the delivery leader relied solely on the AI-drafted communications pack — and who would have been accountable for the outcome.



## Glossary


# PCI PML-AI Body of Knowledge — Glossary of Terms

This glossary consolidates the key terms of the PCI Project Management Leader – AI (PCI PML-AI) Body of Knowledge, Chapters 1–24. Each term is defined once here and carries the same meaning wherever it appears in the Body of Knowledge, the examination blueprint and the Institute's study materials. Candidates preparing for the PCI PML-AI examination should treat these definitions as authoritative.

**Agile delivery** — An iterative, incremental approach in which requirements and solutions evolve through short, time-boxed cycles of planning, delivery and review. Value is released progressively and priorities are reordered as learning emerges.

**AI-enabled project management** — The application of artificial intelligence techniques — such as forecasting, classification and natural-language analysis — to support project planning, monitoring, reporting and decision-making. The Institute's doctrine applies throughout: AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable.

**AI governance** — The policies, controls and oversight arrangements through which an organisation directs and constrains its use of artificial intelligence in delivery. Effective AI governance ensures every AI output is explainable, validated and owned by a competent human.

**"AI proposes; the professional verifies, decides and remains accountable"** — The Institute's governing doctrine for artificial intelligence in project delivery: AI may draft, forecast, flag and recommend, but a competent, accountable human examines, validates and decides. No AI output is acted upon unexplained, unvalidated or unowned.

**Algorithmic bias** — Systematic distortion in an AI system's outputs, typically arising from unrepresentative training data or flawed design, that produces unfair or misleading results. Detecting and mitigating bias is a core obligation of responsible AI practice.

**Assurance** — Independent, evidence-based confirmation, provided to governance bodies, that a project is being managed appropriately and remains likely to achieve its objectives. Assurance is distinct from, and must not be captured by, the delivery team it examines.

**Automation** — The use of technology to execute defined, repeatable delivery tasks — such as data capture, reporting and workflow routing — without manual effort. Automation removes toil from routine work; accountability for the automated outcome remains with the professional.

**Baseline** — The approved, version-controlled statement of scope, schedule or cost against which performance is measured. A baseline may be altered only through formal change control.

**Benefit** — A measurable improvement arising from project outcomes that stakeholders regard as advantageous and that contributes to organisational objectives.

**Benefits realisation** — The structured identification, planning, tracking and embedding of the benefits for which a project was undertaken, continuing beyond project closure under named business ownership.

**Business case** — The documented justification for a project, presenting the options appraised together with expected benefits, costs, risks and timescales. It is maintained as a living document and retested at each governance gate.

**Change control** — The formal process by which proposed departures from a baseline are captured, assessed for impact, approved or rejected at the correct level of authority, and recorded.

**Configuration management** — The discipline of identifying, version-controlling and auditing project deliverables and their documentation so that all parties work from the current approved version.

**Contingency** — Time or money held in reserve against identified risks, released under defined governance rules rather than absorbed silently into estimates.

**Contract** — A legally binding agreement between parties defining the goods, services or works to be delivered, the price and payment mechanism, and each party's rights, obligations and remedies.

**Cost–benefit analysis** — An appraisal technique that compares the whole-life costs of an option with the value of its expected benefits, so that alternatives can be ranked on a consistent economic basis.

**Cost-reimbursable contract** — A contract form in which the supplier is paid allowable actual costs plus an agreed fee, placing cost risk largely with the buyer. It suits work whose scope cannot yet be defined tightly enough for a fixed price.

**Critical path** — The longest sequence of dependent activities through the schedule network, which determines the shortest achievable project duration. Any delay to a critical-path activity delays completion unless recovered.

**Decision intelligence** — The discipline of improving decision-making by combining data, analytics and AI with structured human judgement, making each decision's framing, options, assumptions and consequences explicit and reviewable.

**Decision rights** — The explicit allocation of authority stating who may take which decisions, at what thresholds and by what process — including which decisions may never be delegated to an automated system.

**Digital delivery** — The conduct of project work through connected digital tools, shared data standards and collaborative platforms, so that plans, models and records exist as structured, reusable data rather than static documents.

**Digital twin** — A data-driven digital representation of a physical asset, system or process, kept current with its real-world counterpart and used to simulate, monitor and optimise performance.

**Earned value management (EVM)** — An integrated method for measuring project performance that compares the value of work performed with the work scheduled and its actual cost, yielding objective schedule and cost indices and forecasts.

**Estimate at completion (EAC)** — A forecast of the total cost of the project at its finish, derived from actual costs to date plus an estimate of the remaining work informed by performance so far.

**Explainability** — The degree to which the reasoning behind an AI output can be understood and articulated by the accountable professional. An output that cannot be explained cannot responsibly be relied upon.

**Fixed-price contract** — A contract form in which the supplier delivers defined scope for an agreed sum, carrying the majority of cost risk. It suits well-specified requirements in stable conditions.

**Governance gate** — A formal decision point between stages at which the governing body reviews evidence, retests the business case and decides whether the project should proceed, change direction or stop.

**Hallucination** — Plausible but false or fabricated content produced by a generative AI system. The possibility of hallucination is a principal reason every AI output must be validated by a competent human before use.

**Handover** — The controlled transfer of deliverables, documentation, knowledge and operational responsibility from the project to its business or operational owner, with acceptance formally recorded.

**Human accountability** — The principle that a named, competent person remains answerable for every decision, deliverable and outcome, however it was produced. Accountability may be supported by tools but can never be transferred to them.

**Human-in-the-loop** — An operating pattern in which a competent professional reviews, validates and approves an AI-generated proposal before it takes effect, preserving human ownership of consequential actions.

**Hybrid delivery** — A deliberately tailored approach combining predictive and agile elements within one project — for example, a predictive governance wrapper around iterative build cycles — selected to fit the certainty and cadence of the work.

**Invitation to tender (ITT)** — A formal procurement document inviting prospective suppliers to submit priced, compliant offers against a defined specification and published evaluation criteria.

**Iteration** — A short, fixed-length delivery cycle (in Scrum, a sprint) that produces a reviewable increment of the product and closes with reflection that informs the next cycle.

**Large language model (LLM)** — A class of generative AI trained on large text corpora to produce fluent language outputs such as drafts, summaries and analyses. Its outputs are probabilistic and must be verified before professional use.

**Lessons learned** — Knowledge derived from project experience, captured and shared so that successes are repeated and failures are not. Capture is consolidated at closure but should occur throughout delivery.

**Machine learning** — A family of AI techniques in which models learn patterns from data in order to make predictions or classifications without being explicitly programmed for each case.

**Milestone** — A significant, zero-duration point in the schedule marking completion of a defined achievement, used for progress measurement and reporting.

**Portfolio** — The totality of an organisation's projects, programmes and related change investments, managed collectively to balance risk and resources against strategic objectives.

**Predictive delivery** — A plan-driven approach in which scope, schedule and cost are defined and baselined up front and delivery proceeds through sequential phases. It is best suited to stable, well-understood requirements.

**Procurement** — The process of identifying the need for externally supplied goods, services or works and acquiring them — from make-or-buy analysis and market engagement through supplier selection to contract award.

**Product backlog** — A single, ordered list of everything that may be needed in the product, continually refined and reprioritised by the product owner as understanding grows.

**Product owner** — The role accountable for maximising the value of the product, owning and ordering the backlog and acting as the authoritative voice of the business and its customers.

**Professional judgement** — The considered application of competence, experience and ethical standards to a decision, particularly where evidence is incomplete or automated recommendations conflict. It is the human faculty no tool can replace.

**Programme** — A group of related projects and change activities coordinated to deliver outcomes and benefits that could not be achieved by managing them independently.

**Project** — A temporary endeavour with defined objectives, undertaken to create a unique output, outcome or benefit within agreed constraints of time, cost and quality.

**Project charter** — The document that formally authorises a project, appointing the project manager, granting authority to apply resources and recording the objectives, constraints and success criteria agreed at initiation.

**Project closure** — The formal end of a project, at which deliverables are accepted, contracts are closed, resources are released, performance is evaluated and lessons are recorded.

**Project governance** — The framework of authority, accountability and decision-making — sponsor, board, gates and reporting — through which an organisation directs and controls its projects.

**Project leadership** — The practice of setting direction, building and motivating teams and creating the conditions in which people deliver; it is distinguished from, and complementary to, the management of plans and controls.

**Project life cycle** — The sequence of phases a project passes through from initiation to closure. Its shape — predictive, agile or hybrid — is chosen to suit the nature of the work.

**Project management** — The application of knowledge, skills, methods and judgement to lead defined work from initiation to closure so that objectives are achieved within agreed constraints.

**Project sponsor** — The senior accountable owner of the business case, who chairs governance, secures resources and remains answerable for the realisation of benefits.

**Quality assurance** — The planned, systematic activities that give confidence that the processes producing deliverables are appropriate and being followed. Assurance addresses the process rather than the individual output.

**Quality control** — The inspection, testing and measurement of deliverables against defined acceptance criteria to verify that each output meets its specification.

**Quantitative risk analysis** — The numerical modelling of combined risk and uncertainty — commonly by simulation across integrated schedule and cost models — to express outcomes as ranges and confidence levels rather than single-point figures.

**Requirement** — A documented, verifiable statement of a need or condition that the project's outputs must satisfy, agreed with stakeholders and used as the basis of acceptance.

**Requirements traceability** — The ability to follow each requirement forward to design, build, test and acceptance, and backward to its origin, so that coverage and change impact can be demonstrated.

**Responsible AI** — The design, selection and use of artificial intelligence in ways that are lawful, ethical, transparent, secure and subject to human control, with risks such as bias, error and data misuse actively governed.

**Risk register** — The living record of identified threats and opportunities, recording for each its assessment, owner, planned responses and current status.

**Robotic process automation (RPA)** — Software that executes rule-based, repetitive tasks across existing systems — such as data transfer and report assembly — exactly as configured. It follows rules and exercises no judgement.

**Rolling-wave planning** — Progressive elaboration in which near-term work is planned in detail while later work is held at a higher level until better information becomes available.

**Scope** — The totality of outputs, outcomes and work required to deliver a project, expressed in terms of what is included and, just as importantly, what is excluded.

**Scope creep** — The uncontrolled growth of scope through incremental, unapproved additions that bypass change control, eroding schedule, cost and quality.

**Stakeholder** — Any individual, group or organisation that can affect, is affected by, or perceives itself to be affected by the project.

**Stakeholder engagement** — The deliberate, planned work of identifying and analysing stakeholders and of communicating, consulting and building the relationships needed for delivery — an activity to be led, not merely administered.

**Sustainability in project delivery** — The management of a project's environmental, social and economic impacts across the whole life of its outputs, embedding considerations such as carbon, resource use and community effect into decisions from options appraisal onward.

**Tailoring** — The deliberate adaptation of methods, governance and controls to fit a specific project's size, risk, complexity and context — the judgement at the heart of hybrid delivery.

**Variation** — A formally instructed change to the scope, conditions or price of a contract, processed under the contract's change mechanism and priced and agreed before or as the changed work proceeds.

**Velocity** — An empirical measure of the amount of backlog work a team completes per iteration, used to inform forecasting. It is a planning aid for the team, not a productivity target for comparison across teams.

**Whole-life cost** — The total cost of an asset or output over its entire life — acquisition, operation, maintenance and disposal — used in appraisal so that decisions are not driven by capital cost alone.

**Work breakdown structure (WBS)** — The hierarchical decomposition of the total scope into progressively smaller elements down to work packages, providing the framework for estimating, assignment and control.

**Work package** — The lowest level of the work breakdown structure at which work is estimated, scheduled, assigned to a single owner and controlled.
